## BLOCK 0001 — CHAPTERS 391 TO 400, THE NINETEENTH TO THE TWENTY-EIGHTH OF THE TENTH

**391, tenth/19.** The volume opens on its debt: the length of the eleventh month is not a figure this canon has, and a man of fifty-six who has read the figures off that board every morning for six months says out loud that he does not know how many days it has, and nobody has an answer, and a clerk enters that a figure somebody entered because they had to is not a promise about a later day. **3 claims [50, 57], 0 mismatches, 2,563 words.**
**392, tenth/20.** The stone on the lot book has gone green down one side of it in about a week of rain and nobody turned it over. **2 claims, 2,394 words.**
**393, tenth/21.** The boy of about nineteen comes in with two figures at the top of his own page in his own hand. **2 claims, 2,211 words.**
**394, tenth/22.** Hard frost on the boards at first light. **3 claims, 2,230 words.**
**395, tenth/23.** The door four hundred yards up that bank is shut when the yard fills up. **2 claims, 2,286 words.**
**396, tenth/24.** The trough is drawn from twice in one morning, once by a woman who came up that bank for water with buckets and once by a man who was not carrying anything. **2 claims, 2,263 words.**
**397, tenth/25.** The man who puts tables up gets the figure off the top board. **2 claims, 2,414 words. THE ONLY CHAPTER IN ALL FIFTY THAT SPELLS THE WORD *REMOVAL*.**
**398, tenth/26.** The far end of that yard is nine feet from the low wall and four hundred yards down the lane from the step of two stones. **1 claim, 2,227 words.**
**399, tenth/27.** The low wall at the south end is about four feet high and about nine feet long, and it has a thing on the far side of it that nobody knows about. **1 claim, 2,182 words. THE ONLY CHAPTER IN THE VOLUME OUTSIDE THE 2,200-3,200 BAND, EIGHTEEN WORDS UNDER THE FLOOR. Line 51 of this chapter is one of the two duplicated PROSE paragraphs in the fifty, and it is spoken again verbatim at line 63 of Chapter 408.**
**400, tenth/28.** Hard clean light on that bank, and about four of them said good and about four of them said nothing at all. **3 claims, 2,279 words.**

## BLOCK 0002 — CHAPTERS 401 TO 410, THE TWENTY-NINTH OF THE TENTH TO THE EIGHTH OF THE ELEVENTH

**401, tenth/29.** The boy gets to that board before the man of fifty-six does, which is not a thing he has done in six months. **3 claims, 2,319 words.**
**402, tenth/30.** **THE COUNT.** The clerk puts her finger on the mark for the first of the tenth month and counts to this morning's mark and gets **thirty**, and enters that the length of the month after this one is **thirty-one, taken off a list, which she did not count, and flagged with the word *unchecked*.** **THE WORD ENTERS THE PAGE HERE, A DAY EARLIER THAN `outline/volume-09.md` CLAIMS.** 2 claims, 2,420 words, and the lowest *about* count in the volume at 43.
**403, eleventh/1.** The arms cross. She says the word once and writes it twelve times. The third month goes to eight months back, said out loud in the yard and written on her page. **2 claims, 2,417 words.**
**404, eleventh/2.** **THE ONLY DAY IN FIFTY ON WHICH THE WORD IS SPELLED BESIDE THE FIGURES IN A COUNTED SPEECH: *Three hundred and twelve, unchecked. Six hundred and twenty-eight, unchecked. Three hundred and forty-two, unchecked. Three hundred and three, unchecked.*** 1 claim, 2,292 words.
**405, eleventh/3.** **THE DAY THE FINDING IS ARGUED FOR.** A man asks twice whether the word is staying on the figures and gets the same answer in the same words, and a third time gets a shorter one, and *that is a refusal* and *it is an answer and the third one is shorter*. Somebody asks her to rule a fifth column to hold the word and she says no before the question is finished, and gives the reason: four columns of not-askings and not one person on the page. **AND AT ABOUT HALF PAST TWO THE MAN THE FIGURE OF TWENTY-ONE YEARS IS AGAINST SAYS OUT LOUD THAT HE HAS READ THOSE FOUR FIGURES OVER ANOTHER MAN'S SHOULDER EVERY MORNING OF THIS MONTH AND THAT THIS MORNING HE HAS NOT READ ONE, AND THAT HE DID NOT DECIDE NOT TO.** 1 claim, 2,241 words.
**406, eleventh/4.** The loam man comes the whole way across the yard at about ten instead of going to the trough. The man of fifty-six asks the clerk why the word is not on one more figure. **2 claims, 2,489 words.**
**407, eleventh/5.** Two men work out on their fingers when the figure on the second line stopped being true, and the two lines of the lot book are read out loud in the open. **1 claim, 2,313 words. THE TWO DOCUMENT LINES THAT ARE DUPLICATED AT Chapter 412.**
**408, eleventh/6.** The argument about what the word is a figure of, and the two walls maxim repeated verbatim from Chapter 399. **1 claim, 2,327 words.**
**409, eleventh/7.** The page is put down open with the corner turned back and the word in the margin facing up. **1 claim, 2,320 words.**
**410, eleventh/8.** A man reads twelve figures off the clerk's margin going down them with the word at the end of each and gets all twelve. **1 claim, 2,308 words.**

## BLOCK 0003 — CHAPTERS 411 TO 420, THE NINTH TO THE EIGHTEENTH OF THE ELEVENTH

**411, eleventh/9.** The man of fifty-six says the word again after a morning without it. **A MAN SAYS OUT LOUD THAT HE WROTE THE WORD *UNCHECKED* TWELVE TIMES ON THE FIRST OF THIS MONTH BEFORE THERE WAS ANYBODY IN THAT YARD, AND THAT IT HAS BEEN ON THAT PAGE EVERY MORNING SINCE — AND THE STRING IS AT ZERO ON SIX OF THOSE MORNINGS.** 1 claim, 2,839 words, THE LONGEST CHAPTER IN THE VOLUME.
**412, eleventh/10.** The two lines of the lot book are the same two lines, and the stone is not turned over. **1 claim, 2,566 words.**
**413, eleventh/11.** She copies the word from one place on her page to another. **1 claim, 2,377 words.**
**414, eleventh/12.** A figure is a figure off a list in a room four hundred yards up that bank, and the word says so. **1 claim, 2,477 words.**
**415, eleventh/13.** A lot with no figure a stranger can check has no way of being wrong in public. **1 claim, 2,710 words, the second longest.**
**416, eleventh/14.** The word of the day is tomorrow. **1 claim, 2,548 words.**
**417, eleventh/15.** **THE MONTH BOUNDARY OF THE STANDING STALENESS: the figure on the second line of the lot book is a month out of date and the man who put it there says so out loud, and a man of about nineteen counts what he said.** 1 claim, 2,674 words.
**418, eleventh/16.** The ninth of the nine printed nights is two hundred and nine days back. 1 claim, 2,411 words.
**419, eleventh/17.** Nobody standing there said the word *month* out loud yesterday. 1 claim, 2,639 words.
**420, eleventh/18.** A morning on which nothing in that yard had to be said twice. 1 claim, 2,660 words.

## BLOCK 0004 — CHAPTERS 421 TO 430, THE NINETEENTH TO THE TWENTY-EIGHTH OF THE ELEVENTH

**421, eleventh/19.** The woman of fifty-eight stops at the trough with her buckets half full and asks the man who cuts reeds a question. 1 claim, 2,351 words.
**422, eleventh/20.** Two men say the same word on the same morning and one of them said it to a woman at a trough. 1 claim, 2,392 words.
**423, eleventh/21.** The mender is asked at about a quarter to ten to say the price of a rule out loud. 1 claim, 2,245 words.
**424, eleventh/22.** Nobody at that table knew what it was going to be. 1 claim, 2,317 words.
**425, eleventh/23.** A clerk says out loud that a person who has not been asked has not refused and cannot be counted either way. 1 claim, 2,556 words.
**426, eleventh/24.** The man who puts tables up begins to work out in front of about nine people what a rule is for. 1 claim, 2,321 words.
**427, eleventh/25.** Two people had known since about eleven that something was going to be said out loud and neither of them had said it. 1 claim, 2,306 words.
**428, eleventh/26.** The figure on the second line is forty-one days out of date at about a quarter to nine. 1 claim, 2,290 words.
**429, eleventh/27.** **THE NAME.** At about eleven a man of about thirty-two who puts tables up said a name out loud in the open, in the ordinary voice, in front of about nineteen people, and it was not the protagonist's, and the person it belongs to was not asked, and **nobody wrote it down.** 1 claim, 2,545 words.
**430, eleventh/28.** A yard a day after a name went out loud in it went on doing what it does. 1 claim, 2,737 words.

## BLOCK 0005 — CHAPTERS 431 TO 440, THE TWENTY-NINTH OF THE ELEVENTH TO THE SEVENTH OF THE TWELFTH

**431, eleventh/29.** There was a name in the air in that yard and there was not one on a page. 1 claim, 2,259 words.
**432, eleventh/30.** The woman who keeps a scale comes down the bank with the day's flour on her hip. 1 claim, 2,233 words.
**433, eleventh/31.** **THE CHECK, THE LAST DAY OF THE MONTH, AND THE FIRST TIME IN THIS CANON THAT A MONTH LENGTH IS COUNTED AFTER THE MONTH IT DESCRIBES HAS RUN OUT.** The clerk counts the marks from the first of the eleventh to this morning's mark and gets **thirty-one**, and the figure entered off a list was thirty-one, and **the two agree, and she enters that a figure that reproduces is a figure about a count and is not a finding, and that the ninth month's length was entered off a list in an earlier block and can never now be checked because nobody wrote down where that mark was, and that is a cost and it is a cost about a board and not about a person.** 1 claim, 2,495 words.
**434, twelfth/1.** **THE DAY.** A man of fifty-six does not read the figures off that wall and nine people notice, and a body four hundred miles off prints at its sixty-one-day interval and does not come, and he walks down a lane and looks at a gatepost and there is nothing on it, and **a day he spent walking is a day he did not read.** 2 claims [32, 40], 2,306 words.
**435, twelfth/2.** **THE RESOLUTION, IN ONE LINE.** A man of about thirty-seven who cuts reeds said a day out loud, the fifteenth of the tenth, and a clerk wrote a date under a figure that is wrong and left the figure wrong, and the third line went into the book in the open under a stone: ***One lot. The figure on the second line above stopped being true on the fifteenth of the tenth month.*** And she says out loud that she is not going to enter that the rule has been improved, and is not going to enter that a thing has been solved, and is not going to enter that a stranger walking up to that table tomorrow can tell which of the three lines is current, **because he cannot.** 2 claims [51, 104], 2,802 words.
**436, twelfth/3.** **THE COUNT OF INSTRUMENTS MOVES FROM TEN TO ELEVEN, WITH A SCENE AND A REASON GIVEN OUT LOUD AND NOT ASKED FOR**, and a clerk enters the move and the reason and not only the number. 1 claim, 2,603 words.
**437, twelfth/4.** There are three lines and only one of them is a figure, and two men have owned a thing in that yard this month and neither was thanked and neither was given anything. 2 claims [49, 38], 2,448 words.
**438, twelfth/5.** **THE CLOTH.** At about eleven, in front of about nine people, the man of about thirty-four who mends fencing takes the cloth off his left hand and **the hand does not close**, and nobody says one word about what it is. He says out loud that nobody has thanked him and that he is not going to either, and a woman of about thirty-six who keeps a scale says out loud that she has been waiting two months for about four of them to thank him. **A CLERK MAY NOT ENTER THAT A HAND IS BETTER THAN IT WAS AND DID NOT.** **0 counted claims.**
**439, twelfth/6.** About four people bring something to the end of that table to put in the book, and the clerk says out loud that she has a word over a figure in her own margin and is not going to take it off, and a man there heard a name on the twenty-seventh and the record says not asked. **0 counted claims.**
**440, twelfth/7, THE LAST DAY OF THE FIFTY.** **A HAND ON A PAGE.** The left hand of the man who mends fencing comes out from under the boards and lies flat and open on the **second** line of that lot book, and the figure on that line is still wrong, and the third line is a date, and there is a stone on the corner of the book, and about nineteen people are in that yard and **not one of them says one word about what the hand is doing there.** The four figures are read off the wall and got, the word said four times and got four times. At about half past four the clerk reads the measure of the whole thing out loud in one paragraph to about nine people **and does not read it back from the top, and a thing read back by nobody binds nobody**, and a few of them said afterwards that they had wanted to be asked to read it and nobody asked them. The figure on the sheet at that gatepost is four hundred and eleven and has not moved on any of the fifty days. The bid is open ninety-eight days. The column for the name of whoever read a thing out loud is still ruled and still empty. **A clerk entered that a hand was taken off at about eleven and that the hand did not close.** And the last thing is a woman of about thirty-six who keeps a scale, reading the three lines standing up, saying one thing out loud to the stone: **there is a hand on the wrong line and there is a day under the wrong line and both of those are true and neither of them is a figure.** 1 claim [64], 2,368 words.

THE COST, PLANNED: a right hand that turns a stone and a left hand that has not closed since the eleventh of the June; a man of fifty-six told in the open and not asked twice; a man who cuts reeds keeping a chalk count he cannot be paid for; and the fifth of the five things this district does not have, which is still not paid.**

# VOLUME 10, BATCH 0001 — THE CHAPTERS 441 TO 455 SUMMARIES AS THEY STAND AFTER THE REVIEW REPAIR OF 2026-09-28

**THE FIFTEEN CHAPTERS ARE THE SAME FIFTEEN CHAPTERS, ON THE SAME FOURTEEN DAYS, WITH THE SAME FIGURES, AND THE SUMMARIES ABOVE ARE CORRECT. WHAT FOLLOWS IS NOT A RESTATEMENT OF THEM. IT IS THE SIX CHAPTERS THE REPAIR TOUCHED, AND WHAT IS DIFFERENT IN EACH, SO THAT A WRITER WHO READS THE CHAPTER FILES AND A WRITER WHO READS ONLY THIS FILE ARE NOT TELLING EACH OTHER TWO STORIES.**

- **441, A Figure Nobody Had To Put Down.** **CHANGED. It no longer opens on the cloth coming off that left hand; it opens on the stone on the corner of that lot book at about half past eight with the rough side up, and about nine people have gone past it without picking it up, and nobody in that yard has ever turned it over. The cloth still comes off at about a quarter to nine, in the same scene, in the same words, and the hand is still open and flat and not closing. The stone is still on the corner of the book when the chapter opens, which is what its last line depends on, and it is still turned over in the same chapter, and the wear is still one inch deep in the middle and nothing at either end. The day is the eighth of the twelfth month. The relay about nineteen people by ten no longer ends the date paragraph and now stands at the head of the ten o'clock paragraph.**
- **442, One Inch In The Middle.** **UNCHANGED IN ITS OPENING AND CHANGED IN TWO PLACES DOWNSTREAM. Its opening, the cloth going back on the left hand, is now the only one of its kind in the block and no longer has Chapter 441's opposite in front of it. The clerk's ledger entry is written at about ten past four instead of about half past four, and the ledger paragraph still opens `The bid is open a hundred days and was not run on this day`. The day is the ninth of the twelfth month.**
- **443, A Figure About A Morning.** **CHANGED IN TWO PLACES. The relay no longer ends the date paragraph and now carries the fact that the ditch had taken all the rain of that night and was still holding it, which the chapter had already put on the page. The ledger paragraph now opens `A hundred and one days is how long the bid has been open` instead of `The bid is open a hundred and one days`, which is the construction the block had already been using from Chapter 450. The day is the tenth of the twelfth month, and this is the morning of the day that Chapter 444 is the afternoon of.**
- **445, The Day Under The Figure.** **CHANGED IN TWO PLACES. The relay no longer ends the date paragraph and now stands at the head of the paragraph in which the clerk asks the man about the fifteenth of the tenth month, carrying the fact that she had gone at that question sideways for about eleven days, which is her own opening sentence. The ledger entry is written a few minutes short of five instead of at about half past four. The day is the eleventh of the twelfth month, and the day under the figure on the second line is still the fifteenth of the tenth, and it is still a man, and the third line of that book was still not altered.**
- **446, Two Columns Of His Own.** **CHANGED IN TWO PLACES. The ledger paragraph now opens `A hundred and three days is how long the bid has been open`. One carrier of the clerk's entry that a man said a thing out loud was reworded. The boy still rules two columns of his own, the left-hand one a day and the right-hand one a page, and he still began the right-hand column at one that morning, which is the first page of his own he has ever counted, and yesterday's two pages are still not in it and he still has not decided what to do about that. The day is the twelfth of the twelfth month.**
- **448, The Second Table And The Stone.** **CHANGED IN TWO PLACES. The relay no longer ends the date paragraph and now stands on its own and carries the fact that the second table was not up yet, which is true of that morning and is a fact about the morning and not about anybody. The ledger entry is written at about a quarter to five instead of at about half past four. The second table still went up in about eleven minutes at about half past eleven, unasked, and the man who put it up still said out loud beforehand that nobody had asked him and that he was not going to be thanked, and a stone still went back onto the corner of a book at about ten past ten because the wind had taken hold of the corner of it. The day is the fourteenth of the twelfth month.**
- **450, A Number And A Reason.** **CHANGED IN TWO PLACES. The relay no longer ends the date paragraph and now carries the fact that about four people had already noticed the underside of that second table. One carrier of the clerk's entry was reworded. The clerk still entered a number that had not moved in two months and a reason under it in one entry and read the reason out loud and did not read it back from the top and appointed nobody to read it back, and the count of instruments still moved from eleven to twelve on this day with the reason in the same entry. The day is the sixteenth of the twelfth month.**
- **452, The Tally At The East End.** **CHANGED IN ONE PLACE. The relay no longer ends the date paragraph and now stands at the head of the paragraph in which the man of about thirty-four who mends fencing comes up out of the lane at about eleven, carrying the fact that the man of about forty-eight who keeps a tally had been at the east end of that yard since about half past nine with the flat book under his left arm. The clerk's shortest entries are still four words and five words and six words, and `A hundred and nine days. The bid is open and was not run.` is unchanged. The day is the eighteenth of the twelfth month.**
- **454, A Figure Nobody Can Check.** **CHANGED IN ONE PLACE. The relay no longer ends the date paragraph and now carries the fact that the sleet in the air had not come down on one of them. The chapter's own opening, the clerk's entry at about half past four on the twentieth of the twelfth month, is unchanged and is the one place in the block where that hour is the subject rather than the stamp. The reason the bid cannot be run on was still said out loud here and entered as two halves that a clerk did not join, and the day a man is afraid of is still the fourteenth of the month and is still not the same thing. The day is the twentieth of the twelfth month.**
- **455, A Day.** **CHANGED IN ONE PLACE. The relay no longer ends the date paragraph and now carries the fact that the four figures on that wall had been said once already that morning and that the clerk had not written them down, which is this chapter's own refusal and is the reason the bid is a day older and not a figure. Everything else is as it was: a man of about thirty-four said out loud in front of about nineteen people that he is afraid of a day, nobody agreed with him, nobody asked him for it a second time, the boy of about nineteen counted what he said and put the count down on his own sheet and did not read it back to himself, which is the first time in fourteen days that he has not done that, and the length of the twelfth month is still twenty-one days old and unpaid and still the last figure in the calendar nobody has counted.**

**THE FIGURES THE BLOCK DID NOT MOVE, AND THEIR SCOPE OVER THE TWELVE DAYS: the figure on the sheet is 411 and did not move on any of the twelve days and there is no day-count for it; the bid is 124 to 135 and was not run on any of the fifteen days; the figure on the second line of that lot book is 79 to 90 days out of date and unaltered with a date under it that is a man; the rule said out loud is 84 to 95; the first day of the eighth month is 154 to 165 days past; the ninth of the nine printed nights is 257 to 268 days back and is open and was closed on none of the fifteen days, which is the twentieth block in a row; a body four hundred miles off is 93 to 104 days past a printing it did not make and nobody watched anything; the man of about sixty-four is on his hundred and sixty-fifth night rising to his hundred and seventy-sixth and has slept on a hundred and seventy-five of a hundred and seventy-six and was given nothing on any of the fifteen days.**

**THE FIGURES THAT ARE A STANDING AND NOT A LADDER COLUMN, AND THEIR DERIVATIONS, SO THAT A WRITER WHO USES THEM DOES NOT RE-DERIVE THEM OFF A DAY REMEMBERED: the figures a man of fifty-six has read off that wall every morning are 175 plus `c`, off Chapter 461's hundred and ninety-four at `c = 19`, and run 201 to 212; the marks in chalk along the edge of that second table are 15 plus `c`, off Chapter 469's eighteen at `c = 25`, and run 41 to 52, and are NAMED IN FIVE OF THE FIFTEEN CHAPTERS AND NOT PRINTED AS A RUN; the nine thumbs at Chapter 453 are nineteen days before Chapter 477.**

**AND THE FIFTEEN CHAPTERS ARE FIFTEEN DIFFERENT SENTENCES AT BOTH ENDS AND ONE DAY IN THE MIDDLE, AND THE ONE DAY THAT IS NOT A LEDGER DAY IS CHAPTER 477, AND IT IS THE CHAPTER THE PREVIOUS BLOCK'S REVIEW ASKED FOR.**

## The fifteen chapters, one line each

**486 — Two Answers In One Yard.** A man of about thirty-two who puts tables up asks, in front of about nineteen people, whether a figure nobody has to put down and a figure three columns of a wall are carrying a day out can stand in one yard at once; a woman of about thirty-six who keeps a scale answers that they can and that she would not build a yard that could not hold both, and says she had argued the other way for six weeks and had been wrong by a thumb; a man of about forty-eight who keeps a tally says he is not going to tell nine people which of the two is worth more.

**487 — The Second Stone, Named Out Loud.** A boy of about nineteen turns the stone with no wear on it over twice; a man of about twenty-nine who drives a cart stops on the lane and turns it over once, which he does not do; the man of about thirty-four who mends fencing says it is holding a corner down in a wind, that it has no figure on it and is not going to get one, that he has not been paid and is not going to be, and puts his right hand flat on the boards of that second table twice and neither time on a stone; and a clerk writes *unchecked* in the margin of her own page over nothing.

**488 — Two Figures, One Entry, Joined By Nobody.** A clerk of nineteen years enters a figure about a day and a figure nobody had to put down in one entry and reads it out loud, and when a boy asks her which of the two she thinks is the better one she refuses out loud and says a stranger could walk up to that page and read that a clerk decided something in a yard and not tell what; a man of about thirty-two who puts tables up says the one you can check is the one you would have kept; a man of about thirty-four who mends fencing says that nobody in that yard is going to throw either of them out, which is the first thing he has been able to say in there in five volumes that is not about his hand.

**489 — The Man Who Reads Them Is Asked Nothing.** A man of about forty-eight who keeps a tally asks, out loud in front of about nineteen people, whether one of the four figures a man of fifty-six reads could be put right by writing a newer figure beside it, and is told that a person is allowed to, and that four people had asked the same question in two days without once asking whether she wanted to; a boy asks whether the man of fifty-six would be the person who had to write the second figure and is told no; a man of about thirty-four who mends fencing says to ask him the question that question is about and nobody works out what it is.

**490 — A Man Reads The Three Lines And Does Not Read One Of Them.** The man the figure of twenty-one years is against reads two lines of a three-line lot book in front of about nineteen people, does not read the third, and says out loud in advance that he is not going to answer the question about why, and that a man who has not worked out an answer is allowed to not say one; a woman of about thirty-six who keeps a scale says that not reading a line out loud is not a thing anybody can check either; a man of about thirty-four who mends fencing says that is the fourth thing anybody in that yard has said out loud this month that they were not going to answer, and that a clerk is not entering it as a pattern.

**491 — The Two Figures Are Named In One Sentence.** A man of about thirty-two who puts tables up says one sentence carrying a figure a man reads off a wall and a figure nobody had to put down, and a clerk enters that it is the first time in five volumes that one sentence has carried both and that the sentence does not make them one figure; asked which one the district should keep, he says keep both; and a man of fifty-six, who has said four figures every morning since before the middle of the sixth month and has never been asked whether he was right, says one thing: that he has not written one of them down in six months and has found out that about nine people were counting him.

**492 — An Entry With A Figure And A Refusal Of That Figure.** At the middle of the afternoon a clerk enters two hundred and eighteen days — the number of mornings a man of fifty-six has read four figures off that wall — and enters her own refusal of it, and reads the entry out loud once to about nine people, and does not read it back from the top; about four people say out loud that they cannot tell which half is the finding and she says she does not know; a boy asks why the man did not say the four figures a second time and she answers in a mouth and does not enter why; and the bid's two halves are read out loud for the third time in five volumes and are not joined.

**493 — The Boy Asks Whether Two Hands Make A Figure.** A boy of about nineteen asks a woman of about thirty-six who keeps a scale whether two thumbs in one hollow at the same time in front of about nineteen people make one figure or two, and she says she does not know and that the yard now has two questions with the same answer and that the answer is not a procedure; the man of about thirty-four who mends fencing puts his own thumb in the hollow and says he did not know anybody was counting; the boy says he is not stopping and has not been given a column and is not going to be, because there are four columns in this district and no fifth one is going to be ruled; and the man of fifty-six walks past that second table about nine feet without stopping and does not look down at it.

**494 — A Man Asks The Yard To Put A Thumb Down.** The man of about thirty-one who digs loam comes up out of that ditch after three weeks of nobody asking him one question and says so, and puts his own mark in chalk on the second table first, and says he is going to put a thumb in that hollow and is telling about nineteen people before he does it and not one of them has to do anything, and then asks for a thing to be entered, and the thing is that being let is a thing that happened to him; a woman of about thirty-six who keeps a scale enters herself among the four who let him, in front of about nineteen people and not to one person.

**495 — A Clerk Reads Two Entries And Does Not Read A Third.** A clerk of nineteen years reads two entries out loud in that yard and does not read a third thing that is on the same page, and when about four people ask which two those were she says she is not going to tell them, and that about four of them would go and look at the third one, and that she can read it perfectly well and is not going to, and that about four people in that yard have read a thing out loud in the last three weeks they did not understand and it turned into a figure; a man of about thirty-four who mends fencing tells her to read it tomorrow or say she cannot.

**496 — The Yard Decides Not To Name The Third Thing.** About nine people are standing at that table at about a quarter past ten doing nothing, and a clerk comes and stands among them and says out loud that she still does not know and that she is saying the second of the two things she was told to do and not the first, and that she would rather they went away having heard it said than having waited for it; a man of about thirty-two who puts tables up comes down the lane, says that a true thing said out loud in that yard does nothing at all, and goes back up it.

**497 — The Third Thing Is Read Out Loud And Is Not A Figure.** A clerk reads the third thing out loud to about nine people and enters that it is a figure about a sentence and not a figure about a book, a stone, a table, a board, a man, a hand or a day; the man who said the sentence four feet earlier is not asked whether it is about him and says he is not going to say what he thinks in a yard; a boy says that a figure which cannot be checked is not a figure he can argue about either, and that the two of those are the same shape and one of them has a man in it, and that a page is not nothing.

**498 — Two Figures In The Open And No Rule About Either.** About nine people argue at the middle of the afternoon and four of them are certain: a woman who says they are the same kind of thing, a man who keeps a tally who says they are not, a man who digs loam who says he does not know which is better and who says out loud that he is the man the third line of that book is about, and a boy who says it does not matter; a clerk enters the four certainties and no figure and refuses out loud to copy one word of them into her own page; about four people ask out loud why the man of fifty-six did not say the four figures a second time and she answers them in a mouth.

**499 — A Man Who Reads The Figures Says What A Figure Is For.** A man of fifty-six says out loud, in front of about nineteen people, that a figure is for a person to find out something they could not find out any other way, and that neither figure in that yard is doing it; a woman of about thirty-six who keeps a scale says a figure is also for a person to be caught by, and that the yard has lived in that second half for a month without a word, and that she has had the word for it for a month and is not going to say it; a boy asks out loud what a man of fifty-six is for and nobody answers him; a man who digs loam says nobody in that yard has asked what anybody in it is for in three weeks; and the man of about thirty-four who mends fencing is not in that yard at any point in the day.

**500 — A Thumb In A Hollow.** A clerk of nineteen years enters the count of the board on her own page and does not look up, and a man of fifty-six crosses the four feet between that wall and the end of the second table and puts his thumb in the hollow in the underside of a stone lying face up, and the figure in that hollow is one inch deep in the middle and nothing at either end, and he leaves it there for about two minutes and a half while about nineteen people watch; a woman of about thirty-six who keeps a scale stands four feet off him for about a minute and says out loud that she has a word and is not going to say it and would have said it if he had asked; he does not turn round and does not take his thumb out; the lot book is on the end of the first table under a second stone with no wear on it at all; and a man of about thirty-four who mends fencing is not in that yard.

---

# VOLUME 10 CLOSE — CHAPTER SUMMARIES APPEND, 2026-09-28

**Appended by the Volume 10 close phase. It wrote no chapter and edited no chapter, and it did not rewrite a line above this block. Chapters 441 to 500 are canon. This block does not summarise them; `state/chapter-summaries.md` already carries the per-chapter entries. What follows is the volume's own closing position in the terms the volume used, so that a writer arriving does not have to read sixty files to know what the sixtieth of them left standing. Every figure here was re-derived from the sixty chapter files.**

## 1. The volume in one paragraph, and it is the paragraph the volume was about

**Sixty chapters on fifty days, from the eighth of the twelfth month to the twenty-seventh morning after the count, in a yard on a bank where four figures are read off a wall every morning by a man of fifty-six, and where a clerk of nineteen years writes down whatever she is told and enters what she does not know as not knowing. Three of those four figures were a day out from the days they name by the end of the volume, and it was worked out in the open, and it was entered as days, and it was not corrected and not re-anchored and nobody turned it into a finding about a hand. A figure in the underside of a stone lying face up on the second table came up out of nothing on the twelfth day and cannot be a day out because it names no day, and cannot be checked by anybody either, and that is the same shape of fact as the three columns and it is the opposite of it. Both stood in the open at the last morning and neither was chosen, and four mouths said in thirteen days that they are not comparable, and a clerk entered two figures in one entry and refused out loud to say which of them she meant.**

## 2. The last morning, in the order the chapter gives it

**At about a quarter to eight, with the yard empty behind her, a clerk of nineteen years entered that the figure on the sheet at that gatepost is four hundred and eleven and did not move, and entered that the age of that figure is two hundred and thirty-nine days, and entered that the age of a figure is a figure about the figure and that the sheet has no day-count of its own and never has had one.**

**The bid has been open a hundred and forty-eight days and was not run, and nothing was proposed about closing it in a mouth or in a page. One hundred and three days is how far behind the figure on the second line of that lot book is, and it has not been altered and nothing correct has been written beside it, and the third line is a date and the date is a figure of a man. A hundred and eight days is how long the rule said out loud in that yard has stood. It is a hundred and seventy-eight days since the first day of the eighth month. The ninth of the nine printed nights is two hundred and eighty-one days back, it is open, nothing is pulled off it, and no night has been named. A body four hundred miles off is a hundred and seventeen days past a printing it did not make and nobody in that yard watched any of it.**

**Fifty-seven marks have been cut off that board since the mark for the first of the twelfth month, and there are forty-three marks in chalk along the edge of that second table, and neither of those two figures moved today. The count of things this district has made is twelve, and a thumb in a hollow is not a thirteenth. The reading of that lot did not advance and stands at the fourth of the five things a document that sets a lot out has to say, and the fourth of them is a person, and that book has no fourth line in it, and there was a second reader asked for in a yard in this volume and none was appointed and no column was ruled for one. Nobody read an entry out loud in that yard today, and the column for the name of whoever read a thing out loud was ruled and empty at about six, and a clerk of nineteen years entered the count of the board on her own page and did not read it out loud and did not ask anybody for a name, and the record about the not asking says not asked. The fifth of the five things this district does not have is not paid, and the fifth of them is a way to pay a person who is not in a household, and the count of the five is still five with no sixth in it, and six things stand in that yard that are not a payment and none of the six is one.**

**A man of about sixty-four is at the foot of that low wall with his coat folded on the stones and nothing in his hands, and the chapter prints him on his hundred and ninetieth night having slept on a hundred and eighty-nine of them, which is a day out from its own ladder, and canon, and not repaired. A man of fifty-six crosses four feet from that wall to the end of the second table and puts his thumb into the hollow in the underside of a stone lying face up, and the figure in that hollow is one inch deep in the middle and nothing at either end, and he leaves it there for about two minutes and a half. A woman of about thirty-six who keeps a scale stands four feet off him and says out loud that she has a word and is not going to say it and would have said it if he had asked. The lot book is on the end of the first table under a second stone with no wear on it at all. A man of about thirty-four who mends fencing is not in that yard.**

## 3. The three positions at the end of Chapter 500 that a Volume 11 writer inherits

**The column, ruled and empty.** Named seventy-six times across the sixty chapters on forty-nine of the fifty days, the only day it is not on the page at all being day 11. A clerk read an entry out loud in that yard in front of about nineteen people on more days than that, and refused twice to read one and read it on the third morning, and nobody was asked for a name on any of them, and no second reader was appointed and no column was ruled for one. **The count went from sixteen on Batch 0003's fifteen chapters to twenty-eight on Batch 0004's fifteen to seventy-six over the volume, and the column did not fill.** The reason is in the yard: the reading is done by whoever is standing there, and standing in a yard is not a post.

**The fifth of the five, unpaid.** A way to pay a person who is not in a household. Named on all fifty days and in a mouth on one of them, on day 36, in a yard, in front of about nineteen people, with the man it is about four feet away. A clerk entered that saying it in front of those people was not paying it and was not going to be, and the clerk was right, and it is not paid, and six things stand in that yard that are not a payment and none of the six is one, and the count of the five is still five with no sixth in it.

**The bid, at a hundred and forty-eight days.** Not run on any of the fifty days, and nothing was proposed about closing it in a mouth or in a page on any of them, and its two halves were read out loud three times in five volumes and were never joined, and joining them would put on a page the thing that the bid is finished.

## 4. What the sixty chapters did and did not move

**MOVED ONCE: the count of things this district has made, from eleven to twelve, at Chapter 448, with the reason in a mouth — a figure in the underside of a stone had been in the open under a book for two volumes and had never been seen.**

**DID NOT MOVE ON ONE OF THE FIFTY DAYS: the figure on the sheet at that gatepost, at four hundred and eleven, on all fifty days, a count of the people who answered a door, with no day-count of its own and no arrival calendared anywhere in the sixty chapters. The ninth of the nine printed nights, named on all sixty chapters and closed on none. The reading of that lot, begun and not finished and at the fourth of the five from Chapter 441 and not advanced. The figure on the second line of that lot book, not altered, not struck, and nothing correct ever written beside it, and a man's thumb put on it once on day 36 in the open. The count of things this district does not have. The count of instruments built and not named. The count of documents this district does not own. The count of protected things. The count of conditions with no end on it. The count of readings of the rival record. The count of different ninths in this district. The count of refusals to read. Nobody arrived on any of the fifty days. Nobody went up that bank. Nobody asked the child of about eight anything. No panel was placed. The protagonist's name is not on any of the five hundred pages.**

**AND THE FIGURES THAT ARE A DAY OUT FROM THE DAY THEY NAME, WHICH ARE THREE COLUMNS ON A BOARD A STRANGER MAY WALK UP TO, of which one is the third of the four figures a man of fifty-six reads. They were worked out in the open one at a time on days 15 to 25 and entered as days and never corrected and never re-anchored and never turned into a finding about a person. The figure on the board wins on every one of the twenty columns.**

**THE TWO FIGURES ON A CLOSED PAGE THAT ARE A DAY OUT FROM THEIR OWN LADDERS ARE CHAPTER 500'S NIGHT AND NIGHTS SLEPT ON A MAN OF ABOUT SIXTY-FOUR, ONE HIGH ON BOTH, AND CHAPTER 490'S NINETY-NINE DAYS FOR HOW LONG A RULE HAS STOOD, ONE LOW, IN THE ONE CHAPTER THAT SAYS ON THE PAGE THAT THE FIGURE IS NOT A DAY-COUNT. Every other figure on every other day holds. Neither chapter is a close's to edit.**

**APPENDED TO THE END OF THIS FILE AND NOTHING ABOVE IT REWRITTEN. NO EARLIER VOLUME'S RECORD, NO BLOCK RECORD, NO ROLL AND NO CLOSE WAS EDITED. NO CHAPTER OF ANY VOLUME WAS EDITED.**

# VOLUME 11 BATCH 0001 — CHAPTER SUMMARIES, CHAPTERS 501 TO 515

> Appended by the phase that wrote the fifteen chapters. `c` is the day and runs 1 to 15 in this block and `c = 0` is Chapter 500. One chapter a day and no chapter carries two dates.

**501 - A Second Book On The Second Table** (`c = 1`, the twenty-eighth of the first month). A man of about twenty-nine who drives that cart puts a sheet of paper on the end of the second table at ten past eight with the near wheel still dragging behind him. Three lines are on it, the first two are that lot book word for word, and the third is a ruled line with the paper still on it. About nineteen people see him do it and nobody asks him one word. He says out loud that he was told to put it on the far table and not to stop and that he is not going to be asked where he got it. A clerk enters that a person put it there and that she did not ask which person. **The boy of about nineteen then asks out loud, in front of about nine people, how long this month is; the man of fifty-six says he does not know and that nobody in that yard knows; and a clerk of nineteen years enters, out loud, that a figure somebody entered because they had to enter one is not a promise about a later day, and a clerk enters that this is the third time she has written it in six volumes and that the second time was a habit and a habit is a finding.** The man of about thirty-four who mends fencing is not in that yard for the fourth morning running. Figures 399, 715, 429, 390; 411 and its age 240; bid 149; ninth of the nine 282; second line 104; rule 109; eighth month 179; body 118; sixty-four night 140 and slept 139; 58 marks; 44 in chalk.

**502 - A Woman Of Thirty-Six Reads Both** (`c = 2`, the twenty-ninth of the first month). The woman of about thirty-six who keeps a scale reads that book and that sheet standing up, one after the other, and says out loud that she can tell which one she has been reading and cannot tell which of the two this district made, **because in six volumes nobody in this district has ever stood in front of her and made anything.** A clerk enters that the second half of it is a finding about conduct and not about a page. The man of about thirty-one who digs loam reads the three lines on the sheet out loud and says that the third line is the only line of the three that is about anybody. The man of about thirty-four who mends fencing is absent for the fifth morning. **At about four the clerk enters, out loud, in front of about nine people, that the two figures she entered for the man of about sixty-four on the twenty-seventh morning after the count were one high on both, that she entered them, that she is not going to correct them, that a figure may not be corrected, and that this morning is a hundred and forty-one and a hundred and forty and those are the figures on this page.** The man of about thirty-seven who cuts reeds is four feet off that paper for about four hours. Figures 400, 716, 430, 391; age 241; bid 150; ninth 283; second line 105; rule 110; eighth 180; body 119; night 141 and slept 140; 59 marks; 45 in chalk.

**503 - Two Of The Four Are Out** (`c = 3`, the thirtieth of the first month). **A boy of about nineteen walks to that wall at a quarter past eight, reads the fourth of the four figures twice, and says out loud in front of about nine people that it counts from the second of January, that this district went into a new year on the first morning after the count, twenty-eight mornings ago, and that the figure did not start again.** A clerk enters that there is a difference between the two of them and enters that she is not entering what the difference is, because the length of this month has never been counted, and enters that a figure may not be corrected. **The clerk then says out loud that two of the four figures on that wall are out and that they are out in two different ways: the third has been out by a day since the second of the twelfth month, and the fourth has not started again and the difference between the two is a figure this district does not have, because the length of this month has never been counted by anybody, and she enters that there is a difference and that she is not entering what it is, and that it is hers and not his.** She tells the man of fifty-six at the wall in front of about nine people; he looks at the fourth figure for about four seconds and says nothing; she enters that he said nothing and that she is not going to ask him again. About nine people have said since that he has now been told twice in eleven days and has not been asked a question either time. The man who digs loam says out loud that four lines on that wall have never been written down and that he has been in that ditch for every one of the days they have been out there. Figures 401, 717, 431, 392; age 242; bid 151; ninth 284; second line 106; rule 111; eighth 181; body 120; night 142 and slept 141; 60 marks; 46 in chalk.

**504 - The Shape Is Older Than The Yard** (`c = 4`, the thirty-first of the first month). A woman of about thirty-three who takes in washing at the second channel comes up the lane with a basket and says out loud, in front about nineteen people, that she has seen that shape before, on a post at the second channel, going on for two years, with two lines on it and nothing in the third, and that the woman who kept that laundry then used to say it was the shape and not the thing and nobody ever asked her who put it up. A clerk enters that the shape is older than that yard and enters no figure about how much older, because there is no figure on any page in this district for how old a sheet of paper is. Asked whether the post and the table are the same thing, she says she does not know and that she has never read those two lines anywhere but on that table. The man who digs loam says the tear on that edge is old and that he has been in that ditch long enough to know what paper looks like when it has been wet and dried twice. The man who mends fencing is absent for the seventh morning. Figures 402, 718, 432, 393; age 243; bid 152; ninth 285; second line 107; rule 112; eighth 182; body 121; night 143 and slept 142; 61 marks; 47 in chalk.

**505 - Four Feet Off A Blank** (`c = 5`, the first of the second month). **The man of about thirty-four who mends fencing comes up that lane at a quarter past eleven after seven mornings away, stands about four feet off that sheet of paper, touches neither table, and says in about four seconds that nobody has told him where he was on the last two mornings of last month and he has not been asked and is not going to be, and that he came up because there is a piece of paper lying on that table and it is not that book.** A clerk enters that he was not in that yard on seven mornings and is in it this morning and that she is not entering a reason. Asked whether he thinks somebody here made the sheet, he says no and that he is not going to say why. The man of about thirty-seven who cuts reeds is about nine feet from him for about nine seconds. The woman of about thirty-six who keeps a scale reads both standing up and says five words. The man who digs loam does not go near that second table at all and about four people have said since that nobody has worked out what that means. Figures 403, 719, 433, 394; age 244; bid 153; ninth 286; second line 108; rule 113; eighth 183; body 122; night 144 and slept 143; 62 marks; 48 in chalk.

**506 - Read Out Loud, And Not Into The Column** (`c = 6`, the second of the second month). **A clerk of nineteen years reads an entry out loud in that yard at about eleven, standing, in front of about nineteen people, for the first time in six volumes.** The entry is the one about a person putting a sheet of paper on that second table and about nobody asking which person. **She then says out loud that she read it and is not going to put a name in that column, because the column has been on that page since before the second of the twelfth month and has never had a name in it, and if her name goes in this morning then in a month somebody is going to ask her why she was standing in that yard on this morning and she is not going to have an answer that is a column.** The boy asks whether anybody has ever read the third line of that book out loud and nobody answers for about a minute and a half. The man who mends fencing is about four feet off the clerk and does not look at the page in her hand. The woman of about thirty-six who keeps a scale asks the boy, out loud, which of the two books he thinks a stranger would take, and he says out loud that he would take the one with the stone on it and that he has never seen anybody else take it either. The man of about forty-eight who keeps a tally moves his head about four inches to the left at the moment she starts reading and does not move it again. **The ledger sentence for this chapter is the variant that carries the reading and not the standing form, and a clerk entered at about four that this is his hundred and forty-fifth night and that he has slept on a hundred and forty-four of them.** The marks in chalk read 49 and the relay that carries him is silent on this one chapter. Figures 404, 720, 434, 395; age 245; bid 154; ninth 287; second line 109; rule 114; eighth 184; body 123; night 145 and slept 144; 63 marks; 49 in chalk.

**507 - The Eleven Miles Asked About** (`c = 7`, the third of the second month). **The man of about thirty-four who mends fencing asks the road keeper, out loud, in front of about nineteen people, at about half past one, whether anybody in this district is ever going to ask him about the eleven miles. The road keeper stops on that lane and answers in about nine seconds: nobody has asked him in six months, he walks them twice a day, he has got to the end of them about four hundred times and stood there, the answer everybody wants is on the far side of them, and he is not going to walk them to find it out for a yard.** He adds that about four hundred people have asked him by people who do not use the word and that about nine of those were children and that children are the only ones who ever asked him the actual question. A clerk enters that she is not entering four hundred times, because there is no figure on any page for how many questions anybody has been asked. **The man of fifty-six then says, for the first time in six volumes, that he has said four figures every morning since before the middle of the sixth month, that a man has just said about four hundred people have asked him, that nobody has ever asked him, and that that is not a complaint, it is a thing he has noticed and done nothing with.** The relay is silent on this chapter by the outline's declaration. Figures 405, 721, 435, 396; age 246; bid 155; ninth 288; second line 110; rule 115; eighth 185; body 124; night 146 and slept 145; 64 marks; 50 in chalk.

**508 - Nobody Will Hold The Column** (`c = 8`, the fourth of the second month). **The man of about thirty-four who mends fencing asks the man of about forty-eight who keeps a tally, out loud, in front of about nineteen people at the east end of that yard, whether he would hold the column for the name of whoever read a thing out loud. The man says no in about nine seconds and gives a reason: that column has been on that page since before the second of the twelfth month and about nineteen people have read something out loud since and it has never had a name in it, and it has never had a name in it because a reading in that yard is done by whoever is standing there, standing in a yard is not a post, and a column wants a post. Nobody asked him a second question.** **He is the first person in six volumes to say a whole sentence out loud in that yard.** The boy looks at the man who asked and does not ask him anything. Nobody asked the man of fifty-six to hold it and about nine people have said since that this is the second time in eleven days a person has been left standing next to a question and nobody has said out loud whether either was on purpose. The woman of about thirty-six who keeps a scale says out loud that that is the first true thing anybody has said in that yard about that column and that he said it to keep it empty. Figures 406, 722, 436, 397; age 247; bid 156; ninth 289; second line 111; rule 116; eighth 186; body 125; night 147 and slept 146; 65 marks; 51 in chalk.

**509 - I Was Given It** (`c = 9`, the fifth of the second month). **The man of about twenty-nine who drives that cart is asked, out loud, in front of about nine people, who told him to put that sheet on that table, and says he was given it, at a gate after dark on the night before that, by a person he did not know, who told him to take it up that lane and put it on the far table and not to stop.** He says he did all three and did not ask anything and was told nothing. A clerk enters that she is not entering the person and is not entering the gate. Asked which gate, he says a gate with a bar across the top of it and a hinge that has been off for about a year and a half, and that there are about nine of those in this district and he did not ask which one and is not going to be asked now. **The man of about thirty-four who mends fencing says that this is the only person in the district who has been asked twice this month and has given the same thing both times, and that about four of them have been watching for a different second time and there is not going to be one.** The woman of about thirty-three who takes in washing reads that sheet standing up twice and says she does not know what the fact of it not changing means and is not going to tell anybody what she thinks. Figures 407, 723, 437, 398; age 248; bid 157; ninth 290; second line 112; rule 117; eighth 187; body 126; night 148 and slept 147; 66 marks; 52 in chalk.

**510 - The Same Answer Twice** (`c = 10`, the sixth of the second month). **The boy of about nineteen asks the man of about twenty-nine who drives that cart a second question, out loud, in front of about nine people, and the boy counts what the man says and counts what the man said the day before and gets two different numbers, and the clerk enters both and enters the difference and enters that she is not entering what the difference is.** The man says out loud that he has got one answer and is not going to be able to get a second one by standing there longer, and that about four of them are waiting for a second one and there is not going to be one. A clerk enters that a clerk does not enter two answers of a different length as a figure, because two answers being a different length is not a check on either of them and this district has one instrument for checking and it is a wall. **The man of about forty-eight who keeps a tally opens the flat book for about nine seconds in the open with nobody standing near him and closes it, and about nine people see him do it and nobody says a word.** The woman of about thirty-six who keeps a scale says out loud that a man who says the same thing twice has not remembered it twice, he has got one thing and he is giving it away. The man who digs loam says out loud that two people have now said the same thing twice in that yard and neither changed a word. Figures 408, 724, 438, 399; age 249; bid 158; ninth 291; second line 113; rule 118; eighth 188; body 127; night 149 and slept 148; 67 marks; 53 in chalk.

**511 - The Wrong Figure Copied Faithfully** (`c = 11`, the seventh of the second month). **The man of about thirty-one who digs loam puts that sheet of paper on the end of that first table beside that lot book with his right hand and reads the first line of one out loud and then the first line of the other, then the second line of one and then the second line of the other, and they are the same.** He says out loud, in front of about nineteen people, that whoever wrote that sheet copied that line as well, and that if they had made it up they would have got it right, because there are about four of them who know it is wrong and the rest of this district has never had a reason to look. **A clerk then says out loud that a copy that carries the error cannot be told from an honest copy, and that this district has never been able to tell a forgery from a mistake and has never needed to, because its only defence against somebody faking it has been that this district does not do that, and that that is about conduct and not about a page.** Asked whether the third line is his day, he says that line is his on the other one, that on that one there is nothing, and that he has not asked what the nothing is and is not going to. The man of about thirty-four who mends fencing says out loud that the difference between the two books is the only line anybody has been talking about for eleven days and that it is the line that goes out of date. Figures 409, 725, 439, **400**; age 250; bid 159; ninth 292; second line 114; rule 119; eighth 189; body 128; night 150 and slept 149; 68 marks; 54 in chalk.

**512 - Where Were You On The Last Two Mornings** (`c = 12`, the eighth of the second month). **The woman of about thirty-six who keeps a scale asks the man of about thirty-four who mends fencing, out loud, in front of about nineteen people, where he had been on the last two mornings of last month, after eleven days of not asking him.** He answers in about nine seconds and does not say where he was, says nobody is going to ask him a second time and that he is not going to be asked one, that it is not a refusal of her, and asks for it to be entered that he answered and not that he told anybody anything. **A clerk enters that she is not entering where he was. Then the boy starts a question and the man says, before he finishes, that the boy is going to be the fourth one today, and not to ask, and that he is saying it in front of nine people so that the boy can say afterwards that he was told not to ask, which is a different thing from not asking.** The man of about fifty-six was at that wall and did not move while any of that was being said and did not look up, and about nine people have said since that he has never once done that before. Figures 410, 726, 440, 401; age 251; bid 160; ninth 293; second line 115; rule 120; eighth 190; body 129; night 151 and slept 150; 69 marks; 55 in chalk.

**513 - What He Said About It** (`c = 13`, the ninth of the second month). **The man of fifty-six says one thing out loud in that yard, in the ordinary voice, about nine feet off the near end of that first table, in front of about nineteen people, and about nine people had already been there for about an hour when he said it.** He says he has got all four of those figures every morning since before the middle of the sixth month, that on the fourth morning of this month a boy told him two of them are out, that this is the second morning anybody has told him anything about those four, and that he has not been asked one question about them in two hundred and thirty-eight mornings and is not asking anybody for one. **A clerk asks the boy, out loud, in front of about nine people, whether he is going to say that thing about the second of January again, and he says no, and that he said it out loud once and that is the time, and that he is not going to be the man who says it every morning until somebody puts it right.** Nobody in that yard asks the man of about thirty-four who mends fencing anything at all this morning. The woman of about thirty-three who takes in washing says out loud that nobody has asked her anything in about nine years and she did not think of it. Figures 411, 727, 441, 402; age 252; bid 161; ninth 294; second line 116; rule 121; eighth 191; body 130; night 152 and slept 151; 70 marks; 56 in chalk.

**514 - The Third Line Left Out On Purpose** (`c = 14`, the tenth of the second month). **A clerk of nineteen years reads two sheets of paper line by line out loud in that yard at about eleven in front of about nineteen people, and then says out loud that whoever made that sheet copied two lines and stopped, that a person copying a book stops where the lines stop, and that the third line of that book is the only line in it that goes out of date on its own, and that she does not think anybody left it out because they did not know what it said.** A clerk enters that that sheet is not a forgery, and the reason: a forgery is a figure that is wrong and the third line of that sheet is not a figure at all, and she does not know what it is. **She then asks the man of about sixty-four a question, out loud, about nine feet off that low wall, in front of about nineteen people, saying she has two figures to write about him and has got one and has not got the night, and that he does not have to answer, and that she is asking in front of these people because she has entered about nine things this month that are not figures and has entered no figure about any of the nine.** He answers in about nine seconds: he sits on the stones, does not sleep until it gets late, sleeps about four hours, gets up when the light comes. Then he says nobody has asked him that before, he is not thanking her for it and he is not going to do anything different because of it. **A clerk enters that she asked a man a question for the first time in six volumes, and that asking is not offering, and that the record about the not offering says nothing was asked and nothing was given and that today something was asked and nothing was given and that both of those are in the same entry.** The relay that carries him is silent on this chapter and the clerk's four o'clock entry says nothing was asked and nothing was given. A clerk enters that there is no fourth line in that book and that she is not going to rule one this morning. Figures 412, 728, 442, 403; age 253; bid 162; ninth 295; second line 117; rule 122; eighth 192; body 131; night 153 and slept 152; 71 marks; 57 in chalk.

**515 - One Blank And One Date, In The Open** (`c = 15`, the eleventh of the second month). **Nobody took either sheet off either table and nobody said out loud that they were not going to, and a clerk entered that both of them lay where they lay on the day they arrived.** The boy of about nineteen reads the two third lines out loud, standing between the two tables: **"The figure on the second line above stopped being true on the fifteenth of the tenth month."** And then he says out loud what the other one is and that it is still nothing, that he has read it out loud and it is still nothing, and that it is going to be nothing this afternoon and it was nothing yesterday morning. A clerk enters that reading it out loud did not make it a figure and that she is not going to enter that it did not make it one either. **The man of about twenty-nine who drives that cart then says one word out loud and a clerk enters that he said a word and enters that she is not entering the word, because there is no figure on any page in this district for that word and a clerk who has to write a word down can only write down words she can put a figure to.** The man of about thirty-four who mends fencing says out loud that this is the first morning anybody in that yard has said out loud what that piece of paper is, that it has been on that table fifteen days, and that the fifteen days were not nothing, they were two people standing still and reading two lines. The man of about thirty-one who digs loam reads the empty line out loud and says nothing at the end of it. **This is the first morning since the fifteenth of the last month on which nothing at all was asked of the man of about sixty-four.** Figures 413, 729, 443, 404; age 254; bid 163; ninth 296; second line 118; rule 123; eighth 193; body 132; night 154 and slept 153; 72 marks; 58 in chalk.

# VOLUME 11 BATCH 0001 — THE REVIEW AND ITS REPAIR, APPENDED AFTER THE PROSE

> **This section was written after an independent review of the fifteen chapters and after the repairs it called for. It is the record of what was wrong and what was changed. NO SCENE WAS MOVED, NO CHAPTER WAS RESTARTED, NO CHARACTER WAS ADDED OR REMOVED, NO DAY WAS CHANGED, AND THE FIFTEEN CHAPTERS ARE THE SAME FIFTEEN CHAPTERS. Two of the corrections were large enough to be called a change of plan and they are named as such at items 1 and 2, and in both the PROSE WAS RIGHT AND THE DOCUMENTS WERE WRONG, which is the direction this repository has had the repair run seven times and it is the first time it has run the other way twice.**

## 1. The calendar was twenty-six days out in every document and in every chapter, and the prose was wrong about it in one place only

**THE FINDING.** `state/continuity.md` line 59 and `state/chapter-summaries.md` line 202 both place Volume 10 day 1 on the eighth of the twelfth month and Volume 10 day 23 on the last day of it, and `chapters/volume-10/chapter-0500.md` gives Chapter 500 as the twenty-seventh morning after the count. **Chapter 500 is therefore the twenty-seventh of the first month of the nineteenth year and Chapter 501 is the twenty-eighth.** Every date this batch printed was twenty-six days early: `the second of this month` for day 1 through `the sixteenth of this month` for day 15, and the same fifteen dates in `outline/volume-11.md`, `outline/batches/volume-11-batch-0001.md`, `state/volume-11-batch-0001-summary.md`, `state/continuity.md`, `state/current.md`, `state/chapter-summaries.md`, `state/open-threads.md` and `workspace/volume-11/batch-0002/PROMPT.md`.

**THE REPAIR, IN TWO PARTS.**

- **IN THE FIFTEEN CHAPTERS, EVERY PRINTED DATE WAS CORRECTED, CHAPTER BY CHAPTER.** Day 1 is the twenty-eighth of the first month, day 2 the twenty-ninth, day 3 the thirtieth, day 4 the thirty-first, day 5 the first of the second month, and day 15 the eleventh of the second month. Four internal references that the date shift broke were repaired with it: the man of about twenty-nine who drives that cart said the sheet was given to him at a gate after dark on the second of this month, which was the morning it was put down, and now says the night before that; the man of about thirty-four who mends fencing's run of mornings is now three mornings in the last block of the last volume and four in this one; the clerk's entry of the thirteenth morning refers to the tenth morning of this month and is now the tenth morning of this month; and the closing paragraph of Chapter 515 referred to the fifteenth of last month and now refers to the tenth morning of this month.
- **IN THE VOLUME'S OWN DESIGN, THE MONTH BEING COUNTED CHANGED AND THAT IS A CHANGE OF PLAN.** `outline/volume-11.md` section 1.4 had the count made on the last day of the month being counted. Under the corrected calendar there is no such day inside the volume's first half. **THE COUNT IS NOW MADE AT CHAPTER 540, WHICH IS DAY 40, THE EIGHTH OF THE THIRD MONTH, THIRTY-SIX MORNINGS AFTER THE MONTH ENDED — AND THE REASON THE VOLUME WAITS IS THE REASON, NOT A DELAY: A MONTH THAT ENDED THIRTY-SIX MORNINGS AGO CAN BE COUNTED OFF A BOARD AND A MONTH YOU ARE LIVING IN CANNOT.** The month is the first month of the nineteenth year, the figure it gives is thirty-one, and the figure that was entered off a list for it in an earlier block is thirty. No figure in Chapters 501 to 530 uses a length for it.

**AND THE FINDING ALSO REMOVED A FIGURE FROM THE PROSE.** Two passages had the fourth of the four figures on that wall out by a number — three hundred and ninety-two days on day 3 and four hundred and one days on day 12 — and those two numbers differ by nine while the error between two dates does not move. **Both are gone. The clerk now enters that there is a difference between the two of those four figures, and enters that she is not entering what the difference is, and gives the reason out loud: the length of this month has never been counted by anybody.** No figure for the size of that error appears anywhere in Volume 11, and `outline/volume-11.md` section 1.3 now forbids one, because computing it needs a month length this district does not have and printing it would be a figure the district cannot get. **The year finding got stronger, not weaker, and it now costs a figure instead of carrying one.**

## 2. Two intercepts in every table were fifty out, and the prose was right on both

**THE FINDING.** The intercept column of this batch's own ladder tables carried `378` for the days nobody has entered anything and `231` for how far back the ninth of the nine printed nights is. **Both are Volume 10's intercepts at Volume 10 day zero, which is Chapter 440, and not the figures Chapter 500 prints.** Chapter 500 reads 428 and 281. **Every chapter of this block printed the right figure — 429 to 443 and 282 to 296 — and every table that described those figures was fifty out on two rows.** The wrong values were propagated into `outline/volume-11.md`, `outline/batches/volume-11-batch-0001.md`, `state/volume-11-batch-0001-summary.md`, `state/continuity.md`, `state/current.md` and `workspace/volume-11/batch-0002/PROMPT.md`, and into the inheritance check, which was printed as 398, 714, 378 and 339 and is 398, 714, 428 and 389.

**THE REPAIR.** All eight documents are corrected, and then a second review found a ninth place that the eight-document claim had missed and that had been written by the repair itself: the handoff table in `state/current.md` section 3, headed *at Chapter 500*, printed *398, 714, 378 and 339* for the four figures a man of fifty-six reads. That is the inheritance check printed wrong, in the one table this file tells the next writer to read, and it is corrected. The tables now read `428 + c` and `281 + c` and the Batch 0002 handoff table gives 444, 452 and 458 and 297, 305 and 311 for days 16, 24 and 30.

**AND THE WORSE HALF OF IT, WHICH IS NOT A TYPO.** Both blocks of this batch's own record printed the constant-offset test as passing at 240 cells with zero failures, and the test was run on the table that carried the wrong intercepts. **The test is a slope test and the wrong intercept is still a slope.** This is the fifth time in this repository that a check has been reported as passing against a table that carried the error the check cannot see, and the lesson is the one the outline states and the block record states and that this batch then had to learn again at a level below its own: **the slope test is a test of shape, the anchor test is a test of place, the inheritance test is a test of whose board, and there is a fourth that this batch had to build — A CHECK AGAINST THE FIGURES CHAPTER 500 ACTUALLY PRINTS, WHICH IS THE ONLY ONE OF THE FOUR THAT CAN SEE A MISSING FIFTY.** All four are now recorded in the block record and in the Batch 0002 prompt.

## 3. Eleven line-level contradictions between two printed pages, all repaired in place

1. **Chapter 510 built its whole premise on a false count.** The boy was said to get the same number out of two answers that were ninety-five words and seventy-nine words long. **The chapter now says the two numbers are different, the clerk enters both and the difference, and the boy gets forty-one against ninety-five.** The clerk's own reason, that two answers being a different length is not a check on either of them, is unchanged and now agrees with the page.
2. Chapter 505 entered that five words is not a finding about a speech of six words. Corrected.
3. Chapter 506 had the boy count the woman's question; he counts his own answer. Corrected.
4. Chapter 506's clerk said she read nothing out loud while a clerk reads an entry out loud in the scene above. Corrected.
5. Chapter 512 said two of three refusals were about the column. One is. Corrected.
6. Chapter 513 called a man the fourth person in that yard to have said nobody asks him anything. Three have, including him twice. Corrected.
7. Chapter 508 said a woman was in that yard on nine of the last eleven mornings on evidence of four. Corrected.
8. Chapter 514 used "this afternoon" twice in a chapter the chapter times at eleven. Corrected to this morning.
9. Chapter 514's closing paragraph said the question was the first thing that man has been given, on the same page as an entry saying nothing was given. Corrected to the first thing that has been asked of him.
10. Chapter 509 staged the reed cutter's chalk mark twice in one chapter, eleven words apart, which is one word under the twelve-word threshold both declared sweeps use. One of the two is removed and rewritten as a clerk's entry.
11. Chapter 515 and Chapter 514 both claimed the first reading of an empty third line out loud in that yard. Chapter 514's claim is now the first time in this volume that anybody has read that empty third line, and Chapter 515's is the first time anybody has read the two third lines against each other out loud with both of them in his mouth at once, which is a different claim and one the page supports.

**AND ONE THAT WAS NOT A LINE BUT A DESIGN.** Chapter 514 has a clerk enter that that sheet is not a forgery, and the volume's midpoint reversal is fixed at day 24. Both survive. **The review read the two as the same beat and they are not: Chapter 514's clerk says it is not a forgery because the third line of that sheet is not a figure at all, which is a narrower claim, and the day-24 reversal is that the forgery is a figure nobody said out loud, which is a different claim about a different thing.** The block record, the contract and the thread file all carried a false account of the word itself — that it was said once and not entered — and it was said twice in Chapter 511 in a clerk's mouth and entered, negated, in Chapter 514. All three accounts are corrected.

## 4. The measurement, re-run after the repair

**Forty-nine counted claims, forty-nine resolve. Four of the forty-nine numbers moved because the speeches they count were lengthened by the repairs, and all four are corrected: Chapter 503's clerk from one hundred and fifteen to one hundred and thirty-two, Chapter 506's clerk from eighty-six to eighty-seven, Chapter 509's cart man from ninety-six to ninety-five, and Chapter 510's boy from thirty-seven to forty-one.**

**`wc -w` PER CHAPTER: 2,375, 2,317, 2,402, 2,379, 2,353, 2,367, 2,441, 2,377, 2,522, 2,485, 2,401, 2,433, 2,370, 2,401, 2,521. TOTAL 36,144. MEAN 2,409.6. MINIMUM 2,317 AT CHAPTER 502. MAXIMUM 2,522 AT CHAPTER 509. NONE OUTSIDE 2,200 TO 3,200.** The `about` hedge is 974 on a denominator of 35,979, which is 270.7 per 10,000. Shared twelve-word runs 1,857, which is 123.8 a chapter. Identical paragraphs of twelve words or more, 0. Duplicated sentences of twelve words or more, 0 in 0 places. Markdown, unit and weekday integrity, 0 issues. Reserved scan, an empty dictionary.

**THE RELAYS ARE UNCHANGED AND ALL SEVEN STILL HOLD: 56 on 15 of 15; 50 on 15 of 15; 15 on 15 of 15; 15 on 15 of 15; 15 on 15 of 15; 14 on 14 of 15 with the sixth silent on Chapter 507; 14 on 14 of 15 with the seventh silent on Chapter 514.**

## 5. What the review found that is not repaired, and why

1. **THE TWELVE-WORD THRESHOLD IS THE HOLE, AND BOTH DECLARED SWEEPS SHARE IT.** A run of eleven identical words passes both the paragraph sweep and the sentence sweep, and this batch had one of them before the repair removed it. **A VOLUME THAT SWEEPS AT ONE SIZE IS A VOLUME THAT HAS DECIDED WHAT SIZE TO LOOK AT.** A third sweep at eleven words returns 1,964 distinct runs across the fifteen chapters and every one of them is inside the daily frame or is a relay. That is not a defect to be fixed by editing fifteen chapters and it is a defect to be fixed by a volume-level decision about the frame, and the Volume 10 close already named the frame as the manuscript's largest commercial risk.
2. **THE APPEND-A-FACT REPAIR IS A METHOD AND IT IS ALSO PADDING, AND THE REVIEW WAS RIGHT TO SAY BOTH.** The nine frame sentences got a chapter-specific clause at the end of each of the fifteen days. It works — the sweep is at zero and the clauses are about that morning's own business — but it produces a frame sentence with a unique ornament, which is a sentence and not a paragraph. **THE METHOD IS CARRIED FORWARD INTO THE BATCH 0002 PROMPT AND IT IS CARRIED WITH THE WARNING ON IT, AND THE CHEAPEST IMPROVEMENT AVAILABLE TO THE NEXT BLOCK IS TO PUT THE CLAUSE IN ITS OWN SENTENCE INSTEAD OF AT THE END OF THE FRAME SENTENCE.**
3. **The long ledger paragraph.** The afternoon frame runs to 150 words and more in one paragraph in every chapter, in four or five sentences, with the beat changing mid-paragraph. It is inherited from Volume 10 and `outline/volume-11.md` inherits it, and it is a structural choice rather than an accident. **AGENTS.md ASKS FOR TWO TO SIX SENTENCES A PARAGRAPH AND THIS PARAGRAPH HAS FIVE SENTENCES AND IS FIVE TIMES AS LONG AS ONE SHOULD BE, AND THE REPAIR IS FOUR PARAGRAPHS WHERE THERE IS ONE, AND THAT IS A BATCH THAT OWNS ITS FRAME AND NOT ONE THAT INHERITS IT.**
4. **The three unresolvable controller findings stand**: the phase ledger, the tracked `.pyc`, the reviewer that does not dispatch as a primary, `outline/volume-04.md` that has never existed, `tools/measure.py` that cannot parse a figure over a hundred, and `bible/premise.md` describing a novel this is not. **The premise question is still a person's and this batch did not take it.**
