The man who mends fencing says he is not going to be glad, because the day this yard can count a month is the day after which it will want to count a second one. The man of about twenty-nine who drives that cart says he put that sheet on that table on the first morning of this volume and has never read it. |
| **534** | 34 | second of the third | **The woman of about thirty-six who keeps a scale asks the clerk, out loud, in front of about nineteen people, whether a count of the length of a month is going to tell her which of the four pages in this district was the one this district made, and the clerk answers in about four seconds that it is not.** The woman says she wants it entered that she asked and was told and that she is not going to be one of about nine people pleased about a number while four pages sit in four places. **A clerk says out loud that she is not going to name a day in a yard, on the ground that a clerk who names a day has made a plan in the open.** The man who mends fencing says a yard can be watching on purpose on any morning and that what a yard cannot do is decide on a morning that everybody in it is watching on purpose. He says no, keep them, and says he would rather she had not put a third question in a yard, and that about four people standing behind her have not noticed that a question about whether a man minds is not a question about a sheet of paper, and that she had noticed it because she had asked it. THE FIRST MORNING IN SIX VOLUMES ON WHICH A CLERK ENTERED LESS THAN SHE HEARD.** A boy asks her how many figures she keeps about that man, she says one word, the boy does not count it, and a clerk enters that she answered it and that she is not entering the answer. A clerk says out loud that a sentence she has entered more than once has not once been about the right thing. The man of about twenty-nine who drives that cart says that is four things in that yard that are not on a page and that he has been standing in it for all four. |

A clerk enters that a clerk named a day out loud in a yard and that this district has not had that in six volumes.** She gives the reason for the eighth out loud, which is the light, and that a figure you cannot see is a figure somebody has to take on trust. **The man of about forty-eight who keeps a tally says he is going to read one line out of that book in the open on that morning, and is not going to be asked to and is not going to be thanked for it.** The man who mends fencing says he wishes the number were not coming, because a stranger can read it and then a stranger can ask what a page is for and this yard has not got an answer to that. |

The man who mends fencing says nobody has ever asked that man what the mark is for and that he is not going to ask it now, three days before a count, because it would look like a thing he wants. | **538** | 38 | sixth of the third | **The man of about thirty-four who mends fencing begins a question to the man of about fifty-six at about two in the afternoon, gets about six words into it, and does not finish it, and a clerk enters that a man began a question and did not finish it and that nobody asked him to finish it. The man of about fifty-six turns round from that wall, which he has not done at a question in about two hundred and sixty-three mornings, and says out loud that he is not going to help him and is not going to refuse him either, because a refusal out of him that afternoon in front of all of those is a figure about him in a yard, and he has had one of those a month.** The boy says out loud that a man began a question and did not finish it so that about nineteen people have it and not just him. THE COUNT GIVES THIRTY-ONE.** The man of about forty-eight who keeps a tally takes the flat book out, opens it on the boards of that first table, puts his own finger on a line, and reads it out loud, and the figure is thirty. | **541** | 41 | **THE YARD ASKED WHAT THE SHEET ON THAT SECOND TABLE WAS FOR AND A CLERK SAID SHE DID NOT KNOW, AND FOUR WORDS WENT ON A PAGE OF THIS DISTRICT.** A woman of about thirty-three who takes in washing set a basket on those boards because the step into that yard is two inches and asked in front of about nineteen people. The clerk said `I do not know` and entered it, and about four people have said since that this district has never had a page that said it. A man who mends fencing said a thing is for whoever has to find out what it was for and that it is not finished being wrong yet. A man who keeps a tally said a document is for the day somebody needs it and that he would not burn his. The man of about twenty-nine who drives that cart said he has not read that sheet one word since he put it down. |
| **542** | 42 | **TWO SHEETS WERE HELD UP IN THE LIGHT AND NOBODY COULD TELL WHICH WAS THE BOOK.** A man of about thirty-two who puts tables up and a boy of about nineteen each held one at arm's length for about eleven minutes. A boy said out loud that there are three more of those sheets in this district and he has found all three, and a clerk entered that the count of places where those three lines can be read is four. A man who mends fencing said out loud that the only way to tell a stranger is for the person who made it to stand beside it and say something about himself, and a clerk entered that no person has done that. A woman who keeps a scale said she has never been told which one was made here and that she is not saying she would not ask. |
| **543** | 43 | **THE MAN WHO DIGS LOAM SAID, UNASKED, THAT A DATE IS THE ONLY KIND OF FIGURE HE HAS EVER BEEN IN, AND HE HAS BEEN IN TWO, AND NOBODY HAS ASKED HIM ABOUT EITHER.** He set the blade of his spade into the ground at the edge of that yard and said it before anybody had said anything to him and went back down the bank inside a quarter of an hour. A man who mends fencing said out loud that nobody is going to ask that man and gave the reason, which is that whatever goes on that line cannot be a thing this yard went and got off a man standing in a ditch. A man who keeps a tally said he has never asked a man for a word and that the day he has to say it out loud it is not a tally any more. |
| **544** | 44 | **A CLERK WROTE THREE SENTENCES ABOUT A FOURTH LINE IN ONE MORNING AND REFUSED ALL THREE OUT LOUD IN FRONT OF ABOUT NINETEEN PEOPLE, TURNING HER PAGE OVER AFTER EACH ONE.** `The third line above is a person`, refused because a book that says a person and does not say who has told the stranger there is somebody to ask. `The third line above is the man who digs loam`, refused because there is nowhere in this district for a name to go. `Nobody`, refused because a page that says nobody about a man is the last thing this district is going to put in a book. **She entered that a fourth line is not a column and that a column is something a person looks down and a line is something a person reads, and put her pen down flat on the boards at about half past eleven and did not pick it up again.** A man who mends fencing said what is wanted is a sentence a person says about himself and not a rule. |
| **545** | 45 | **THE WOMAN WHO KEEPS A SCALE ASKED WHAT IT WOULD COST AND WAS TOLD, IN FRONT OF ABOUT NINETEEN PEOPLE, AND THE COST WAS THREE THINGS.** She carried a counter weight up that bank and set it on the far end of that second table about a foot from that sheet and left it there all day, and the cart had to swing wide of it at about three. A man who keeps a tally said the district would give up the one instrument it has that stops being true by itself. A man who mends fencing said a copy made after this is a copy of an admission and costs its copier nothing, and that after this the district can never prove the other sheet is a copy. **The page at that table said twelve all day and said thirteen on the day after, and the woman was not thanked.** |
| **546** | 46 | **A SHEET CAME OFF THE END OF THAT SECOND TABLE FOR THE FIRST TIME IN FORTY-SIX MORNINGS AND WENT BACK ON THE SAME TABLE ABOUT AN INCH ALONG.** The man of about twenty-nine who drives that cart lifted it with his right hand, held it flat against his own chest for about a minute, and gave a reason out loud, which is that the far end of that table is going. A clerk entered that it moved and entered that she is not entering where it went and entered that an inch is not a distance anybody can enter. A woman who keeps a scale said she had asked for that sheet eleven mornings ago and been refused and that nobody in that yard is going to tell her those are the same size of thing. A man who cuts reeds said he is not moving his own chalk mark to meet a sheet of paper. |
| **547** | 47 | **NINE WORDS WERE SAID OUT LOUD IN THAT YARD AND WERE NOT WRITTEN DOWN.** A woman who keeps a scale offered a form of them and about four people said in about four seconds each that it was not it, and a man who keeps a tally refused one for a reason. A man who mends fencing said it has to be a thing a person would say about himself, out loud, standing next to it, with his hands doing nothing. **Then the clerk said it, the boy counted nine, and she said she was not writing it that afternoon and was writing it in the morning in daylight with that book uncovered, in her own hand and not out of anybody's mouth, and that if it rains she will write it in the rain.** A man of about thirty-seven took a splinter out of a board about a foot from that book with his own knife to clear a place for a hand. |
| **548** | 48 | **THE FOURTH LINE WENT ON AND A SHEET OF THAT COPY WAS READ OUT LOUD IN THAT YARD THE SAME AFTERNOON, AND IT IS THE CLIMAX OF THE VOLUME.** At about ten a clerk took the stone off the corner of that lot book with both hands and wrote nine words in her own hand at nobody's dictation and read them out loud, and the boy counted nine. She entered that the count of things this district has made moves from twelve to thirteen and that the thirteen is smaller than the twelve, and entered that no column is ruled for it and why, and entered both of the other costs. A man who digs loam came up out of the ditch, put his own thumb in the hollow in that stone, pressed, took his thumb out and said nothing, and was not asked whether he agreed. **At about half past four the man of about twenty-nine who drives that cart held that sheet out at arm's length in the middle of that yard and read its three lines out loud in the ordinary voice and said out loud that the third line of it is not there. HE READ THE FIRST TWO LINES AND THEN SAID THE THIRD IS RULED AND EMPTY; THE REVIEW FOUND THE SPEECH READING A THIRD LINE AND THEN DENYING IT AND IT IS REPAIRED. The boy got ninety-three. AFTERWARDS HE SAID OUT LOUD THAT HE HAS SAID A THING ABOUT THAT SHEET AND NOT ABOUT HIMSELF, AND THAT IT IS STILL NOT THE BOOK, and about four people in that yard have said since that a man stood in front of both of those things and said a thing out loud about one of them and that that is not a person saying something about himself.** A clerk entered that she is not entering the name of whoever read it. |
| **549** | 49 | **A QUESTION WAS ASKED IN THAT YARD OUT LOUD AND WAS NOT ASKED OF ANYBODY IN PARTICULAR, AND ABOUT FOUR PEOPLE LOOKED AT THE END OF THAT SECOND TABLE AND NOBODY SAID ANYTHING.** The man of about thirty-two who puts tables up put the stone back on the corner of that book with both hands, unasked, and said he is not going to be thanked. A woman who keeps a scale said her shop is open, that she lost a morning of trade ten days ago, that she has not been thanked and is not asking. A man who drives that cart said a sheet read out loud once is still not the book and that nobody in that yard can tell which of the two is the one this district made. **This is the only one of the three chapters that states it, and it stands because the review repair removed the two answers that contradicted it.** **The man of about thirty-four who mends fencing said one thing about his own trade and it was not his name, and a clerk entered that nobody asked him a question and that she is not entering what he said as a name, and said out loud that she is leaving the question where it is.** About four people at that first table have said since that they do not know whether the man who digs loam agreed. |
| **550** | 50 | **THE FIFTIETH MORNING, AND THE FIXED FINAL IMAGE, AND THE NAME IS NOT SAID.** The first table with that lot book under a stone with no wear on it and four lines on it and the fourth of them nine words in a clerk's hand. The second table with the stone lying face up and a hollow in it one inch deep in the middle and nothing at either end, and the copy beside it with three lines and nothing in the third, on that table for the fiftieth morning and fifty days. A clerk entered the count of that board on her own page and did not look up. A man of about sixty-four at the foot of that low wall with his coat folded on the stones, given nothing, on his hundred and eighty-ninth night, having slept on a hundred and eighty-eight of them. A man who drives that cart said a sheet was read out loud once on the afternoon of the sixteenth and he is not going to read it again. A woman who keeps a scale said she has not been thanked and is not going to be. **The man of about thirty-four who mends fencing was at the end of that second table with his right hand flat on the boards and not on the stone and not on the copy, and a clerk entered that and entered that she is not entering it as anything, and his name is on no page. **HE ALSO SAID OUT LOUD AT ABOUT HALF PAST TEN THAT ABOUT NINE PEOPLE IN THAT YARD CAN NOW TELL YOU WHICH OF THE TWO HAS A LINE WITH NOTHING ON IT, AND THAT NOT ONE OF THEM CAN TELL YOU WHICH OF THE TWO THIS DISTRICT MADE, AND THAT IS THE FIRST THING THAT YARD HAS EVER SAID OUT LOUD ABOUT A PAGE AND IT IS NOT A WAY OF TELLING A STRANGER. The boy got one hundred and ten, the first count in this block above a hundred. Chapter 550 as first written said the opposite, that nine people can tell which is ours and gave the reason as a line count, and the review repair is what stopped Chapter 550 from undoing Chapter 549.** |

## 7. THE FIVE MOMENTS A VOLUME 12 WRITER NEEDS AND DOES NOT HAVE TO READ FIFTY CHAPTERS FOR

1. **DAY 1, CHAPTER 501.** A man of about twenty-nine who drives that cart puts a sheet of paper on the end of the second table at about ten past eight in front of about nineteen people and nobody asks him one word. Three lines, the first two the same as the first two of that lot book including the error, and nothing in the third.
2. **DAY 3, CHAPTER 503.** A clerk of nineteen years enters that there is a difference between two of the four figures a man of fifty-six reads off that wall, enters that she is not entering what the difference is, and enters that a figure may not be corrected. The man of fifty-six is told in that yard in front of about nineteen people and says nothing, and nobody asks him twice.
3. **DAY 24, CHAPTER 524.** A person asks out loud, in front of about nineteen people, who said a thing has to be in writing to be true. Nobody said it. **The whole of what six volumes built is a book made out of things people said out loud in a yard, and the forgery is a figure nobody said out loud, and the copy is not wrong and not true and not a book.**
4. **DAY 40, CHAPTER 540.** A clerk counts the length of the first month of the nineteenth year off a board with a finger on the wood, in the open, in daylight, with about nineteen people at the wall, and it is thirty-one, and a figure of thirty off a list is one out, and the difference is entered as a day and is not an error.
5. **DAYS 47 AND 48, CHAPTERS 547 AND 548.** Nine words said out loud in about eleven seconds to about nineteen people and not written, and then written at about ten the next morning in her own hand at nobody's dictation in the open, and then a sheet of the copy read out loud in that yard at about half past four by the man who put it there, who said the third line is ruled and it is empty, and afterwards said out loud that he has said a thing about that sheet and not about himself and that it is still not the book.

## 8. THE THREE THINGS THE LAST CHAPTER IS, ALL OF THEM ON THE PAGE

**Chapter 550 is the fixed final image and every element of it is there, and it is also a summary of the volume's argument and it is also the volume's own test applied to itself and failed, and those three are not in tension.**

The image: two tables, a book under a stone with four lines and the fourth of them nine words in a clerk's own hand, a stone face up with a hollow one inch deep in the middle and nothing at either end, a copy beside it with three lines and nothing in the third, on that table for fifty days, a clerk entering the count of that board without looking up, a man of about sixty-four at the foot of that low wall with his coat folded on the stones, given nothing, on his hundred and eighty-ninth night having slept on a hundred and eighty-eight of them, and the man of about thirty-four who mends fencing at the end of the second table with his right hand flat on the boards and not on the stone and not on the copy.

The argument: `The count of things this district has made is thirteen, and the thirteen is smaller than the twelve, and a clerk entered that on the sixteenth of this month and not before.`

The test applied to itself and failed: `That book has four lines on it and the other one has three and there is a copy lying next to that stone, and about nine people in this yard can now tell you which of the two of those has a line with nothing on it, and about four of them would not have been able to do that nine days ago, and not one of us can tell you which of the two this district made, and about four of them have said that is the first thing this yard has ever said out loud about a page and it is not a way of telling a stranger.` **The boy counted one hundred and ten, the first count in the last block above a hundred, and `tools/measure.py` returns `None` for it.**

## 9. THE FIFTEENTH OF THE SECOND MONTH, AND WHAT IT MEANS FOR THE LAST BLOCK

**CHAPTER 515, DAY 15, IS THE CHAPTER A LATER PHASE MUST READ BEFORE IT BELIEVES ANYTHING THE BLOCK RECORDS SAY ABOUT DAY 48.**

The boy of about nineteen read the two third lines out loud, standing between that first table and that second table at about eleven, one after the other, in the ordinary voice, in front of about nineteen people, and about nine people have said since that it is the first time that anybody in that yard has read the two third lines against each other out loud, on purpose, with both of them in his mouth at once. He said of the copy's third line: `And that one is nothing, and I have read it out loud, and it is still nothing, and it is going to be nothing this afternoon and it was nothing yesterday morning.` A clerk of nineteen years entered that a boy said a thing out loud in that yard and entered the thing, and entered that an empty line was read out loud in that yard in front of about nineteen people and that reading it out loud did not make it a figure, and entered that she is not going to enter that it did not make it a figure either. And the ledger of that chapter says, on the page: `A piece of paper with two lines on it and nothing in the third of it has been on the end of the second table in that yard for the fifteenth morning, and a boy read its empty third line out loud in front of about nineteen people this morning and it was still nothing when he had finished.`

**THE DAY-48 READING IS THE FIRST TIME THAT MAN READS THAT SHEET, THE FIRST TIME THAT SHEET IS READ AS A WHOLE DOCUMENT, AND THE FIRST TIME ANYBODY SAYS OUT LOUD THAT ITS THIRD LINE IS NOT THERE. IT IS NOT THE FIRST TIME THAT SHEET OR ITS EMPTY THIRD LINE HAS BEEN READ OUT LOUD IN THAT YARD. A CLERK SAID ON DAY 15, IN AN ENTRY, THAT READING AN EMPTY LINE OUT LOUD DID NOT MAKE IT A FIGURE, AND SAID SHE WAS NOT GOING TO ENTER THAT IT DID NOT MAKE IT A FIGURE EITHER. THAT IS THIRTY-THREE DAYS BEFORE THE CLIMAX AND IT IS STILL THE TRUEST SENTENCE IN THE VOLUME ABOUT WHAT AN EMPTY LINE DOES.**

## 11. THE FIVE MOMENTS A VOLUME 12 WRAPPER NEEDS AND DOES NOT HAVE TO READ FIFTEEN CHAPTERS FOR

1. **DAY 1, CHAPTER 551.** A clerk begins entering the number of sheets on the end of that second table every morning of that month and it is one, and says out loud that if it moves she is not writing why unless a person in the yard says why out loud. **A count that this district has never taken before, entered every morning, and it is not a ladder.**
2. **DAY 7, CHAPTER 557.** A second sheet is on that table. Four lines, the third ruled and empty, the fourth carrying `The line above is a person, not named here.` in a hand that is not this district's. A man who was at the table at a quarter to eight and at a quarter to nine gives the reason out loud, the man who made the copy says out loud unasked that it is not his hand, and the man the third line is about reads the fourth line and says nothing.
3. **DAY 8, CHAPTER 558.** The count of documents this district does not own moves from three to four at about a quarter to eight with the man who keeps a tally beside the clerk, who says the reason out loud first, and a clerk says out loud that she is not going to say what the next one will be. **No fifth was proposed.**
4. **DAY 13, CHAPTER 563.** A person asks out loud whether there is another day of the month. A clerk says out loud that she is not going to answer and gives the reason, and a man who keeps a tally has a blank page in his own book and is not writing on it. **The month ends on that morning and no figure for its length is on any page of this block.**
5. **DAY 15, CHAPTER 565.** A man says the reason out loud before he lifts a sheet, the count of sheets goes from three to two, and a woman of about thirty-six who keeps a scale takes it up that bank in her own two hands, puts it on her own counter with her shop open, and says again that she cannot tell which of them is this district's.

## 12. WHAT THIS BLOCK DID NOT DO, AND A WRITER MUST NOT INFER THAT IT DID

**It did not answer who made the sheets. It did not have anybody work out why a copy of an admission is cheaper than a copy of a figure, which is day 25 and Chapter 575 and is not this block's. It did not have the protagonist ask anybody a question, and nobody noticed that he had not. It did not fill the column for the name of whoever read a thing out loud, which was ruled and empty at about six on all fifteen days and was named more often in this block than in any fifteen mornings of Volume 11. It did not name a night, run the bid, propose closing it, pay the fifth of the five things this district does not have, advance the reading of that lot past the fourth of the five, correct a figure, add a person to this district, make a fourteenth thing, add a fifth place where those three lines can be read, or print a panel. It printed no morning-after-the-count ordinal at all, as its own first rule requires. It settled nothing about the protagonist's name and named no day for it and no person who would say it and prepared for it in nothing.**

## 13. THE SIX MOMENTS A VOLUME 12 WRAPPER NEEDS FROM THIS BLOCK

1. **DAY 17, CHAPTER 567.** A boy says out loud that a man came up that lane with a page with three lines on it and the third ruled and empty, asked whether it came out of this yard, was told it did not, said it looks the same as the one he has got at home, and went on down the lane without saying where he got it. A clerk and a man who keeps a tally each say out loud that a man's hands are not a place, and the count of places in this district stays at four.
2. **DAY 20, CHAPTER 570.** A woman asks a clerk to read out loud what she entered about her on the twenty-eighth of the month before. **The clerk refuses, out loud, in front of about nineteen people, and gives the reason: a thing that gets read out loud in that yard gets a name put against it, and she wrote it, and it does not turn into a different thing because somebody else says it with their mouth instead of hers.** A boy says out loud that he is not keeping a count of refusals, because there is no figure on any page in this district for how many times anybody has been asked anything.
3. **DAY 22, CHAPTER 572.** The man of about twenty-nine who drives that cart says a thing about himself for the first time in six volumes, in about nine seconds, in front of about nineteen people: he does not sleep in this parish, he has slept in nine places since the first morning of that year, and he has four of his own teeth. A clerk enters all of it and enters that it is not a name.
4. **DAY 25, CHAPTER 575. THE MIDPOINT REVERSAL.** At about eleven, in the open, in the ordinary voice, in front of about nineteen people, the boy asks `How much does it cost to copy a thing`. **Nobody answers it for about eleven seconds.** Then a man of about forty-eight who keeps a tally answers it: a figure has to be true before anybody can write it down a second time, and a man who copies a figure has had to stand in a room with somebody who knew the figure, and the third line on that page is the one line nobody can copy off anybody. The boy says the consequence: a copy of this district's own book would have a date in the third line, and none of these four has anything in the third, so somebody took the shape of that book, left out the one line about a person, and copied the nine words that say the line above is a person, **and that is not a forgery, that is a form.**
5. **DAY 27, CHAPTER 577. THE BREAK.** A woman gives her reason out loud before she asks, and then asks the road keeper whether anybody who does not live in this parish can get to that bank without a cart. He stops four feet short of the gate and answers in about nine seconds: that lane is the only way in, it is eleven miles, there is a gate at the ninth mile, and after that gate the road goes down to the water, and a man on foot who is not from this parish will be told there that the road ends. **He then says that nobody has ever asked him whether the road ends at the water, that it does not, and that there are four more miles of it past that gate.**
6. **DAY 30, CHAPTER 580.** A man who keeps a tally says out loud that this district has worked out in a fortnight that a page cannot carry a person, and that about four of them have been saying since two days ago that somebody is going to have to be asked, and that he has a man at that wall who has said four things out loud to him every morning since before the middle of the sixth month and whom he has never once asked. **A clerk enters that a man said a thing out loud about him and is entering it as a thing said and NOT as a question, and the reason is that nobody asked it out loud and a thing which was said and not asked does not move anything. The man of fifty-six says the four off that wall for the three hundred and fifth time and nobody in that yard asks him one thing.**

## 14. WHAT THIS BLOCK DID NOT DO, AND A WRITER MUST NOT INFER THAT IT DID

**It did not answer who made the sheets. It did not resolve whether this district will put a person on a page, and the cost of the reversal is on the page from day 26 and is unpaid. It did not have the protagonist ask anybody a question, and nobody noticed that he had not. It did not ask the man the third line is about anything, on any of the fifteen days, and he agreed to nothing. It did not fill the column for the name of whoever read a thing out loud, which was ruled and empty at about six on all fifteen days and was named on all fifteen. It did not name a night, run the bid, propose closing it, pay the fifth of the five things this district does not have, advance the reading of that lot past the fourth of the five, correct a figure, add a person to this district, make a fourteenth thing, add a fifth place INSIDE this district where those three lines can be read, or print a panel. It printed no morning-after-the-count ordinal at all, as its own first rule requires. It printed no figure about the length of the third month and none about the length of the fourth month, and no size for the error in the fourth of the four figures on that wall. It settled nothing about the protagonist's name and named no day for it and no person who would say it and prepared for it in nothing.**

# Volume 12, Batch 0003 — Chapters 581 to 590, Days 31 to 40, the eighteenth to the twenty-seventh of the fourth month

**TEN CHAPTERS ON TEN DAYS. `c = 31` IS CHAPTER 581 AND `c = 40` IS CHAPTER 590. NO DAY CARRIES TWO CHAPTERS AND NO CHAPTER CARRIES TWO DATES.**

## 581 — day 31, the eighteenth of the fourth month. THE YARD FINDS OUT WHAT ASKING WOULD COST IN PUBLIC

Three pages at the end of the second table; the copy on its seventy-ninth morning. A man of about thirty-seven who cuts reeds works out, out loud, in about nineteen people's hearing, that the cost is not the asking but the saying of it, because from the morning this yard does it the not-asking becomes a thing about nine of them chose. A woman who keeps a scale says that a person at her counter said the reason out loud on her behalf this morning, which is that a woman who cannot tell should not be holding them at all. A man who keeps a tally has his flat book open for the second morning and says there is one page in it he would not want anybody to read. The man who drives the cart says he would rather be asked than be ready. The man the third line is about comes up at about half past ten and says nothing and is asked nothing. The man who mends fencing puts a rail he has not gone to down against the side of the table, stands about nine feet off, and says that about four of them have worked out he comes up here instead of going to the rail and they are not wrong. Nothing was read out loud; the column was ruled and empty.

## 582 — day 32, the nineteenth of the fourth month. THE YARD WRITES THE ASKING ON A BOARD AND IT IS NO USE

A man of about thirty-two who puts tables up stands a board against the side of the second table before eight and writes a notice on it. A boy reads it twice with his thumb against his teeth and says it is a notice and not a question, and that about four of them have read it as a thing the yard has already decided. The man who keeps a tally says he is the one who knows the name and is not going to write it, and gives the reason out loud: a name on a board is this district publishing a man on the strength of a board nobody agreed to. A woman who takes in washing reads it standing up and says a board with a question on it and nobody in it is a thing people walk past. The man the third line is about comes up at about one, walks to the end of the table the board is standing against, reads it from that end because that is the only end it can be read from standing up, and stands at the far end of those boards with his hands behind his back; nobody asks him one word and a clerk enters that she is not entering what he made of the board. The man who mends fencing says the man at that end of the table cannot read it from where he is standing and that he noticed that before anybody said a word about it. A clerk says out loud that a notice is not a line and a line is not a notice and that she is not entering that board as either. The board comes down at about four. The page count did not move.

## 583 — day 33, the twentieth of the fourth month. A MAN SAYS EXACTLY WHAT WOULD HAVE TO GO ON A PAGE AND ASKS NOBODY

A man who keeps a tally holds his own book down against the wind with his own hand and says out loud that this district would have to put three things on a page: a third line that is a date, a fourth line saying the line above is a person, and an instruction to a stranger holding that sheet telling him who to ask and how he might get to him — and that the instruction is not the answer, it is the road to asking. He does not turn round while he says it. Nobody in that yard looked at the man the third line is about while it was said, and nobody asked him anything. A woman who keeps a scale says she has been giving about four people nothing for a month. The man who mends fencing says he is not going to be the reason that man hears about it from a clerk. A clerk says out loud that she is not writing any of it and that a road to a man is not a remedy. NO FIFTH LINE WAS WRITTEN.

## 584 — day 34, the twenty-first of the fourth month. A PAGE COMES UP THE LANE FROM ELEVEN MILES OFF AND THE COUNT OF FOUR DOES NOT MOVE

A man of about forty who is not from this parish comes up that lane before eight with a page in the inside of his coat, says the reason out loud before his hand goes on it — it has been on his mother's kitchen table since before the frost, he cannot tell where it came from, he carried it eleven miles rather than have it in that house not knowing, and he is not staying and he is not asking anybody anything — puts it down, squares it with the side of his hand without touching the other three, and goes back down the bank. The count on the second table is four and was three. A clerk opens her book on the count of documents this district does not own and does not add anything to it, and gives the reason out loud: a fifth kind of document would be a thing this district could go and get if somebody asked for it, and nobody here asked him for it and nobody here could have got it, and a man standing in a yard is not a fifth of anything. A woman who keeps a scale says out loud that she has been the reason that count is four and not the page on the table, and that she has known it for a fortnight and has not said it until now because nobody asked her and she is not going to start.

## 585 — day 35, the twenty-second of the fourth month. SOMEBODY NAMES THE FIFTH OF THE FIVE AND NOBODY CAN SAY WHICH MAN IT MEANS

The man who drives the cart leans on the end of the second table with his back to about nineteen people and says that the fifth of the five is a way to pay a person who is not in a household, that it gets said in that yard every morning, and that this morning he has worked out that nobody in that yard can tell him which man it means — and that about four of them have been assuming it means the man at the end of the table, and that he digs loam and goes home to a house, and that it might not be him at all. Nobody answered him. A clerk says she is not going to say which man he means either, because a thing that does not have a person in it is the only kind that stays true. The man who mends fencing came down off the top of that bank for the first time in a month and says he has still not said anything and would like it written down that the ground made no difference. A man who puts tables up says nobody in that yard has a wage or a price or anything anybody else would take. A woman who keeps a scale says she has a way of paying about nine people and it does not pay a man who digs loam, and did not pay her for the first two years she had that shop. The reading of that lot did not move.

## 586 — day 36, the twenty-third of the fourth month. SOMEBODY SAYS OUT LOUD THAT A MAN HAS HAD A CHOICE ABOUT IT AND HAS TAKEN IT

The yard thins out at about one. A woman who keeps a scale comes up the bank from her own shop with the door open behind her and says out loud, in front of about nineteen people, that she has been standing in that doorway since the twelfth of the month, that the man who mends fencing had a choice about it on the twelfth and took it, and that he has had it every morning since and has taken it every morning, and that she is the one saying it out loud because she is the only one of them who has to stand behind a counter and look at about nine people a day who want somebody to blame — and that she knows what it is to be looked at when you cannot tell. **The man who mends fencing does not look at her and does not look at the man at the far end of the table, and he says out loud that she is not to thank him and that he is not going to stand there and be a man who has been patient, and that he has a question in his mouth every morning and a rail he has not gone to and a man nine feet away, and that he is not going to say the reason because the reason is his and it is not a good one and he would like the yard to go on not asking him for it.** A clerk enters that he said he should not be thanked and enters the reason. A man who keeps a tally says he has never opened his book in front of about nineteen people and that he has four things in it he could not read out in that yard if he were paid.

## 587 — day 37, the twenty-fourth of the fourth month. SIX PAGES ON ONE BOARD AND A COMPARISON THAT IS ENTERED

A woman who keeps a scale comes down that bank before eight with two pages under one arm and says the reason out loud first: about nine of them have been talking for a week about comparing them, this is the only way to compare two of them, her shop is not going to be a shop nobody can get into after this morning, and she will take them up at about one. All six are squared up against a straight edge and nobody in that yard touches one of them. A man who puts tables up holds the straight edge and then takes his hands away and says he has set tables for about twenty years and has never put his hand on a thing to square it. Nobody reads a word out of any of the six; about nine people describe what is on each of them while a clerk writes it in a column. A boy says out loud: six, four lines on every one of them, nothing in the third of any of them, the same nine words on the fourth of every one of them and not one word different, and four different hands in the fourth line, which are not four people. A man who keeps a tally says that is the finding and the being alike is not the finding, because there is nobody here to stand beside them. The man the third line is about comes up at about eleven, stands at the far end of that table with the six pages on it and looks down the two long sides of it; nobody asks him one word and a clerk enters that she is not entering what he made of six pages on one board. A clerk says a comparison is not a test. The two pages go back up the bank at about one with a person and a reason, and at about four the man who is not from this parish carries his own page back down the lane with his reason said out loud first, and the count is three.

## 588 — day 38, the twenty-fifth of the fourth month. A DECISION IS ENTERED THAT IS NOT THE DECISION

Frost in the cracks of the boards. About nine people stand along the two long sides of the second table without being told to. A man who puts tables up lays a straight edge across the boards a yard from the near end and says that about four of them have been standing at that table in a heap all month like a queue at a door, and that a room is set in a line so that everybody in it can see the person at the end of it. A man who keeps a tally says he wants it said out loud by him and not by anybody else, because he is the one who has been getting ready since the twelfth, and that he is not able to ask that man anything and would rather be told by about nineteen people at once in the open than find it out quietly on a day when it matters. A clerk enters at about half past eight that a yard in that parish has decided to find out what the asking would sound like, entered as a decision about a rehearsal and not as a decision about a man, the reason being that nobody asked the man. About four people read the entry over her shoulder and about four of them have said since that there was no line in it saying it could not be done the other way, and nobody in that yard said anything about that, and she wrote it in the ordinary way and did not look up while she wrote it. A woman who takes in washing says they are all standing in a line like people at a counter waiting to be served, and that the thing they are waiting for is a thing nobody has got behind the counter. The man who mends fencing says he would like a day where he does not have an opinion.

## 589 — day 39, the twenty-sixth of the fourth month. THE ASKING IS REHEARSED OUT LOUD WITH THE MAN DOWN THE BANK

The man the third line is about is not in that yard at any point while the asking is being said out loud in it, and he is not in it when the last of the nine has been said, and nobody in that yard says why. A boy says he is going to say it exactly as somebody would say it to a man and has practised it walking up the bank, and that about four of them are going to answer as though it were real. A man who keeps a tally answers: he is going to ask why that line was left off, he has four of them in front of him and cannot get a straight answer out of a page, he is not going to ask twice, and if the man does not want to answer he can say so and that will be entered. A man who cuts reeds gets through the first half of it and stops about four feet short and says he has said it to three men and got about nine feet further each time and stopped further short every time, and that he does not think it is practice and is not going to say what he thinks it is. **The boy asks the man who mends fencing, and he says out loud why he has not asked: that if he asks and gets a no, then he is a man who asked a man and got a no, and he will not be the man who mends a fence any more, he will be the man who asked.** Nobody in that yard says anything for a while. A woman who keeps a scale says the reason is that not one of them has anything to lose by the answer, and that a rehearsal of a thing nobody has to live with is a rehearsal. A clerk says a question was said out loud nine times and nobody read a thing out of anything, and that this column is for the second of those and not the first, and that she is not entering anybody into it, and the reason is that a person who practises a question is not a reader. The rehearsal is entered. **At about half past one, after the last of the nine, the man the third line is about comes up that bank, looks at about nine people standing along that second table and says nothing at all to anybody, and nobody in that yard asks him one word, and a clerk enters that a man was in that yard and enters that nobody in that yard said one word to him.** No person has been appointed to the column.

## 590 — day 40, the twenty-seventh of the fourth month, AND THE COUNT OF THE THIRD MONTH

**About nineteen people are at that wall by about a quarter to eight, which has never happened on any morning of that yard.** A clerk of nineteen years puts the board up on the low wall with its face to the light, puts her left forefinger on the first mark of the run, and says out loud that she is counting the marks of the third month on that board in front of them because a month that has ended can be counted, and that she has never counted a month off a board in daylight before this morning and is not going to pretend she is not pleased about it. She runs her finger along the board in the open, about nine people count with her out loud, and she stops on the last mark and says: that is thirty-one. A clerk enters that the length of the third month of this year is thirty-one, that it was counted off a board with a finger on the wood, and that a month length has now been counted three times in this district, the other two being the twelfth month at thirty and the first month at thirty-one. **THE DAY MAP OF THE SECOND HALF OF THIS VOLUME IS RIGHT.**

**At about half past nine the man who keeps a tally stops in front of that wall and says out loud that the fourth of those four is out; that he has known it was out for two volumes and has not been able to say how far; that he can say how far this morning because she has just counted a month off that board; that the figure counts days from the second of January and has never stopped and went over the turn into this year without stopping; and that the year it went over was three hundred and sixty-four days long, which is a sum of twelve months, three of which have been counted by somebody and nine of which are off a list at the back of his book, the twelfth at thirty and the first at thirty-one and the third at thirty-one as of a quarter to eight this morning.** A clerk enters the thing, the person and the figure.

**The man who mends fencing crosses the yard and stands at the end of that wall and says out loud that he has mended fence for nineteen years and has never once said a thing out loud in front of about nineteen people about a number on a wall, and that he would like the yard to notice that he did it and not to make anything of it, because that man has said four of those numbers every morning for three hundred and fifteen mornings and it has cost him whatever it costs him every one of them.** A clerk says out loud that she is entering that a man said the fourth of those four figures is out by a whole year and that she is not entering what it ought to say, and gives the reason: a figure on that wall may not be corrected and it is not hers to correct, and a wall that has been wrong for a year is still standing, and it keeps standing until somebody with more standing than a clerk takes it down, and there is nobody in this parish with that.

**A clerk of nineteen years then goes across the yard to that wall and says out loud, in front of about nineteen people, that the fourth of those four figures is out by a whole year, and the man of about fifty-six is standing at that wall and hears every word of it and says nothing at all and does not move and does not look at the wall, and nobody in that yard asks him one thing about it then or afterwards.** A woman who keeps a scale says she has been asked four times in a week which of two sheets is this district's and has said four times that she cannot tell, and that neither a month counted nor a wall counted helps her at all, and that she is glad about both, and that she has not shut her shop. **NO FIGURE ON THAT WALL WAS CORRECTED AND THE FIGURE ON THE WALL STANDS AT FOUR HUNDRED AND SEVENTY-NINE.**

## 14. WHAT THIS BLOCK DID NOT DO, AND A WRITER MUST NOT INFER THAT IT DID

**It did not ask the man the third line is about anything, on any of the ten days, and he agreed to nothing and was not named on any page. It did not resolve whether this district will put a person on a page, and the cost of the reversal is unpaid. It did not write a fifth line, and it did not advance the reading of that lot past the fourth of the five on any of the ten days, and a clerk said out loud twice that a road to a man is not a remedy and that a rehearsal is not a reading. It did not fill the column for the name of whoever read a thing out loud, which was ruled and empty at about six on all ten days and was named on all ten, and it did not appoint a person to it on the one morning when about nine people said a question out loud in that yard. It did not name a night, run the bid, propose closing it, pay the fifth of the five things this district does not have, correct a figure, add a person to this district, make a fourteenth thing, add a place inside this district where those three lines can be read, or print a panel. It printed no figure about the length of the third month on any of the nine mornings before the last, and none about the length of the fourth month on any morning of the block. It printed the size of the error in the fourth of the four figures on that wall on one morning, once, in a scene, in the open, with a person and a reason in the room, which is what outline/volume-12.md section 5.1 step 5 authorises for this stretch, and it corrected nothing. It settled nothing about the protagonist's name, named no day for it, named no person who would say it, prepared for it in nothing, and let no character prepare for it. It paid none of the sentence the volume owes.**

---

# VOLUME 12 BATCH 0004 — CHAPTERS 591 TO 600, DAYS 41 TO 50, APPENDED

> **APPENDED BY THE VOLUME 12 BATCH 0004 PHASE, NOT REWRITTEN ABOVE. ONE LINE A DAY. WHERE A CHAPTER AND A DOCUMENT DISAGREE THE CHAPTER IS CANON.**

- **591, day 41, the twenty-eighth of the fourth month.** The yard counts how many mornings are left in its month of days, ten counting that morning, and nobody has decided anything for sixteen mornings. A man of about thirty-four who mends fencing says he has been counting something since the sixteenth of the month and that it is a number of days that goes down one every morning, and does not say the figure. A clerk says out loud that the fourth of the four figures on that wall is out and that she said so on the twenty-seventh and is not going to say it a second time. Three pages on the end of that second table, unchanged, and a copy on that wood for eighty-nine mornings.
- **592, day 42, the twenty-ninth of the fourth month.** A woman of about thirty-six who keeps a scale says out loud, in front of about nineteen people, that the man the third line is about is the only person in that yard nobody has ever promised anything to, and that about nine people had been wanting him to say something for a month and had not gone and asked him. A man of about forty-eight who keeps a tally says that he has said out loud twice that he cannot ask that man a question and that wanting nineteen people to know at once is not the same as not being able to. The man who digs loam puts his own hand flat on the boards at about half past ten and is asked nothing.
- **593, day 43, the thirtieth of the fourth month, and the month ends.** A clerk writes the date and enters that this is the last morning of a month of days this district has not counted off a board, and that she is not going to count it, and gives the reason, which is that a month a person is living in is not a month that has ended. A man who cuts reeds says his chalk marks on the underside of those boards are not for anything. The count of the marks in chalk along the edge of that second table moves once, from a hundred and thirty-five to a hundred and thirty-six, with the person who put it there and the reason he gave out loud. Three pages, unchanged.
- **594, day 44, the first of the fifth month.** A clerk writes a date at the top of her own page before four people are in that yard and about nine of them come up that bank and none of them says one word about it, and about four of them had come up meaning to mention it and had not managed it by about ten. A woman of about thirty-three who takes in washing at the second channel says that the women in eleven houses did not mention it either. A man of about fifty-six has now read those four figures off that wall on the first morning of four different months without once being told which month it was. Three pages, unchanged.
- **595, day 45, the second of the fifth month.** A man of about thirty-four who mends fencing comes up that bank before about four other men, crosses the yard, sets a rail down and goes to the far end of that second table, and at about ten asks a man of about thirty-one who digs loam, in the plainest words anybody in that yard could find, whether a man who is not from this parish may come up that bank and ask him about a page in his hand. The yard is quiet for about a minute and a half. The man says ask me that again this afternoon and that he is not going to say it on a morning when nobody has told him what he is agreeing to. About four people say he did it, he went and asked. He says out loud that he is not to be thanked and asks for it to be entered. Three pages, unchanged.
- **596, day 46, the third of the fifth month.** A man of about thirty-one who digs loam comes up that bank about two hours earlier than he has come in two months and stands at the far end of that second table for about an hour and a half and about nine people in that yard have that hour and a half to say something and do not use it. A woman of about thirty-six who keeps a scale says out loud, before anybody asks him to pay it, that every copy of that book made from this morning carries the district's own words about where he is, and that the number of copies is not a figure this district can hold, and that she is saying it before he agrees and not after. A clerk turns her own book round so about nine people can see what she writes and the man it is about cannot. At about ten past one he says yes. A clerk writes it down without looking up. He says out loud that he is not to be thanked. Three pages, unchanged.
- **597, day 47, the fourth of the fifth month.** A clerk of nineteen years comes up that bank at about half past six and squares her own book with the flat of her hand before anybody is in that yard. A man of about thirty-four who mends fencing stands nine feet off a man and does not put his hand on the boards. The clerk says out loud, before she writes, what she is not going to write and gives the reason. She writes a way of being found on a page in the open in front of about nineteen people, and a boy reads it out loud as she writes it: that he digs loam in the low ground at the second channel from before eight until the water is over him, and a man is to ask any house on that channel and is not to ask at this yard. She enters that she is not entering his name. He says he did not ask for it and is not taking it back. Three pages, unchanged.
- **598, day 48, the fifth of the fifth month.** A woman of about thirty-six who keeps a scale has her table out on the pavement before about seven, which she does not do before the middle of the year, and a man with a page in his coat comes up that bank at about half past seven, takes it out, puts it back in his coat and does not put it down. A man of about thirty-four who mends fencing asks her, in front of about nineteen people and with the reason said out loud first, to hold one of the pages on her own counter with her own door open. She says again that she cannot tell which of them is this district's, and says her shop will not be shut to do it and she will not be thanked. He carries a page up that bank in his own two hands, says the reason out loud in a shop in front of about nine people, puts it on her counter, and the number of pages on the end of that second table is two. A man who digs loam looks up that bank at that shop for about two seconds and is asked nothing.
- **599, day 49, the sixth of the fifth month.** A clerk comes up that bank before about eight other people and the copy is lifted off the boards by about four people and put back by about four others. A man of about thirty-one who digs loam stands at the near end of that first table, which is the first time in about two months he has stood at the near end of anything. He says out loud that the fifth line is at his dictation and not at anybody's. A clerk reads it back to herself out loud and enters that he said it before she had the pen down. She writes the fifth line in her own hand, in that yard, in the open, in front of about nineteen people, at his dictation, and a boy reads it out loud as she writes it and again after. It says that the third line of a page with these five lines on it is a day a man was in a ditch, and that a stranger with a page who wants to know whether it came out of this yard is to go down that lane and through the gate and past the gate to the water and along the water to where a man is digging, and ask that man and not anybody else. The fourth line is not touched. A clerk says out loud that a line is not a column and a column is something a person looks down and a line is something a person reads, and that no column is being ruled. A man of about thirty-four who mends fencing says out loud that it is the only sentence this district has ever written that tells a person what to do with a piece of paper and that he is not going to be thanked for it. Two pages on the end of that second table, unchanged.
- **600, day 50, the seventh of the fifth month, the hundred and twenty-seventh morning after the count.** THE FIXED FINAL IMAGE. The yard of Lot Seventeen. The first table with that lot book under a stone with no wear on it, and the book has five lines on it, the fifth in a clerk's hand at the dictation of the man the third line is about, and the fourth untouched. The second table with its stone lying face up, the hollow in the middle of it about an inch deep and nothing at either end, and two pages along the end of it, which is not one more than the morning before. A clerk enters the count of the board on her own page without looking up. A man of about sixty-four is at the foot of that low wall with his coat folded on the stones and still given nothing, on his hundred and thirty-ninth night of that run, having slept on a hundred and thirty-eight of them. A man of about thirty-four who mends fencing is at the end of that second table with his right hand flat on the boards and not on the stone and not on the two pages. A boy said the morning before that the count of that yard this morning is the biggest number he has counted in two volumes and that he is not going to say what it is a number of until somebody else says it. The column for the name of whoever read a thing out loud is named and empty.

# CHAPTER SUMMARIES — VOLUME 13, CHAPTERS 616 TO 630, BLOCK 0002

> **APPENDED BY THE VOLUME 13 BATCH 0002 PHASE. NOTHING ABOVE THIS BLOCK WAS REWRITTEN. ONE LINE A CHAPTER. THE FULL BLOCK RECORD IS `state/volume-13-batch-0002-summary.md`.**

| chapter | day | date | what the chapter is |
|---|---|---|---|
| 616 | 16 | the twenty-third of the fifth month | **THE MAN WITH THE PAGE COMES UP THAT LANE AGAIN AND SAYS OUT LOUD WHY HE DID NOT PUT IT IN THE WATER.** He got up at five, went down the lane, stopped at the fourth post and stood there about a quarter of an hour, and did not go on to the water, because a page that says go and ask that man does not say where the water is and the water is past the man. He says he has come back up that bank twice since and told nobody. **A clerk says out loud that her figure for that lane has not moved on any of the three mornings he has been in that yard and that she is not going to change what the figure is about to make it fit what she can see.** The man who mends fencing says he put that line on that book and did not know there was a fourth post on that lane. **The man who digs loam says he had his back to the lane and never knew the man was there and is not going to turn round next time.** The count of people who have come down that lane is one and did not move on its sixteenth morning. |
| 617 | 17 | the twenty-fourth of the fifth month | **THE YARD WORKS OUT THAT A PAGE IN A HAND IS THE ONLY THING MAKING A STRANGER, AND A MAN ASKS THE MAN WHO DIGS LOAM ABOUT A QUESTION AND HE DOES NOT ANSWER IT.** A man of about thirty-two who puts tables up has been standing at the foot of the bank looking at hands since half past seven and has seen one sheet of paper in about forty pairs. The boy says he has counted what came UP that lane for a month and never what went DOWN it, and that about four of you have worked out that I have had a figure in my own head for seventeen mornings and it has been a figure of the wrong thing. **A clerk says out loud that her figure is not a figure of that lane, it is a figure of the hands.** At about half past eleven the man who mends fencing asks the man who digs loam whether a man came down that lane yesterday with a page in his hand and asked him whether it came out of that yard, **and the man looks at the boards about four feet in front of his own boots, takes his hand off the rail, wipes it down his own leg and says nothing at all for about fifteen seconds, and nobody in that yard asks him why.** |
| 618 | 18 | the twenty-fifth of the fifth month | **THE COUNT RISES FROM ONE TO TWO AND A CLERK ENTERED A REASON BEFORE SHE ENTERED THE FIGURE.** At about a quarter to nine a clerk says out loud what she will write down if a person comes down that lane and what she will not, and says she has stopped writing what they ask. At about ten a woman of about forty-three comes down that lane with a page folded in four, stops about nine feet off the near end of the second table, and says she has walked two days, has not opened it since the fourth of this month, will not say what is in it, and will not say what she is going to ask the man. **A clerk entered that she came down that lane and that she had a page in their hand and the hour, and that she is not entering her name and not entering what she asks, and the reason was in the room an hour before the figure moved.** The man who digs loam says there is a woman standing about six feet off him he has never seen and that the fifth line says a man and does not say how many, and about four of you have noticed that I have just said how many and that I did not have to think about it. |
| 619 | 19 | the twenty-sixth of the fifth month | **THE MAN OF ABOUT THIRTY-FOUR WHO MENDS FENCING SAYS OUT LOUD WHAT HIS OWN QUESTION DID.** He asked a man on the second of this month whether a stranger with a page in his hand might come down that lane and ask him whether it came out of that yard, he asked it in front of about nineteen people, the man said he did not ask to be written down, and **he wrote it down anyway, and two people have come down that lane since with a page in their hand and not one of them has asked him that.** Nobody in that yard says anything for about a minute and a half. **The man who digs loam says that is not why he said it and that he will not help anybody turn the fifth of the five things this district does not have into an argument about owing him something, because a man can be owed a thing and still be standing in a yard on his own account.** A woman of about forty-three says she has been in the parish since the twenty-fifth, has not opened the page, has not gone down the lane, and has not worked out whether that is care or fright. |
| 620 | 20 | the twenty-seventh of the fifth month | **THE WOMAN WHO KEEPS A SCALE IS ASKED A SECOND TIME AND SAYS THE SAME THING AND IT IS THE SIXTH TIME SHE HAS SAID IT, AND SHE IS STILL RIGHT.** The man who mends fencing goes up the bank before eight, into a shop with the door propped open, and says he is the second person to ask and is not going to say who asked him. **She puts her hand flat on the beam of her own scale to stop it moving and says that she has never in her life been able to tell whether a page came out of a yard, that that is what she said the five times before this one, and that a woman who says the same thing six times is not repeating herself, she is finding out whether it was true the first time, and that it was.** Four people at her door and none of them came in. Back in the yard he says a person who answers you early is telling you something about the question and not about the answer. **A clerk says she enters everything the man says down to the foot of the rail he leans the rail on, and that the one thing she has not entered this month is the only thing anybody has gone up that bank to find out.** The boy counted a room of two people and it took longer than counting the yard. |
| 621 | 21 | the twenty-eighth of the fifth month | **A CLERK SAYS ONE THING OUT LOUD ABOUT THE COLUMN FOR THE NAME OF WHOEVER READ A THING OUT LOUD THAT SHE HAS NOT SAID IN THIS VOLUME.** She says that column is a thing a person looks down, that she has no standing over a man who is not from this parish, and that she has never once said out loud why she keeps it ruled and empty on the mornings when it would be easy to fill, and the reason is that she has been keeping it empty for thirteen volumes on purpose and the purpose is older than the standing and she does not have words for the purpose. **A man who puts tables up, from under that table, says that is not a reason, it is a habit, and that nobody in that yard has ever told those two things apart.** The man who mends fencing says a morning is coming when a name will go in that column and says he will not say a word to her about it. **And a clerk enters that she had thought about entering a page in the column for documents this district does not own, and had not, and enters the reason, which is that nobody in that yard has asked her to hold it.** |
| 622 | 22 | the twenty-ninth of the fifth month | **THE COUNT RISES TO THREE AND A BOY OF ABOUT NINETEEN IS FOUND OUT FOR HAVING GONE DOWN THAT LANE AT SIX IN THE MORNING.** A man who cuts reeds says he watched him go from the gate at the ninth mile and came back at about half past seven and has not said a word for nine hours. **The boy says that on the fifteenth of this month he said he was going to stop and that he was not going to say why, and that he is going to say why now: a man came down that lane on the twentieth with a page in his hand and did not go to the end of it, and I have counted a yard for a month and I have never once walked a lane to see whether it goes anywhere.** A clerk says she has left the hour he went out on purpose this time and is not going to say why. At about half past ten a young man of about twenty-two comes down that lane and says out loud the hour he means to go down it, so that nobody has to stand and watch him go. **The man who digs loam tells the yard there is no fourth post between that lane and his own ditch, and that if the young man asks him tomorrow he is going to tell him the same thing he told the other one.** |
| 623 | 23 | the thirtieth of the fifth month | **A MAN OF ABOUT SIXTY-FOUR IS AT THE FOOT OF THAT LOW WALL ON THE SECOND TO LAST MORNING OF THAT MONTH OF DAYS, AND A CLERK SAYS THAT EVERYTHING IN THAT YARD THIS MONTH HAPPENED TO SOMEBODY WHO CAME ON PURPOSE.** A man who puts tables up says he has been coming down that bank every morning since the sixth of this month and nobody has asked him why, and that nobody has ever put it in a book. **The clerk enters every figure and every event of the block so far and says the only thing about them that can be entered as a fact about the yard, which is that every one of those things happened to somebody who came here on purpose.** The man who mends fencing says he is the only person in that yard who is here by accident and that it is his own fault. **The man who digs loam says he has been here every morning of his life and neither of them has said one word to the other about it in thirteen volumes, and the word is that I would rather be here by accident than be found.** The count of strangers is three and did not move on its twenty-third morning. |
| 624 | 24 | **the thirty-first of the fifth month, and the month ends** | **THE LAST MORNING OF A MONTH NOBODY IN THAT YARD HAS COUNTED, AND THE LEDGER SAYS SO.** A man of about thirty-two who puts tables up walks the stone a foot along the second table because the boards under it are hollow and the boards under the chalk are sound, and stops when it comes to rest against the chalk, and looks at the chalk for about a minute without touching it. **A clerk says out loud that a month that has ended can be counted off a board in daylight in front of anybody, that she counted one in that yard on the twelfth of this month and it took her about a minute, and that this one she is not going to count, and enters that she has decided not to, and the reason is that a month a person is standing in is not a month, it is a month.** The man who digs loam says that stone has been on that table longer than he has been digging and that he does not mind it being moved and would have said so on any other morning and is saying it on this one. **Nobody in that yard has counted the fifth month and a clerk has entered that she is not going to and no figure about how long that month is is on any page.** |
| **625** | **25** | **the first of the sixth month** | **THE MIDPOINT REVERSAL. A man who cuts reeds asks, in the open, in the ordinary voice, in front of about nineteen people, at about eleven, what nobody in that yard has asked in thirteen volumes, and nobody answers it for eleven seconds, and then a man answers it out loud.** The man who cuts reeds stood at the gate at the ninth mile every morning of the month and is in this yard instead this morning and has not been asked a question about it. A clerk says a month that has ended is the only kind of month that is the same to everybody in it. The man who mends fencing says he put one of the two silent men in that yard there. The man who digs loam says he did not ask to be on a page and is not taking it back. And then nobody says anything at all for about two minutes. **Then: "What is a man who comes down that lane for?" The boy counts it and reads the number back to himself in a low voice. Nobody answers it. The boy counts eleven seconds on his own fingers. The man of fifty-six reads four figures off that wall without turning round. And then the man who digs loam answers: a page told him where the man is and not one word of that page told him what he was to ask, he has never in his life had a page in his hand, about four of you have been standing in that yard a month waiting for me to be the answer to a question, and there is no question and there has never been one, and the three of you who came down that lane are carrying three things this yard did not write and I am not the answer to any of them, and I am still standing here at the end of it.** The boy says he counted eleven seconds because there was nothing else to count, and that this morning the thing was the not answering. |
| 626 | 26 | the second of the sixth month | **AFTER THE REVERSAL THE YARD'S QUESTION IS WHAT THE FIFTH LINE LETS A STRANGER ASK, A PERSON PUTS A PAGE FACE DOWN ON THE SECOND TABLE AND TAKES IT AWAY, AND TWO MEN SAY WHAT AN INSTRUCTION IS OVER A TABLE WHILE A SCRAPER GOES ALONG THE BOARDS.** A person of about twenty-nine puts a page down face down on those boards at about a quarter to nine, about nine people look at it, nobody says a word, and about a minute later they pick it up and go up the bank; **a clerk enters both moves and the reason, and the count of pages on the end of that second table is two at a quarter to eight and two at about six.** The man who mends fencing says the thing the fifth line does not say is now what the yard is talking about and that he would rather be asked than not. **And a man of about thirty-two who puts tables up, scraping, says that a page this yard wrote is the only thing in this parish that can send a man who has never set foot in it up a bank he has never climbed, and that it does that by not saying what it is for, and that every way of saying what it is for would have cost them the sending, and a man who keeps a tally answers him without stopping work that nothing he has written down has sent anybody anywhere either, and that on the sixth of this month somebody wrote four lines on a page in a clerk's hand and a stranger walked two days, and that a clerk entered all three of those and entered neither of these two.** |
| 627 | 27 | the third of the sixth month | **THE COUNT RISES TO FOUR, AND THE ELEVEN-MILES RELAY IS BROKEN ONCE, IN A SCENE, WITH THE REASON ON THE PAGE, AND THE ROAD KEEPER ANSWERS.** A man of about fifty comes down that lane at about ten and says he has come down it four mornings this month and turned round at the gate three times, and that on the second morning there was a boy of about nineteen at the top of that lane at about six and he could see him from about nine hundred yards off and did not go down. **A clerk says she has written on that page every morning for nineteen years that a man came up that lane and she did not put a question to him, that this morning somebody did, and that she is not going to strike out what she wrote yesterday.** At about half past eleven the road keeper comes up that lane instead of at half past one and the man who mends fencing asks him one question: **How far is the eleven miles. The road keeper takes his cap off with both hands and answers: it is eleven miles and it has been eleven miles all his life, and about a mile and a half of it has gone since the frost, and he has told that council twice and been told twice, and that is my answer and I have not got another one, and I am not asking you for anything, I am telling you what I came up this lane to say.** The man who digs loam says he owes the road keeper an apology and not anybody else in that yard. |
| 628 | 28 | the fourth of the sixth month | **THE MAN WHO DIGS LOAM ASKS THE MAN WHO MENDS FENCING WHAT HE THINKS AND THE MAN SAYS A THING OUT LOUD ABOUT HIMSELF.** A clerk says out loud that there is a person in that yard everybody would ask and that she has noticed it herself before anybody said it, and that is her. A man who puts tables up says he has been putting tables up since he was thirteen and nothing he has ever written down has sent anybody anywhere, and that he was in that yard every morning of the month and was not sent. The man who digs loam says every man who has come down that lane this month has been sent by four lines on a page, and that he is going to stand there and not say anything else until he has thought about that. The boy says he did not count the lane this morning and that a man of about fifty could see him from nine hundred yards off. **And then at about twelve the man who digs loam walks the length of the table and asks him one question: What do you think. And the man says he thinks he should not be asked and is going to answer anyway, that the man who should be asked is standing at the top of that lane, and that he has known since the second of this month what he would say if a person in that yard asked him that, and that this is the third time he has said it out loud and he is saying it to the man the page is about and not to the yard.** The count of strangers is four and did not move on its twenty-eighth morning. |
| 629 | 29 | the fifth of the sixth month | **THE COUNT RISES TO FIVE AND THE MAN WHO DIGS LOAM SAYS OUT LOUD THAT HE WAS NEVER TOLD WHAT THE FIFTH LINE WOULD BE USED FOR, AND THAT HE PUT IT THERE FOR ONE QUESTION, AND THAT A PERSON WHO WROTE DOWN ONE QUESTION DID NOT WRITE DOWN THAT IT WOULD BE THE ONLY ONE.** A woman of about thirty-one comes down that lane at about ten, stops about four feet short of that second table, which is nearer than anybody has stood this month, and says that she chose where to stand. **A clerk notices that the count of strangers and the count of the five things this district does not have are both five this morning, says it out loud, and enters the two separately and says she has decided they are not the same thing and that is all she has.** The man who mends fencing says he is going to be asked something in that yard before the middle of the day and is telling the yard that morning instead of waiting. And then the man who digs loam says: I was never told what that line would be used for, I said I would put it there and I said I would put it there again, and nobody has ever told me what it is for, and I put it there for one question, and a person who wrote down one question did not write down that it would be the only one, and I have been at that end of this table for a month saying the first of those things and not saying the second, and I am saying the second one now and it took me a month and I am not going to take it back. **The boy says that is the first thing said out loud in that yard this month he could have told you what it was for before the man said it.** |
| 630 | 30 | the sixth of the sixth month | **THE BLOCK ENDS ON THE FIFTH LINE WORKING EXACTLY AS IT WAS WRITTEN AND ON NOBODY IN THAT YARD HAVING ASKED THE MAN IT NAMES ANYTHING ABOUT WHAT HE IS TO BE USED FOR, AND ON THE COUNT OF STRANGERS STANDING AT A FIGURE THIS DISTRICT CAN HOLD.** A clerk says the count of that lane was zero a month ago this morning and is five this morning and that five is a number this district can hold in its head. A man who puts tables up says five people have come down that lane and there are five lines on that book and nobody has ever asked him whether those are the same five, and that he has wanted to ask that all month and has not. **The man who mends fencing says that every one of the five has been answered by a man who has not been asked what he is for, and that the fifth line says ask that man and not ask anybody else, and that it does not say what about, and it has not been altered this morning and nobody in that yard has put a hand on it this month.** The man who digs loam says nobody has asked him what he is to be used for and that he is not going to help anybody along by saying something that would make it easier, and that a man who has said no to nobody is not a man who is owed an answer. **A clerk enters at about four that the fifth line has stood on that table for a month working exactly as it was written and that nobody in that yard has asked the man it names anything about what he is to be used for, and the number of people who have come down that lane with a page in their hand is five.** The ninth of the nine printed nights is four hundred and eleven days back, which is the figure on the sheet at that gatepost, and about four people near that wall have said that a figure is not a figure because it agrees with another figure. |

**14. WHAT THIS BLOCK DID NOT DO, AND A WRITER MUST NOT INFER THAT IT DID.** It did not pay the remedy and the reading of that lot is the fourth of the five on all fifteen days. It did not fill the column for the name of whoever read a thing out loud and appointed nobody to it. It did not correct, strike, supersede, replace or take out a figure, and it did not print the size of the error in the fourth of the four figures on that wall in a mouth. It did not run the bid or propose closing it. It did not name a night or close the ninth of the nine printed nights. It did not give the man of about sixty-four anything or ask him anything. It added no person to this district, no place inside it, no protected thing, no column, no fourteenth thing, no fifth document it does not own, no rate and no panel. It counted no month. It settled nothing about the protagonist's name and promised nothing and let no character prepare. It asked the reversal question once and did not foreshadow it. It restored no piece of the premise divergence, and the reserved scan over the fifteen files returns an empty dictionary.
