**631, sixth/7.** The count of that lane is five and did not move, and a clerk of nineteen years says out loud at about a quarter to eight, to about nineteen people, that on the ninth of this month she is going to count a month off a board with a finger on the wood in the open in front of everybody, and that she is telling them before she has done any of it because a clerk in that yard has done that thing twice and told them after both times. A man of about thirty-two who puts tables up says out loud that he has one question and has been carrying it about a week. A man of about thirty-four who mends fencing says he has noticed and that he has been mending fence. A man who cuts reeds says that a man who is not being asked anything has got nothing to put a question to. A man who keeps a tally says he has a figure for that month and is not going to say it before she counts it or after. A man who digs loam stands at the far end of that table for half an hour and says nothing. **5 claims, 3,179 words.**
**632, sixth/8.** The count does not move, and a man of about thirty-two who puts tables up puts his question — `What do you say to one of them` — at about ten, and the man who digs loam answers that whatever is true at the time, that it has been the same eleven words five times, and that a man who says the same thing five times has not had five conversations, he has had one, and that he is the one having it. A clerk enters a question and an answer on her own page for the first time in nineteen years and says out loud that she is not going to decide what that does before the next person has had a turn. The man who mends fencing says the question was not what he would have asked and that what he would have asked is worse. **4 claims, 2,954 words.**
**633, sixth/9. THE COUNT OF THE FIFTH MONTH.** Word had got round that parish before seven. A clerk counts the fifth month off a board with a finger on the wood, in the open, in daylight, with about nineteen people at the wall, in about nine minutes, with the boy of about nineteen at her left shoulder counting the marks with his own thumbnail and saying no number out loud the whole time. She says the figure out loud, once, in that yard, and says that the first of that month and the thirty-first of it are both on a page in this district and neither was written down by anybody who knew, and that she has corrected nothing because there was nothing to correct, and that about four people have been waiting for her to say so since the last time she counted. The man who mends fencing says he did not know what he expected and is going up that bank to put a rail in a fence. The boy says he could not count the nine minutes because he was counting the people counting the board, and that it is the one thing in that yard he has never once tried to do. The man who digs loam came up that bank at about a quarter to seven and watched the whole nine minutes and said nothing. **3 claims, 3,158 words. THE ONLY FIGURE ABOUT THE LENGTH OF THE FIFTH MONTH ON ANY PAGE OF CHAPTERS 601 TO 650 IS IN THAT ONE SPEECH.**
**634, sixth/10.** The count does not move, and a clerk enters the result of that count and enters a figure about a person on the same page — three hundred and fifty-nine mornings on which a man of about fifty-six has read four figures off that wall — **which is the first figure about a person that page has carried in nineteen years**, and she enters the figure and does not enter that he says them out loud, because nobody asked her to, and she says out loud that she sat at that table for about a quarter of an hour deciding which of two things to leave out. The man who mends fencing says the whole difference between him and her is that she waited to be asked to enter a figure about a man and he did not wait to be asked to put a line on a book. The man who digs loam says the morning he put that line on that book was the morning after a counted month. **3 claims, 2,914 words.**
**635, sixth/11. THE COUNT RISES TO SIX.** A man of about thirty-four who mends fencing says the reason out loud BEFORE he does it and then gets down on one knee in the grit and puts two staples in the loose near leg of that first table with a hammer, and says the reason is that a leg is a leg and that it is not the only thing in that yard nobody is being paid for and that is going to come loose, and that he is not doing it for anybody. A clerk enters the job and enters that she did not ask him why. A man of about thirty-two who puts tables up looks at the two staples for ten seconds, takes his own piece of slate out from under the leg, carries it to the low wall and says nothing at all. A man of about thirty-eight comes down that lane with a page in his hand and says that he has been down it twice and came back up both times and could see the yard from the top of that lane and did not know it was a yard. **3 claims, 2,964 words.**
**636, sixth/12.** The count does not move. A man of about thirty-two who puts tables up takes a barrowful of grit out of the gap between the two tables and down that bank with it and a clerk enters it. A clerk says out loud that she has not ruled a line and is not going to. **A man of about thirty-one who digs loam says out loud what he minds about the fifth line, which is that it is the only one anybody reads, because it is the only one of the five that is not a number and not a date and not a name, and he says he would rather be a man who says what he minds than a man that somebody has been standing about waiting on.** The man who mends fencing says he is not going to agree with him about it, because agreeing with a man about what he minds is a thing he has no standing to do. **3 claims, 2,899 words, the shortest chapter of the block.**
**637, sixth/13.** The count does not move. A clerk announces at about a quarter to eight that before eleven she is going to ask a person in that yard one question about one line in that book, and gives the reason, which is that four people at that wall have asked her this month whether anybody in this district had ever asked and that she has said no four times. A woman of about fifty-eight comes up that bank at about eleven with an empty pail. **The clerk asks `What is on the fourth line` and the woman does not answer and does not move and picks her pail up and goes down that bank, and about nine people in that yard say nothing at all, and a clerk enters the question and enters that nothing was said to it and enters that a clerk may not enter nothing said as an answer.** This is the sixth person in that yard to say nothing this month and the first of the six anybody has asked. The man who mends fencing says he is the man who put a line on that book and that nobody asked him one thing about the fourth line and that he would not have known what to do with it. **4 claims, 3,168 words.**
**638, sixth/14. THE COUNT RISES TO SEVEN.** A woman of about twenty-six comes down that lane at about ten with a page in her hand and says one thing out loud in that yard that is not about the page, which is that she got to the water at about two in the afternoon on the second day and a man in a ditch asked if she wanted a hand and she shook her head, and that she has not spoken above a murmur since, and that she has still not opened it. A clerk enters that she is not entering her in the column for the name of whoever read a thing out loud, and gives the reason, which is that she is not from this parish and that the clerk has never had to decide that about a person standing in front of her in that yard. The man who mends fencing says there is a thing about that lane which is not the page and that he has no figure at all for it. The man who digs loam says that a man who answers about a page does not get asked about anything else. **4 claims, 3,173 words.**
**639, sixth/15.** The count does not move. A clerk asks the man of about thirty-four who mends fencing one question — `What is that count a count of` — and he answers that he is the only one in that yard nobody has asked anything today, and that he has counted every morning of that off that wall since the fourth morning of this month and got seven off it this morning before about nine, and that he has not told anybody he does it, and that the reason he does it is that a man who is counting is not standing about, and that he has been standing about, and that he did not know that until this morning. **The clerk then says out loud that it is a count of people who have come down that lane with a page in their hand, that it is not a count of pages, that it is not a count of the questions anybody has put to the man at the end of that line, that she has been calling it a count of strangers since the sixth morning, and that she is changing the name of a thing she has been entering for a month and not the thing.** The man who digs loam says he has been called a stranger in that yard every morning this month and has not said one word about the word. **4 claims, 2,997 words.**

A clerk says out loud that she has entered a figure about a person and put a question to a person and taken a word off a count in the last ten mornings, and that not one of those three is a thing this district has made, and that the count of the things this district has made is thirteen and has been thirteen on every morning of this month, and that she would like that entered by somebody and nobody has asked her to enter it. **A man of about thirty-one who digs loam says out loud that he has been at the end of that table at about half past eleven on most mornings this month and has said one thing out loud on almost every one of them, and that a man who says one thing at the same hour on the same morning is a man who is waiting, and that he has worked that out about himself, and that he is not going to stop, and that he said it on a morning when nobody asked him one question.** The man who mends fencing says he has put two staples in a leg and carried wire up that bank four mornings and taken a barrow of grit down it and has not been asked for one item of that and has not been paid for one item of that, and that a clerk found out yesterday that he counts that wall every morning. **3 claims, 3,072 words. THE CLOSING LEDGER NAMES THE FIFTH LINE WORKING EXACTLY AS IT WAS WRITTEN, THAT NOBODY IN THAT YARD HAS ASKED THE MAN IT NAMES ANYTHING ABOUT WHAT HE IS TO BE USED FOR, THE READING OF THAT LOT AS THE FOURTH OF THE FIVE, THE COUNT OF THE LANE AS SEVEN, THE COUNT OF COUNTED MONTHS AS FIVE, AND A MAN WITH HIS RIGHT HAND FLAT ON THE BOARDS AND A HAMMER IN HIS OWN HAND.**

**Chapter 641, the seventeenth of the sixth month, day 41.** The count of that lane stands at seven and did not move. A clerk of nineteen years says out loud at about half past nine, to about nineteen people, that she is going to enter the same figure again that afternoon whether it moves or not, and gives the reason, which is that a figure is what I write in the morning and a fact is what I write in the afternoon, and that a number that has stood all morning without anybody in that yard saying anything that would move it is a figure and not a fact. A man of about thirty-two who puts tables up is in the yard before seven and rocks a low corner of that second table four times with his own shoulder and says nothing to anybody. A man of about forty-eight who keeps a tally comes up that bank at about ten with a flat book shut under his left arm, stands at the east end of that yard where he has stood for about thirty years, and says his one thing out loud in this block: that he has a figure for every month in that book and has never showed one of them to anybody, and that on the ninth of this month a clerk counted a month off that board with a finger on the wood in the open with about nineteen people at that wall, and that he stood about nine feet off her with the book shut under his arm and did not open it, and has not opened it since. A man of about thirty-one who digs loam comes up at about half past ten and says nothing. The boy of about nineteen says his one thing out loud in this block, about what he cannot count: that he has counted the people who came down that lane three ways this month and has not got the same number out of any two of them, and nobody in that yard counts that lane but the clerk, and he has not asked her. Three things were said out loud. The remedy was unpaid and the reading of that lot was the fourth of the five.

**Chapter 642, the eighteenth, day 42.** The count does not move. The boards have a grey skin on them and the gap between the two tables is clear for the first time in most of a month, because the grit in it went down the bank in the water. A man of about thirty-two who puts tables up has a straight edge of his own making under the near end of that second table and goes along the boards with his own eye for about ten minutes and nobody says anything to him about it. A clerk asks him one thing out loud at about half past nine, seven words long, and he answers it in about ninety-three words, and the answer is about himself: that he set both those tables up himself, that he set the first one and came back eleven days after it and set the second, that he has not been able to make the two of them level on one single morning since, that he has come up that bank about every morning to see whether that has changed, and that nobody in that yard ever asked him to do any of it and he is not going to ask anybody to help him do it. A clerk enters the question and the answer and enters that the answer was about himself and not about a page. A man of about thirty-four who mends fencing comes up at about ten and says out loud that a man at the near end of that table has said he will not ask anybody for help, that that is the third man in that yard this month to stand at his own work and say a thing like that, and that he is not going to be the one who says it to the other two. A man of about thirty-one who digs loam says one thing about a ditch a mile off that is full today and a man standing at the top of it, and that he is not going to tell him to get out of it. Four things were said out loud, two of them by one man, which the ledger enters as three speeches and a question. The remedy was unpaid.

**Chapter 643, the nineteenth of the sixth month, day 43. THE MIDPOINT OF THE BLOCK.** The count does not move. The boards are pale and dry and cracking along the grain and there is a fine pale line of dust in the gap between the two tables. **A man of about thirty-four who mends fencing comes up that bank at about ten with nothing in his own hands, which nobody at that low wall can remember him doing before, puts his own right hand flat on the boards, and says out loud in about a hundred and six words that he is not going to stand in his own yard for another morning and pretend that he does not know what it is for, that it is for keeping a number, that that is the whole of what it has ever been for, and that nobody in that yard has asked him about it and he is not going to wait until somebody does.** A man of about thirty-one who digs loam answers it out loud, at about half past ten, and says that he is not going to say it after him, because a man who says a thing out loud after another man has said it out loud is not saying it. A clerk says out loud at about half past eleven, in about a hundred and eighteen words, that she entered what he said and did not enter it as a reason, and that a reason is a thing a person gives out loud before a hand moves, and that what he said came after five weeks of nothing happening, and that this is the first time in nineteen years that a person in that yard has told her what to enter, and that nobody told her and he is not a clerk. Three things were said out loud. **The count of the things this district has made did not move, and a clerk entered the reason out loud with it, which is that what a yard is for is a thing a man says and not a thing a district has.** The remedy was unpaid and nobody in that yard said that the fifth line does not say what it is for.

**Chapter 644, the twentieth, day 44. THE COUNT RISES TO EIGHT.** A clerk at about a quarter to eight enters seven at a quarter to eight and enters that a person and not a clock is what will decide the figure. A man of about thirty-four who mends fencing, standing at the near end of that second table with his own two hands in his own pockets, says out loud at about ten that there has been a person standing at the top of that lane for about twenty minutes and has neither come down it nor gone back up it, and that he has not gone up there to look, and that he does not know what a person stands at the top of a lane for. **A man of about forty-one then comes down that lane at about ten with a folded page in his own right hand, stands about five feet short of the near end of that second table, and says out loud that he has not come down that lane to find out whether the page came out of this yard and has not come down it to hand it to anybody, and has come down it because it says there is a man at the end of it.** A clerk enters the hour, that he had a page in his hand, and that she is not entering his name, and then says out loud at about half past ten that the figure is eight, that it was seven at a quarter to eight, that she is entering the reason now and the reason is that a person said a thing out loud in this yard, and that a page a person has carried down a lane in his own hand is not a fifth of the four documents this district does not own. Three things were said out loud. **The count moved because a person spoke, not because a clock passed.**

**Chapter 645, the twenty-first, day 45. THE WORD IS SAID OUT LOUD AND THE YARD DOES NOT MOVE.** There is low cloud and the boards have gone cold and dark. A clerk entered at about a quarter to eight that she was going to say one word out loud in that yard this morning, and entered that before she said it. **At about half past nine the clerk says the word remedy out loud in that yard, in about a hundred and forty-seven words, and says that she has been writing it in that book every morning of this month and has never once said it out loud, that a remedy is the fifth of the five things a document that sets a lot out has to say, that it is unpaid and has been unpaid on every morning that book has stood on that table, and that what the fifth line does is tell a person with a page in their hand where to go and who to ask, and that that is the whole of what it does, and that she has said the word and nothing in this yard has moved, and that is what she wanted to find out.** A man of about thirty-one who digs loam says one thing out loud about having read four of the five lines about four hundred times and not reading the fifth this month once, because it is his and he knows what it says. A man of about thirty-four who mends fencing says out loud that he has heard the word off the end of her book every morning this month and never in a mouth, and that he is not going to say out loud which of the two he thinks is the true one, and does not finish. **The count of that lane did not move, the count of the things this district has made did not move, the count of conditions with no end on it did not move, and the reading of that lot is still the fourth of the five.** Three things were said out loud.

**Chapter 646, the twenty-second, day 46.** The count does not move. A man of about thirty-four who mends fencing puts his own right hand flat on the near end of that second table at about nine, rocks it, and says out loud that he is going to take up about half an inch of the footing of that low leg and put a wedge of his own under it, and that he is saying that out loud before he touches it, and that nobody in that yard has asked him to, and that he is not doing it for the book and not for the two tables, and that a table that rocks cannot have anything written on it, and that he would not want anything written on it in a yard like this one. **He then gets his own right knee on that bank at about ten, levers the footing up about half an inch with a flat bar of his own, drives a wedge of his own under it with the heel of his own hand, puts his own palm flat on the boards and pushes, and the corner does not move, and about nine people watch all of it and say nothing at any point in it.** A clerk says out loud at about half past ten that she entered the job and did not ask him why, that he is the only person in that yard who says the reason before he touches it, and that a repair is not a making and the count did not move when he got his knee on that bank. A man of about thirty-one who digs loam says out loud that he watched a man fix a thing he could not fix in a fortnight, that he is not going to say thank you, and that the reason is that he does not know what a man is paid in, and that a figure is not a way of paying anybody. Three things were said out loud. **The fifth of the five things this district does not have was named and was not paid, and a man saying out loud that he does not know what a man is paid in is not a payment.**

**Chapter 647, the twenty-third, day 47.** There is a thin rain that does not soak anything. **A woman of about fifty-eight comes up that bank at about eleven with an empty pail in her own hand and stops at the near end of that second table, which she has not done in the whole of the time about nine people at that low wall can remember, stands there about twenty-five minutes, reads three lines in that lot book standing up with her own shoulder four inches off the boards, and says nothing at all to anybody, and about nine people in that yard say nothing at all to her.** A clerk says out loud at about half past eleven that she is not going to ask that woman one thing, and gives the reason before the afternoon, and the reason is that she asked her one question in this yard earlier this month and she said nothing, and that a person who does not answer a question the first time is not asked it a second time, and that she told about nineteen people beforehand that she was going to ask it and she asked it and got nothing and entered that she got nothing. A man of about thirty-four who mends fencing says out loud, standing four feet off that woman, that he has stood at that end of the table for five weeks saying things out loud about ten feet off a man who has not answered one word, that it is the same as what is happening over there, and that he is not going to move off that board and give her more room than she has taken. A man of about thirty-one who digs loam says out loud that a man being asked nothing and a woman not answering a question are not the same thing and that this district has never put them in the same count, and neither has he. **She did not come up past those tables at about ten to six that evening, which about four people at the foot of that bank noticed, and she went back down that bank at about half past twelve with an empty pail.** Three things were said out loud. She is not a resident and is not in the column.

**Chapter 648, the twenty-fourth, day 48. THE COUNT RISES TO NINE.** A hard dry day and by about two in the afternoon the shadow of that low wall has come across the boards and gone off them again, and a man who is not from this parish stands in it and watches it do that. **A man of about thirty-three comes down that lane at about ten on the words of the fifth line, stands about four feet off the end of that second table with his own back to the low wall, and says out loud that he has read the line twice and understood all of it, and that a person can understand every word of a thing and still not know what it is for, and that he is going to stand there for a while and is not going to bother anybody.** A man of about thirty-one who digs loam says out loud that he has watched a stranger stand there for half an hour and has not asked him one thing, and has not gone over, and that a man who digs loam a mile off is not a man to walk up and introduce himself to somebody. **At about one the stranger says out loud that he has stood in that yard about three hours and nobody has asked him one thing, that he is going to put the page down on the end of that table and is telling about nineteen people why before he does it, and that the reason is that it came out of his own pocket this morning and it is going back into his own pocket before he goes up that bank, and that he is doing that so that he cannot say afterwards that he came down a lane and stood in a yard and never once put the thing down.** He then puts it down flat about a foot off the near copy, looks at the four of them on it for about four minutes, picks it up and puts it back inside his own coat, and about nine people in that yard say nothing while he does both of those things. A clerk says out loud at about half past two that the figure is nine, that it moved because a person said two things out loud in that yard, that a person can come down a lane on the words of a line in a book and stand in a yard for a whole day and be asked nothing, and that this district has no count for that and is not going to get one out of her this afternoon, and that a page a person puts down and takes off again is not a fifth of the four documents this district does not own. **The number of pages on the end of that second table went to three for about four minutes and came back to two, and it is a different figure from the tenure of the copy at the near end, which stood at a hundred and forty-six mornings that day.** Four things were said out loud, two of them by a person who is not from this parish.

**Chapter 649, the twenty-fifth of the sixth month, day 49. THE ASKING AND THE NO.** It had rained in the night and stopped about six, and the boards were running with it by about seven, and the whole of that bank was the colour of a wet road, and there was a rail in the fence at the far end of that second table. **A man of about thirty-three who is not from this parish comes down that lane at about nine and stands at the near end of that second table with his own hands in his own coat and says nothing at all until the man who digs loam is at the far end of it.** A man of about thirty-one who digs loam comes up that bank at about half past ten with the black of the ditch running off the back of his own right leg and down onto the boards, sets his own spade up against the leg of that second table, goes to the rail in the fence at the far end of it and puts his own two hands on it. **At about eleven the stranger says, in about five seconds and four words, `What do you want.` and nobody in that yard says anything at all for about nine seconds, and the water came off the boards at the low side of that second table while it went on.** The man of about thirty-one turns round at the rail and says no, and that he did not come down that lane for that, and that the line of his names a place and names him and that is all it does, and that nobody in that yard has asked him one question in five weeks, and that the stranger stood in that yard yesterday for a whole day and did not ask him one either, and that he is not taking it back, and that he is going to go on standing at that end of that table in the morning and saying things out loud now and then, and that he is not going to be a person that a line in a book points at. A clerk enters the question, the answer, and that the answer began with the word no. **A man of about thirty-seven who cuts reeds, standing about eight feet off the near end of that second table, says out loud that those two sentences have never been in the same morning before in that yard, and that a man who says both of them on one morning has made a thing this district has no word for, and that he has been cutting reeds on that water since he was about eleven years old and has a word for a good many things and has not got one for that.** **The same stranger then turns round to the near end of that second table and asks the man of about thirty-four who mends fencing, in six words, `What do you think about it.`** And the man of about thirty-four answers out loud, in about a hundred and sixty-five words in all across two speeches, that he put that line on that book, that he asked the question in this yard on the second of the fifth month and did not ask whether a stranger might come down that lane with something else, and that he knew the answer to that at the time and said it out loud anyway, and that he is the only person in that yard who can be asked that, and that he is not to be thanked for that morning and nobody should thank him for it, and that he would like that entered before anything else anybody says about it. **A clerk says out loud at about half past eleven that she is going to enter a no on that page that afternoon, and that it is the first time in nineteen years that a thing in that yard has been a person refusing something rather than a figure going down, and that she is entering the reason with it, which is that a person said no in that yard in the ordinary voice and a page is where a person says a thing when it is going to be said twice, and that a clerk is the only person there who can make a no permanent, and that she is not going to strike it out tomorrow, and that she has been right about a great many things this month and that a clerk who is never wrong is not a clerk.** Six things were said out loud. **At about four a clerk enters a no on her own page for the first time in nineteen years and enters the reason with it, and the reading of that lot is the fifth of the five and is not the sixth, and the fifth line is untouched, and the count of that lane is nine and did not move, and the fifth of the five things this district does not have is not paid, and a man saying no is not a payment.**

**Chapter 650, the twenty-sixth of the sixth month, day 50, the hundred and seventy-seventh morning after the count. THE FIXED FINAL IMAGE, AND VOLUME 13 ENDS HERE.** There is a hard white rime on the boards of that second table by about seven and the whole of that bank is dry and hard and the colour of a road that has not been touched since the frost, and the fifth line of that lot book is still on that book. **The first table has that lot book on it under a stone with no wear on it, and the book has five lines on it, and the fifth of those five is in a clerk's hand at the dicta of the man the third line is about and has not been touched, and the fourth of those five is still nine words in that same hand, and nobody in that yard put a hand on either of them on any morning of this month. The second table has the stone lying face up on it, and the hollow in the middle of that stone is one inch deep and there is nothing at either end of it, and about nine people at that low wall have said since that they have looked at that hollow about four hundred mornings and that nobody has ever put a thumb in it and that nobody has ever said out loud in that yard what it is for.** Two pages were lying along the end of that second table, the copy at the near end has now been on that wood for a hundred and forty-eight mornings, and the number of pages on the end of that second table is two and is entered as two and is not a figure off that other figure and is not one more than it was the day before. **A clerk of nineteen years enters the count of the board on her own page at about a quarter to eight and does not have to look up to do it.** A man of about thirty-four who mends fencing comes up at about half past ten with nothing in his own hands and says out loud that he said earlier this month what that yard is for and is not going to say it twice, that he has not been back down to that ditch since, that he is going back down to it after this, that nobody has told him what he is going to be asked down there, that nobody told him the last time either, and that he is going down there anyway. **A man of about thirty-one who digs loam comes up that bank at about eleven with his spade over his own right shoulder and mud to the thigh, sets the spade against the leg of that second table at the far end of it, goes the three steps to the rail in the fence and puts his own two hands on it and stays there, and says one thing out loud: that a clerk entered a thing about him yesterday and did not put it in that book, that he has not asked her to and is not going to, that he came up this bank this morning before she had that book open, that he has come up this bank every morning this month, that that is all he has got and he is not going to make it into more than that, and that there is a man standing in that yard who is still paid nothing and he is him.** The road keeper came up that lane at about half past one and was not asked about the eleven miles. At about half past three a man of about sixty-four was at the foot of that low wall with his coat folded on the stones beside him and nothing in his hands, and nobody offered him anything and nobody asked him anything, and the record about the not offering says nothing was asked and nothing was given, and this is his two hundred and eighty-ninth night of that run and he has slept on two hundred and eighty-eight of them. **Two hundred and ninety-eight days is how long the bid has been open and it was not run on this day. The ninth of the nine printed nights is four hundred and thirty-one days back and no night has been named. A body four hundred miles off is two hundred and sixty-seven days past a printing it did not make and nobody in that yard watched it. The count of things this district has made is thirteen and a remedy being paid is not a fifteenth, because a remedy is a thing a person does and a protected thing is a thing a district has. The reading of that lot is the fifth of the five and not the sixth, the page is still on the table, the fifth line is untouched, and the man is still findable in the morning and is still not paid. The fifth of the five things this district does not have is not paid and the count of the five is still five. The column for the name of whoever read a thing out loud was ruled and empty at about six, and no person has been appointed to it, and a man who said no yesterday is not in it and a man who said what he is still not paid is not in it.** Two things were said out loud. **The chapter closes on a clerk's entry that names the fifth line, the man, the reading of that lot, the count of that lane, and the spade against the leg of the table and the two hands on the rail, and it does not reach the twenty-seventh of the sixth month.**

**AND THE COUNTS EVERY BLOCK SUMMARY IN THIS VOLUME MAY REUSE UNCHANGED:** thirteen, five and paid on none, four, five, four, four, nine and standing, four places, **two pages on the end of that second table at the quarter-to-eight entry on all fifty mornings, and the one morning of the fifty on which that count moved is Chapter 648, where it went to three for about four minutes in the middle of the day and came back to two before the light went, with a person in the room and the reason in the room before the hand both times. Two state files had said it never moved and both were corrected in place on 2026-09-29; the earlier of the two, at this volume's Block 0003 line, is scoped to those ten mornings and is correct as it stands**, four hundred and eleven, the column empty, the bid not run, and the count of people who have come down that lane with a page in their hand, which is not a ladder and stands at nine on the fiftieth morning.

**AND THE FIGURES THAT ARE NOT A CHAPTER SUMMARY'S BUSINESS AND ARE NOT OMITTED FOR BEING LARGE: the protagonist's name is on no page of six hundred and fifty chapters and was not named, promised, dated or prepared by this block; there is still no Volume 12 close, no Volume 12 roll summary and no paid sentence for what an admission is for, across six hundred and fifty chapters and across two volumes; the count of counted months is five and **the length of the fifth month is NOT the first debt of whatever comes after this volume: Chapter 633, the thirty-third morning, counted it off a board with a finger on the wood in the open in about nine minutes and it came out at thirty-one. The length of the fourth month was counted earlier, at Chapter 605, and came out at thirty. `outline/volume-13.md` says in three places that the fifth month's length is uncounted and is Volume 14's first debt, and contradicts itself at its own section 1.7; the chapters are canon and the outline is not a close's to edit**; two of the four figures on that wall are out and the size of neither was printed in a mouth; and the next phase of this pipeline is a close, and a close may not read a premise failure as a named decision.**

**AND THE REVIEW FIX OF 2026-09-29, WHICH TOUCHED SIX OF THESE FIFTY CHAPTERS AND NOTHING ELSE.** A review of this block found `five weeks` on twelve pages across Chapters 643, 645, 646, 647, 648 and 650, ten mouths, describing the second table's tenure. **That table's own on-page tenure is the FORTIETH morning at Chapter 640 and the FIFTIETH at Chapter 650, so the correct figures for days 43 to 50 are forty-three, forty-five, forty-six, forty-seven, forty-eight and fifty mornings, and thirty-five was a bystander's rounding carried forward from day 35.** Each occurrence was replaced with the figure for its own day. **Every substitution was two words for two words, so all nineteen counted claims, all nineteen word figures and all thirty-four stamped durations in this block are unchanged and were re-verified after the edit with zero mismatches. No summary above was withdrawn, no plot was altered, no chapter was restarted, and no date moved.** The nine surviving instances in Chapters 631 to 639 were left alone because that block is canon from an earlier phase and a review fix does not edit another block's chapters; **one of them, at Chapter 635, is exactly right, because day 35 is thirty-five mornings.**

## THE THREE CHAPTERS A BLOCK 0002 WRITER MUST READ IN FULL

**Chapter 655, because the sixth month is counted on it and its own printed speech settles every date from day 5 to day 50 of this volume. Chapter 659, because it is the one morning the lane count moved, and the person and the reason were in the yard before the clerk's hand. Chapter 665, because it is the block's last morning and it carries the two fifths on facing clauses, and because a block that reads the two fifths as one fifth will conflate them at exactly the point the page is built to stop.**

## THE REVIEW FIX OF 2026-09-29, APPLIED TO BLOCK 0001 AFTER THE FIFTEEN CHAPTERS WERE WRITTEN

**THIS IS AN ENTRY IN THE ROLLING STATE AND NOT A CHAPTER AND IT ADDS NOTHING TO THE STORY. NOT ONE EVENT, FIGURE, DATE, COUNT, SPEECH, CLAIM, DURATION, LADDER ROW, RELAY OR NAME ON PAGES 651 TO 665 CHANGED. WHAT CHANGED IS FIFTEEN TITLES, FOURTEEN BYSTANDER PARAGRAPHS, AND FOUR FIGURES IN TWO STATE FILES THAT WERE MEASURED WRONG.**

**THE FIFTEEN TITLES.** All fifteen were summaries of their own morning's two speeches and ran 28 to 43 words with a median of 35, fourteen over the volume's own thirty-word cap. All fifteen now run 17 to 25 with a median of 22 and none over the cap. **The `On The <Date>` prefix is on all fifteen and was not touched, so the date of record for each morning is unchanged and the fifteen dates are still fifteen consecutive days, the twenty-seventh of the sixth month to the tenth of the seventh month.** A writer loading these fifteen pages for voice should load the prose and not the old titles.

**THE FOURTEEN BYSTANDER PARAGRAPHS.** Fourteen paragraphs across twelve mornings nested one attribution clause inside another inside another, at up to 173 words with no internal sentence end after the first sentence. They were broken into plain sentences. The secondhand-observer register is kept, because it is this volume's signature and it is the reason the fifth line goes unanswered; what was removed is the stacking. The clauses `about four of them have said that`, `about four of you have worked out that` and `have said since that` stand at 49, 76 and 113, against 80, 76 and 117 before. **The rule is written down as guardrail 21 in `workspace/volume-14/batch-0002/PROMPT.md` and a later block does not have to rediscover it.**

**THE FOUR FIGURES.** `state/volume-14-batch-0001-summary.md` Section 9 said three identical paragraphs and the sweep returns two; the third was the second table, which is a sentence inside a longer paragraph, and a sentence is not a paragraph. That miscount had already been copied into the next phase's prompt and is corrected in both. The cart row's `where` column said `every one but 663` and the cart paragraph is absent from 652 and 663. The next prompt's body-word figure of 43,566 was wrong against 43,575 and has been re-derived again after the prose repair at 43,258 body words over 630 segments. Length over the fifteen files is now 43,655 by `wc -w`, mean 2,910.3, against 44,185 and 2,945.7 before.

**AND THE SIX THAT WERE NOT DEFECTS.** `logs/batch-0001.review.log` reports the eighteen-row ladder broken on all fifteen chapters, `three hundredth` resolving to 103, Chapter 661 missing a row, Chapter 660 missing its ledger line, the clerk's short speeches stamped at twice the block's speaking rate, and six titles without a date. All six are wrong and all six were bugs in the reviewer's own hand-built word-number parser — compound ordinals, round-hundred ordinals, ordinal suffixes after `hundred`, the singular verb on a single-claim morning, a reciprocal rate, and hyphenated date words. The ladder is 270 cells with zero failures, Chapter 661 carries its row, Chapter 660 correctly reads `About one thing was said out loud`, and all fifteen titles carry their date. **A later block that builds its own resolver should read the two traps the block record already names at Section 2 and Section 3, because this review re-derived them from nothing and reported them as new.**

# VOLUME 14, BLOCK 0002 — CHAPTERS 666 TO 680, FIFTEEN MORNINGS, THE ELEVENTH TO THE TWENTY-FIFTH OF THE SEVENTH MONTH

**CONVENTION: ONE LINE A CHAPTER, THE THING THAT HAPPENED, AND THE COUNTED CLAIMS FOR THAT MORNING. THE MEASUREMENT IS AT `state/volume-14-batch-0002-summary.md`.**

- **666, day 16, the eleventh of the seventh month.** Neither of the two men was standing in that yard at about eleven and a clerk entered that both of them were at the bottom of that bank and entered no reason for either. A clerk and a boy said one thing out loud each. 2 claims.
- **667, day 17, the twelfth.** The man of about thirty-two who puts tables up said out loud that nobody in that yard has ever once asked him how many tables he has put up in it, and a clerk said out loud that a counter is a place and is not on the list of four. 2 claims.
- **668, day 18, the thirteenth.** A clerk said out loud that nobody has said one word out loud in that yard this week about either of those two men, and a boy said out loud that everything said twice in that yard this month is about a person and not about a figure. 2 claims.
- **669, day 19, the fourteenth.** A clerk said out loud that there is water standing in the bottom of that ditch and that she has not put a number on the depth of it. One thing said out loud in the yard. 1 claim.
- **670, day 20, the fifteenth.** The man who puts tables up said out loud that a man came down that lane with a page in his own right hand and did not stop, and a clerk entered eleven after he had said it. 2 claims.
- **671, day 21, the sixteenth.** A boy said out loud that he has two numbers for the people who come up that bank before a quarter to eight and is going to say neither, and a clerk said out loud that she has entered the lane count on a person's word twice and never once on her own eyes. 2 claims.
- **672, day 22, the seventeenth.** A clerk said out loud that the figure on the sheet at that gatepost and the age of that figure have arrived at the same number on their own, and a boy said out loud that a count which never changes is a count of him and not of the people. 2 claims.
- **673, day 23, the eighteenth.** The man who puts tables up said out loud that he is not shimming that leg again this month, and a clerk said out loud that she is entering that he said it and not that it is done. 2 claims.
- **674, day 24, the nineteenth.** A clerk said out loud that she has entered that neither of those two men was in that yard at about eleven for a fourth morning this month and entered no reason four times, and a boy counted that yard at about ten and at about eleven, got the same number both times, and named nobody. 2 claims.
- **675, day 25, the twentieth. THE MIDPOINT.** A man of about forty-eight who keeps a tally asked out loud, in the ordinary voice, in the open, at about eleven, to about nineteen people, who is going to answer a stranger next week. Nobody answered it. The man of fifty-six went on reading his four off the wall. The boy counted the silence on his own two fingers, got fourteen, read the number back to himself in a low voice, and said it out loud. A clerk entered the question, the fourteen and no answer. 3 claims, and the ledger says three.
- **676, day 26, the twenty-first.** A clerk said out loud that those three pages up that bank are the only three things a stranger can read with nobody standing next to him and that reading one where it lies is not owning it, and a boy said out loud that he counted the quiet yesterday and not the question. 2 claims.
- **677, day 27, the twenty-second.** A clerk said out loud that a page in this district points at a man and not at a yard and that she is not going to put a place on it, and the man who mends fencing said one thing out loud, about a post in his own fence and four visits to it, and that he is not going to say a thing a fourth time. 2 claims.
- **678, day 28, the twenty-third.** A clerk said out loud that not one figure in that book has ever been handed to a person, and a boy said out loud that a man went down that lane at about half past nine with a page in his own right hand and did not stop, and the count moved to twelve after he said it. 2 claims.
- **679, day 29, the twenty-fourth.** The man who digs loam said one thing out loud, about the top inch of that loam being colder than the inch under it and about not guessing, and a clerk entered that nobody has asked him to stay at the bottom of that bank and nobody is going to. One thing said out loud in the yard. 1 claim.
- **680, day 30, the twenty-fifth.** A clerk said out loud that two of the four figures on that wall are out and that she is not going to correct either of them, and a boy said out loud that he has a number for what that man says and a much bigger one for what it is for and cannot put the second one in a figure. The two fifths went into one sentence pair on purpose. 2 claims.

# VOLUME 14, BLOCK 0003 — CHAPTERS 681 TO 690, TEN MORNINGS, THE TWENTY-SIXTH OF THE SEVENTH MONTH TO THE FOURTH OF THE EIGHTH MONTH

**CONVENTION: ONE LINE A CHAPTER, THE THING THAT HAPPENED, AND THE COUNTED CLAIMS FOR THAT MORNING. THE MEASUREMENT IS AT `state/volume-14-batch-0003-summary.md`.**

- **681, day 31, the twenty-sixth of the seventh month.** The man of about thirty-two who puts tables up said out loud that he put both legs of that first table in himself, that he has been at them twice this month, that both are sound, and that nobody in this yard has ever asked him about it. A clerk said out loud that the count of counted months is six, that the seventh has not been counted by anybody, and that she is not going to count a month she is standing in. 2 claims.
- **682, day 32, the twenty-seventh.** The man who puts tables up put his own palm flat into the lifted corner of that second table and said out loud that the corner has been coming up since the beginning of the sixth month and has got about the width of a thumbnail of board under it where there was none. A boy said out loud that he has been counting the marks in chalk along the edge of that second table since the first morning of the seventh month, that he has got a number, and that he is not going to say it. 2 claims.
- **683, day 33, the twenty-eighth.** The man of about thirty-four who mends fencing put his own two hands flat on the near end of that second table and said out loud that the wood moved under them and that he has been putting them on that end about once a morning for a month and has never told anybody in that district. A clerk said out loud that in nineteen years this is the first morning a man in that yard has told her a thing with his own two hands instead of his own mouth. 2 claims.
- **684, day 34, the twenty-ninth.** The man who digs loam came up that bank before it was light with mud to the thigh and said out loud that the ground at the foot of that bank is hard on the west side to about a foot and soft on the east side to the knee and that he has been cutting in the soft part. A boy said out loud that he has counted the pages on the end of that second table every morning this month and it has been two every morning, and named the figure in this district that does not move. 2 claims.
- **685, day 35, the thirtieth. THE LAST MORNING THE SECOND TABLE IS IN THAT YARD.** The man of about forty-eight who keeps a tally stood at that low wall before it was light and said out loud that he has been getting nobody standing at it for about a month and got none this morning. A clerk said out loud that the near leg of that second table has been out of square for about a month and that she has entered it every morning of that month as a leg and not as a figure. 2 claims.
- **686, day 36, the thirty-first of the seventh month. THE CARRYING.** The man who puts tables up and the man who drives that cart got that second table up off the ground together at about ten past ten and carried it down that bank with one of them at each end and set it down at the foot of it at about half past one. A clerk took the stone and the lot book down on it with the five lines face up, and no hand went on any of the five lines while it was going. Nobody said one word to either of them about doing it and nobody was asked to. The man who mends fencing went down that bank at about ten and did not come back up it that day. The man of fifty-six read his four at the top of that bank with about four people hearing him and did not come down. Three things said out loud: the man who puts tables up, on the morning it took, on not getting it back up, and on never having been paid for one table in that yard; the man who drives that cart, on having come up that bank on his own two feet for the first time; and a clerk, on having entered that a man carried a table and where he carried it to and nothing about anybody must. 3 claims, and the ledger says three.
- **687, day 37, the first of the eighth month. TENURE ONE.** A clerk said out loud that the table has stood at the foot of that bank one morning, that she entered nothing yesterday morning and one this morning, and that a count of nothing is not a count of nobody. The man who puts tables up said out loud that he has not put a card under that leg and is not going to. 2 claims.
- **688, day 38, the second of the eighth month. TENURE TWO, AND THE LANE COUNT MOVES.** A boy said out loud that a man went down that lane at about half past nine with a page in his own right hand, came to the top of that bank, looked at that first table, found nothing on it, and went on down. A clerk entered thirteen after he had said it, before she had that book open for the second entry, and entered the reason with it. 2 claims.
- **689, day 39, the third of the eighth month. TENURE THREE.** A boy counted that yard at about ten and again at about eleven, got about nineteen both times, and said out loud that a table went down that bank two mornings ago and that the number of people in the yard did not go down with it. A clerk said out loud that the book is on that table at the foot of that bank and that the fifth of those five lines is still one condition with no end on it and still working exactly as it was written. 2 claims.
- **690, day 40, the fourth of the eighth month. TENURE FOUR.** The man who puts tables up went down that bank at about eight, put the near leg of that second table square with his own hands and his own knee, and said out loud that he said on the first morning of the eighth month he was not going to put a card under that leg and has not, and that a man is not thanked in this district for a thing he does to a table. A clerk said out loud that the number of mornings that table has stood at the foot of that bank is four, that she has entered it four mornings running, and that she entered nothing on the morning it went down. 2 claims.

### VOLUME 14, BLOCK 0003, THE REVIEW REPAIR OF 2026-09-29 — WHAT CHANGED IN CHAPTERS 681 TO 690, AND WHAT DID NOT

**NO EVENT, NO SPEAKER, NO AUDIENCE, NO ORDER AND NO COUNT CHANGED IN ANY OF THE TEN CHAPTERS. WHAT CHANGED IS HOW TWENTY-ONE MEN AND WOMEN JOINED THEIR OWN SENTENCES TOGETHER, AND EVERY FIGURE THAT IS A COUNT OF A SPEECH WAS RE-DERIVED BECAUSE THE SPEECHES MOVED.**

**THE DEFECT WAS ONE SENTENCE SHAPE REPEATED AS FILLER. `about four of you have worked out that` stood at sixty-five occurrences across the ten chapters, six to nine a chapter, and up to four times inside a single speech on chapter 686, and it closed all twenty-one relay speeches with one of two signatures. It is now twenty-one, exactly one per relay speech, and every one of the twenty-one speeches closes differently. No content was removed from any speech: the second and third frames in each became plain predicates, which says the same claim without an attribution wrapper around it.**

**CONSEQUENTLY, AND BECAUSE THE HOUSE COUNTS EVERY SPEECH: all twenty-one counted claims and all twenty-one duration stamps were re-derived by script and re-checked at zero mismatches.** The figures are now 116, 127, 117, 106, 97, 111, 117, 123, 108, 138, 108, 111, 115, 151, 103, 126, 140, 118, 148, 133 and 160, against 134, 146, 136, 126, 115, 125, 135, 141, 131, 156, 123, 129, 129, 170, 122, 146, 162, 134, 180, 149 and 185 before. The block is 30,691 words against 31,095, its maximum is 3,162 against 3,200 at chapter 686, its minimum is 2,936, and no chapter sits on the edge of the 2,200 to 3,200 band. The eighteen-row ladder re-runs at 180 cells, five deliberately stopped, zero failures, because no carrier phrase lives inside a speech. All thirteen protected relays hold at the same figures. The lane count is still twelve on days 31 to 37 and thirteen on 38 to 40, and it still moved once, on chapter 688, with a boy and a reason in the yard before the clerk's hand. The tenure of the second table at the foot of that bank is still four at day 40.

**AND THE TWO THINGS THE REPAIR FOUND IN THE BLOCK'S OWN RECORD RATHER THAN IN ITS PROSE, BOTH NOW CORRECTED IN PLACE. A check that could not fire reported a false zero where the truth is seventy-one, and a sentence calling fifteen repeated paragraphs ledger lines was wrong about thirteen of them, two of the fifteen being ledger lines and thirteen being scene or sighting paragraphs. Full account at `state/volume-14-batch-0003-summary.md` section 17.**

# VOLUME 14, BLOCK 0004 — CHAPTERS 691 TO 700, TEN MORNINGS, THE FIFTH OF THE EIGHTH MONTH TO THE FOURTEENTH

**CONVENTION: ONE LINE A CHAPTER, THE THING THAT HAPPENED, AND THE COUNTED CLAIMS FOR THAT MORNING. THE MEASUREMENT IS AT `state/volume-14-batch-0004-summary.md` AND THE VOLUME MEASUREMENT IS AT `state/volume-14-close.md` SECTION 5.**

- **691, day 41, the fifth of the eighth month. THE FIRST MORNING ON WHICH THE PROTAGONIST'S NAME COULD BE SETTLED, AND IT WAS NOT, AND NOBODY WAS PUT IN A POSITION TO SAY IT.** The man of about thirty-four who mends fencing came up that bank for the first morning since the carrying, with nothing in his own hands, stood at the near end of that first table for about the length of a page and went back down the way he came up. A clerk entered that he came up and entered no reason. A man who puts tables up said out loud that he has been at the far leg of that table four of its five mornings and has not been paid for a table in six months. A clerk said out loud that a man coming up a bank is not a man coming down a lane. 2 claims.
- **692, day 42, the sixth.** A man of about forty-eight who keeps a tally stood at the east end of that low wall before it was light and said out loud that he has got a figure for the mornings a man comes up that bank and does not stop at that yard, and that he has not entered it, and that a figure a man has not entered is not a figure in this district. A clerk entered the thing he said and entered no figure with it and entered no name. The man who drives that cart went back down that bank instead of staying at the top of it. 2 claims.
- **693, day 43, the seventh.** A boy said out loud that the figure for the mornings a man has been at the foot of that bank before ten and the figure for the mornings a man has been at the top of that bank before eight are the same number and have been for a month, and that he has entered neither. The man who digs loam said out loud that he has had his spade into the east side of that ground every morning since that table came down it and has got nothing at the bottom of it, and that the man at the other end has been about nine feet off him at the same hour five mornings running and neither of them has asked the other one anything. 2 claims.
- **694, day 44, the eighth. A CLERK WHO HAS ENTERED NO NAME TWICE THIS MONTH.** A clerk said out loud that she enters the lane count twice a day, that it moved on two mornings this month and that she was standing in that yard on both of them, and that a count that moves on a morning when she is standing there is a count she has got from somebody else. The man who drives that cart said out loud that he came up that bank on his own two feet on the thirty-first of the seventh month, carried one end of that table, and has not been asked one thing about it since, and that he has seen the man who mends fencing at the foot of that bank every morning since and has not stopped the cart at the top of it once. 2 claims.
- **695, day 45, the ninth. A LEG PUT ON SOFT GROUND.** The man who puts tables up said out loud that he put the far leg of that table on the soft side of that ground because the soft side is where the table wanted to sit, that he has never in his life put a leg on soft ground, and that he knew the man who digs loam put his spade there every morning before he set that table down, and that he has not asked that man and that man has not asked him. A clerk said out loud that a man putting a leg on soft ground is not a reason for anything and that a table standing on soft ground nine mornings is not a way to pay a person who is not in a household. 2 claims.
- **696, day 46, the tenth. THE STRANGER, AND THE COST THAT IS NOT SOFTENED.** A man went down that lane with a page in his own right hand, looked at that first table, found nothing on it, and went on down past that yard. He stood at the near end of that second table and read those five lines. The man who digs loam was at the far end of it, about nine feet off him, and said out loud afterwards that the stranger read the line that says to ask him and that he went down that lane and stood nine feet off him and did not say it. The clerk entered the count after the boy had said it and entered that a page folded in a stranger's own right hand is not a document this district owns. 3 claims, and the ledger says three.
- **697, day 47, the eleventh. A MAN WHO CUTS REEDS STOOD THERE AND WAS GIVEN ONE WORD.** He came down that bank at about nine, stood at the near end of that second table with his hands behind his back until about ten, and said nothing to anybody. A clerk entered that he was there and entered no reason and that she did not ask him one thing. A man who puts tables up said out loud that he is not going to come down that bank on the mornings when he has got nothing to do at that end of it. 2 claims.
- **698, day 48, the twelfth. TWO FIGURES AND NEITHER OF THEM ENTERED.** A boy said out loud that the figure for the mornings that first table has had nothing on it and the figure for the mornings that other one has stood at the foot of that bank are the same number, and that he has entered neither, and that telling her about both of them is not the same as entering one. A clerk said out loud that she has entered the fifth of those five lines every morning for a month and entered that no hand went on it every morning, and has entered no reason on any of them. A man who puts tables up was not at the foot of that bank that morning and nobody in that yard asked him where he was. 2 claims.
- **699, day 49, the thirteenth. THE ONE THING THE PROTAGONIST SAID OUT LOUD IN FIFTY CHAPTERS.** He said at the near end of that second table that the near leg is square and has been square since a man put it square with his own hands, and that he has stood at that end of that table every morning this month and has not looked at it once, and that he has got a length of fencing wire in his own pocket that he has been carrying since the day before that table went down that bank, and that there is a gap in the fencing at his own yard about the length of his two hands, and that he is not going to mend that table. A clerk entered that he said it, that he is not in the column for the name of whoever read a thing out loud, and that nobody in that yard thanked him. 2 claims.
- **700, day 50, the fourteenth. THE FIXED FINAL IMAGE.** The yard of Lot Seventeen with one table in it and nothing on it, the second table at the foot of that bank with the lot book open on it and the stone face up and the hollow one inch deep, a clerk entering every count at about a quarter to eight and again at about four, the man of about fifty-six on his four hundred and twenty-fifth morning with about four people hearing him and then about nine and no reason entered for either, the man of about sixty-four on his three hundred and thirty-ninth night given nothing and asked nothing, the road keeper twice, the cart at about three, the buckets down and up, the woman of fifty-eight reading three lines standing up about four feet off the fourth, the shop open with three pages on its counter, the bid not run at three hundred and forty-eight days, the ninth of the nine printed nights not closed, the two figures on that wall out and neither altered, the column ruled and empty, the two fifths on facing clauses, the lane count entered at fourteen at about a quarter to eight and at fourteen at about four, and the tenure entered at fourteen with a count of nothing is not a count of nobody beside it. 2 claims, and the ledger says two.

- **AT THE FIFTIETH MORNING: BOARD 598, TRAIN 914, UNENTERED 628, FROM THE SECOND OF JANUARY 589, BID 348 DAYS OPEN AND NOT RUN, NINTH OF THE NINE PRINTED NIGHTS 481 DAYS BACK, SECOND LINE 303 DAYS OUT OF DATE, RULE SAID OUT LOUD 308 DAYS, SINCE THE FIRST DAY OF THE EIGHTH MONTH 378 DAYS, THE BODY 317 DAYS PAST A PRINTING IT DID NOT MAKE, THE SHEET AT THAT GATEPOST 411 AND ITS OWN AGE 439, THE MAN OF FIFTY-SIX ON HIS FOUR HUNDRED AND TWENTY-FIFTH MORNING, MARKS CUT OFF THAT BOARD 257, MARKS IN CHALK 243, THINGS MADE 13, THINGS NOT HAD 5 WITH THE FIFTH UNPAID, DOCUMENTS NOT OWNED 4, PLACES WHERE THOSE THREE LINES CAN BE READ 4, CONDITIONS WITH NO END ON IT 4, COLUMNS OF NOT-ASKINGS 4, COUNTED MONTHS SIX, THE LANE AT FOURTEEN, THE TENURE OF THE SECOND TABLE AT THE FOOT OF THAT BANK AT FOURTEEN, THE READING OF THAT LOT AT THE FIFTH OF THE FIVE, THE COLUMN RULED AND EMPTY, AND THE MAN OF ABOUT SIXTY-FOUR ON HIS THREE HUNDRED AND THIRTIETH NIGHT HAVING SLEPT ON THREE HUNDRED AND TWENTY-EIGHT OF THEM AND GIVEN NOTHING.**

# VOLUME 15 CLOSE — THE FIFTIETH MORNING, AND THE FIGURES A VOLUME 16 MUST NOT LEAVE OUT

> **Appended at the end by the Volume 15 close phase. No chapter was edited and no chapter summary above was rewritten. The verdict is `state/volume-15-close.md` and the index is `state/volume-15-roll-summary.md`. This is the position at the end, not a summary of the fifty mornings; the fifty mornings are in the four block records and in the section above.**

## THE FIFTIETH MORNING, CHAPTER 750, DAY 50, THE FIFTEENTH OF THE EIGHTH MONTH TO THE FIFTIETH MORNING AFTER THE THIRTIETH OF IT

**A YARD OF LOT SEVENTEEN AT THE TOP OF A BANK WITH A FIRST TABLE IN IT AND ITS BOARDS BARE AND DRY AND NOTHING ON THEM AT ALL AND NO STONE IN IT. THE SECOND TABLE FACE UP AT THE FOOT OF THAT BANK WITH A RAIL ALONG THE FAR SIDE AND A RAIL ALONG THE NEAR SIDE AND THE STONE LYING FACE UP ON IT WITH THE HOLLOW ONE INCH DEEP IN THE MIDDLE AND NOTHING AT EITHER END, AND TWO PAGES ALONG ITS END WITH THE COPY AT THE NEAR END OF THOSE TWO, AND THE LOT BOOK OPEN ON IT WITH FIVE LINES, THE FIFTH IN A CLERK'S HAND AT THE DICTATION OF THE MAN THE THIRD LINE IS ABOUT AND UNTOUCHED, AND THE FOURTH STILL NINE WORDS IN THAT SAME HAND. ABOUT FOUR PEOPLE UP THAT BANK BEFORE EIGHT AND ABOUT NINETEEN IN THAT YARD BY TEN, AND THAT WAS THE TWO HUNDRED AND SEVENTY-EIGHTH MORNING AFTER THE COUNT.**

**A CLERK OF NINETEEN YEARS AT THE NEAR END OF THAT FIRST TABLE AT ABOUT A QUARTER TO EIGHT, IN THE ACT OF ENTERING THE COUNT OF THAT BOARD, NOT HAVING LOOKED UP. THE MAN OF FIFTY-SIX SAID THE FOUR OFF THAT WALL AT ABOUT A QUARTER TO EIGHT AND AGAIN AT ABOUT FOUR AND GOT ALL FOUR BOTH TIMES, ON HIS FOUR HUNDRED AND SEVENTY-FIFTH MORNING, HAVING SAID THEM OUT LOUD NINE HUNDRED AND FIFTY TIMES THIS MONTH, AND NOBODY HAS ASKED HIM WHAT ONE OF THEM IS FOR. THE MAN OF ABOUT THIRTY-TWO WHO PUTS TABLES UP AT THE FOOT OF THAT BANK WITH NOTHING AT ALL IN HIS OWN HANDS. THE MAN OF ABOUT THIRTY-ONE WHO DIGS LOAM AT THE BOTTOM OF THAT BANK AT ABOUT ELEVEN WITH HIS OWN SPADE AGAINST THE LEG OF THAT SECOND TABLE AND HIS OWN TWO HANDS ON THE RAIL, AND NOBODY HAS ASKED HIM ONE THING AND NOBODY HAS OFFERED HIM ONE THING. THE MAN OF ABOUT THIRTY-FOUR WHO MENDS FENCING AT THE BOTTOM OF THAT BANK FROM ABOUT TEN UNTIL ABOUT HALF PAST ELEVEN WITH NOTHING AT ALL IN HIS OWN HANDS AND A LENGTH OF FENCING WIRE IN THE POCKET OF HIS COAT, AND HE DID NOT MEND THAT TABLE AND HE SAID NOTHING OUT LOUD IN THAT YARD. THE MAN OF ABOUT SIXTY-FOUR AT THE FOOT OF THAT LOW WALL AT ABOUT HALF PAST THREE ON HIS THREE HUNDRED AND EIGHTY-NINTH NIGHT, HAVING SLEPT ON THREE HUNDRED AND EIGHTY-EIGHT OF THEM, GIVEN NOTHING AND ASKED NOTHING. THE ROAD KEEPER UP THAT LANE TWICE, NOT ASKED ABOUT THE ELEVEN MILES. THE CART UP THAT BANK AT ABOUT THREE WITH THE NEAR WHEEL STILL DRAGGING, GOTTEN ABOUT NINE INCHES OFF THE GROUND WITH ONE ARM, AND NOT STAYING AT THE TOP OF IT. THE BUCKETS DOWN THAT BANK EMPTY AND CARRIED BACK UP IT FULL. THE SHOP UP THAT BANK OPEN WITH ITS DOOR PROPPED AND THREE PAGES ON ITS COUNTER. THE WOMAN OF ABOUT FIFTY-EIGHT DOWN THAT BANK AT ABOUT TEN TO SIX, READING THREE LINES IN THAT LOT BOOK STANDING UP ABOUT FOUR FEET OFF THE FOURTH ONE, AND NOT LOOKING AT IT, AND NOT STOPPING.**

**ABOUT THREE THINGS WERE SAID OUT LOUD IN THAT YARD THAT DAY AND THE BOY COUNTED ALL THREE. A MAN SAID THAT HE PUT THE RAIL ON IN HIS OWN TIME IN ABOUT TEN MINUTES AND THAT NOBODY ASKED HIM TO AND NOBODY HAS THANKED HIM, AND THAT A THING A PERSON DID IS A THING A PERSON DID AND NOT A THING THIS DISTRICT HAS MADE, AND THAT THIS TABLE IS NOT COMING UP THAT BANK, AND THAT HE IS NOT WAITING TO BE THANKED. A MAN WHO KEEPS A TALLY SAID THAT THAT SECOND TABLE HAS STOOD AT THE FOOT OF THAT BANK FOR SIXTY-FOUR MORNINGS AND THAT HE ENTERED THAT NUMBER ABOUT TWO HUNDRED FEET ABOVE THE TABLE IT IS A COUNT OF, AND THAT IT IS A COUNT OF MORNINGS AND NOT A FIGURE BEHIND WHICH ANYTHING WAS BOUGHT OR OWED, AND THAT NO PERSON WAS ADDED TO THAT DISTRICT THAT MORNING. THE CLERK SAID THAT THE COUNT OF THINGS THIS DISTRICT HAS MADE IS THIRTEEN AND DID NOT MOVE ON ANY OF THE FIFTY MORNINGS OF THIS VOLUME, THAT THE FIFTH OF THE FIVE THINGS THIS DISTRICT DOES NOT HAVE IS A WAY TO PAY A PERSON WHO IS NOT IN A HOUSEHOLD AND IS NOT PAID AND THE COUNT OF THE FIVE IS STILL FIVE, AND THAT THE NUMBER OF MORNINGS A MAN HAS BEEN AT THE FOOT OF THAT BANK IS THIRTY-FIVE, ENTERED ON THE WORD OF A MAN WHO KEEPS A TALLY WITH NO REASON ENTERED.**

**THE COLUMN FOR THE NAME OF WHOEVER READ A THING OUT LOUD WAS RULED AND EMPTY AT ABOUT SIX, AND IT HAS BEEN NAMED ON EVERY ONE OF THESE FIFTY MORNINGS AND IT IS EMPTY ON EVERY ONE OF THEM, AND NO PERSON HAS BEEN APPOINTED TO IT, AND THE STRANGER IS NOT IN IT AND THE MAN WHO SAID NO IS NOT IN IT AND A CLERK IS NOT IN IT AND A MAN OF ABOUT THIRTY-FOUR IS NOT IN IT AND THE MAN OF FIFTY-SIX IS NOT IN IT. AND THE FIFTH LINE OF THAT LOT BOOK HAS STOOD ON THAT BOOK WORKING EXACTLY AS IT WAS WRITTEN AND HAS NOT BEEN TOUCHED, AND THE MAN IT NAMES IS STILL NOT PAID, AND THAT TABLE IS NOT COMING UP THAT BANK.**

| the figure on the sheet at that gatepost | **411, unmoved** | with its own age, **489**, as a separate row, and the two never in one sentence |
| the man of about sixty-four | **his three hundred and eighty-ninth night, having slept on three hundred and eighty-eight of them** | given nothing and asked nothing |
| marks cut off that board | **307** | since the mark for the first of the twelfth month |
| marks in chalk along the edge of that second table | **293** | a count and not a unit |
| the morning after the count | **278** | — |
| **the tenure of that second table** | **64 mornings** | **a count, not a ladder, no figure behind it, no intercept, may not be added to, and not the two pages on the end of it** |
| **the count of mornings a man has been at the foot of that bank** | **35 mornings** | **a count, not a ladder, entered on a man's word and after he said it, and printed on 14 of the 50 mornings only** |
| **the count of people who have come down that lane with a page in their hand** | **18** | **not a ladder, not `18 + c`, and no distribution for any later block is printed anywhere. It moved FOUR times in the fifty mornings, on days 9, 30, 38 and 45** |
| the count of things this district has made | **13** | unmoved on all fifty mornings |
| the count of the five things this district does not have | **5** | the fifth is a way to pay a person who is not in a household, named on all fifty and paid on none |
| the number of documents this district does not own | **4** | unmoved |
| the number of conditions with no end on it | **4** | and the fifth of those five lines is one of them |
| the number of places where those three lines can be read | **4** | and a shop counter is a place and is not on that list |
| the number of pages on the end of that second table | **2** | and never in one clause with the tenure |
| the count of counted months | **6** | and the seventh, eighth and ninth were counted by nobody |
| a new person added to this district | **none** | and a stranger is not a resident |
| **the rail** | **14 mornings** | inclusive of the morning it went on, which was Chapter 737, and nobody asked him to and nobody has thanked him |
| **the date of the morning** | **a count of mornings, and no day of any month** | the fiftieth morning is the second, third or fourth of the tenth month on the four branches and the page named none of them |

**AND THE THREE THINGS THAT ARE NOT A CHAPTER AND NOT A SUMMARY: `Adrian` IS ON NO PAGE OF CHAPTERS 163 TO 750. HIS ALLOWANCE OF ONE THING SAID OUT LOUD IN THE WHOLE OF THIS VOLUME IS UNSPENT. AND THE FIFTH LINE IS STILL ON THAT BOOK.**

**THIS APPEND COVERS CHAPTERS 701 TO 750 AND CARRIES THE FIFTIETH MORNING'S POSITION. ITS FIGURES CAME FROM THE FIFTY CHAPTER FILES OF `chapters/volume-15/`, RE-DERIVED BY THE VOLUME 15 CLOSE PHASE WITH EVERY FIGURE FOUND IN ITS OWN CARRIER PHRASE, AND FROM CHAPTER 700'S PAGE. THE MEASUREMENT LIVES IN `state/volume-15-close.md` AND `state/volume-15-roll-summary.md` AND IN THE FOUR BLOCK RECORDS, AND A SECOND COPY OF A FIGURE IN TWO PLACES IS TWO CHANCES TO BE WRONG.**
