# VOLUME 16 — CHAPTERS 751 TO 800, BLOCK 0001, DAYS 1 TO 10

**APPENDED 2026-09-30 BY THE BATCH PHASE. `c = 0` IS CHAPTER 750. THE CONTRACT IS `outline/volume-16.md`. THE BATCH RECORD IS `state/volume-16-batch-0001-summary.md`. THE HOUSE REGISTER CHANGES IN THIS BLOCK, DELIBERATELY, PER `state/maintainer-escalation.md` ITEM 5.**

- **751, the thirty-fifth morning after the thirtieth of the eighth month.** The man who puts tables up is at the foot of that bank at ten past eight, earlier than he has ever come, and tells Adrian not to ask what the fifth line is for, because the day it is a sentence about a man it stops being a condition and becomes a fetch. Adrian does not ask it. Instead the clerk enters the question itself in the lot book — `WHAT IS THE FIFTH LINE FOR / NO ANSWER ENTERED` — and turns the page round so the yard can read it, and nobody strikes it. Adrian says out loud that he is thirty-eight and not what the yard guessed. **The road keeper stops for the first time in six years and asks Adrian how long the bid is open, because he has eleven miles of road to plan and cannot plan for a thing with no end on it.**
- **752, the thirty-sixth.** Adrian asks the woman of fifty-eight who wrote the fourth line and finds she said nine words, a man dictated them, and the clerk wrote them fifteen years ago. Adrian uses the clerk's name for the first time and is told to say it again when he means it; he tells her she is thirty-four and not nineteen and that the nineteen has been eating her own age since she wrote the rule that stops her correcting it. Orren is seventeen, not nineteen, and Adrian learns that the sheet the boy carries has a fourth column headed *who was standing next to them*. He asks for it and is refused: *you'd be reading it in a room by yourself and calling it a public record.*
- **753, the thirty-seventh.** A stranger comes down that lane at a quarter to nine with a page in his right hand and nothing in his left, lays it face down on the second table and goes back up without a word. The lane count moves eighteen to nineteen, entered after the boy says it out loud at about ten and not before, with no name and a note that a stranger is not a resident. The page is a notice from the First House convening a sitting on the emergency clause of the Common Measure, and it asks for one name, one figure of pay and one term. The corner carries four hundred and eleven, which is the figure on the sheet at a gatepost forty miles away. The clerk has asked in writing three times whether that figure is a count of people or a count of sheets and was told *the count is a count*. Orren has seen the worn corner mark before, in a corridor at Brass Verdict Hall, eleven years ago.
- **754, the thirty-eighth.** The nine terms are read out at about eleven. The shopkeeper refuses for a reason about a woman who comes down that lane once a week. Adrian puts his own name near the sentence and the yard goes quiet. **The clerk states the whole of the block's problem out loud: the reason this district cannot answer the page is the fifth of the five things it does not have, a way to pay a person who is not in a household.** A Tally panel on the reverse of the notice says that a lot has seven parts and that where a man is sent instead of an object the six that can be filled in do not make a lot. **The man of fifty-six is asked what a term is and answers: the bit where somebody stops. Then he says that one of the four figures is doing something he cannot describe and that he cannot tell you which, and refuses to guess in a yard.** Seth asks Adrian for his name and Adrian does not give it.
- **755, the thirty-ninth.** Adrian says it out loud at about eleven in a yard of about nineteen people — *Adrian Vale* — after saying why he stopped sixteen years ago, which is a charter with his name at the head of it that he could not take off. The man of fifty-six says it back twice. **The clerk fills the column for the name of whoever read a thing out loud, which has been ruled and empty since the book was opened, and enters that she was talked into it.** Orren asks what goes in the second column.
- **756, the fortieth.** Orren explains the fourth column: a figure on a wall is a figure about nobody, and what makes a figure worth something is who heard it and who was standing next to the man who said it. He refuses to give Adrian the sheet and refuses to let him publish his row, because a public row turns a man in a yard into a man performing being a man in a yard. His mother's row says *the household*: Sela Pike, a bridge charter sixteen years ago, a toll assigned to the household, a letter saying she was the household, and then nothing. He says the list of about nineteen people is the only thing he has, and that one of them is going to have a word where a person should be.
- **757, the forty-first.** The man who puts tables up is not there and the yard runs on it. Adrian tries to write four lines saying what Lot Seventeen is and cannot, because there is no second person in the business. **Four people draw a map on the back of the stranger's page over two mornings and there is a four-inch hole in it, because none of them is ever at the bottom of that bank at eleven o'clock except one man with a spade.** The count of things this district has made moves thirteen to fourteen and a reason is entered for the first time in fifteen years. The shopkeeper says *ask me about the three pages on my counter*.
- **758, the forty-second.** Three pages on a shop counter: one for a woman who comes once a week, one in the back, one under the till that came down the lane in a coat with somebody else's name inside it. The shopkeeper argues the count of places where the three lines can be read must stay at four and is right. The man the fifth line names says it is five and then says no, and asks them to keep it at four because the counter is the only safe thing this district has. **Seth comes back from Alder Reach with Room Four, and a boy in a corridor, and the line that they bind about four hundred conditions a week and that a name only has to be written down. And he says the clerk wrote the fifth line.** She admits it: written by her, at his dictation, on an afternoon fifteen years ago, when she was nineteen, and she did not know anybody would read it.
- **759, the forty-third.** Adrian tries to end the fifth line and Orren stops him. The man who digs loam explains that he wants to be *findable*, not fetched, and that removing the line would make him nothing rather than something that can be got hold of. He has said no once in fifteen years and will not say to what. **He gives Adrian his name at the foot of that bank, in front of four people, and Adrian does not write it down.** The clerk is told afterwards and enters that she did not see it, with a reason. Orren notices the near miss in his own list and refuses to write it down and walks away.
- **760, the forty-fourth.** Adrian writes the answer and shows it to four people before he lets anybody have it. The clerk catches him before he puts a figure on it and makes him write out that the district can send a person and not a promise. The woman of fifty-eight pays the nine-copper plant and Adrian offers to ask the question she will not ask herself. **Orren's proposal is not a column but a rule: any person may write on any paper they are carrying who was standing near them, and no such paper is to be entered in the book by anybody including the clerk.** A page goes up that lane at half past four in a cart with a dragging wheel, and the lane count does not move. A stranger comes down it at about five with the only copy of a note a man took in a corridor in Room Four, and asks for it to be read out in that yard by somebody with no interest in it.

# VOLUME 16, BLOCK 0001 — CHAPTER SUMMARIES CORRECTED BY THE REVIEW FIX OF 2026-09-30

**THE TEN SUMMARIES ABOVE ARE CORRECTED HERE RATHER THAN REWRITTEN, AND ONLY WHERE THE REVIEW FOUND THEM WRONG. NOTHING ELSE IN THEM MOVED.**

- **754.** ADDED: after Adrian puts his own name near the sentence about standing in a room and the clerk says no, **the woman of fifty-eight tells him in the yard that saying it out loud has taken away the district's only remaining answer — *we have nobody* — and that from here on, sending nobody up that lane is a question that will be asked of him personally. He has no answer and he writes nothing down.** ALSO: the man of about sixty-four's three hundred and ninety-third night is now printed here, which it was not.
- **756.** ADDED: **Adrian goes down the bank and asks the man of about twenty-nine who drives the cart to stop so that a page can be written down, which is the only thing the boy asked for all week. The man refuses, and says that stopping is the one thing he cannot do, because a man standing still is a man somebody chose to put in a place, and he asks Adrian to tell the next stranger down that lane that he was there and said nothing. Orren's answer is that a man refusing to be on a page is a man on a page, he just does not know which column, and he puts it on Adrian.** The sixty-four-year-old's night is now printed once in this chapter, not three times.
- **757.** **THE WHOLE CHAPTER IS NOW THE FORTY-FIRST MORNING AND NOTHING IN IT HAPPENS ON THE FORTY-SECOND.** The map, the argument, the hole and the clerk's entry all run between about eleven and about half past three on the forty-first, and **the count of things this district has made moves thirteen to fourteen at about half past three on the forty-first, not on the forty-second.** The two-day retrospective digest is gone and the argument is a scene. The week-day name is cut.
- **759.** **THE NAME IS NO LONGER PRINTED IN THE NARRATION AND THE OPENING IS ADRIAN'S.** Orren's colon now delivers his line. The sixty-four-year-old paragraph is a coda at about half past three, which is where it belongs in the clock. *The way Seth had* is now the thirty-seventh morning, not the fortieth.
- **760.** The narration's *fifteen volumes* is now *fifteen years*, and the marks-and-chalk block is no longer printed twice.

- **755.** The man who digs loam has *said one word in that yard yesterday and nothing at all this morning*, not four mornings of silence, because he spoke at the end of the thirty-eighth. **The near rail's nineteenth morning is printed once, the table's sixty-ninth is printed once, and the man's own four hundred and eightieth is printed.**

# VOLUME 16, BLOCK 0002 — CHAPTERS 761 TO 770, THE FORTY-FIFTH THROUGH THE FIFTIETH-FOURTH MORNING AFTER THE THIRTIETH OF THE EIGHTH MONTH

- **761, the forty-fifth.** A page went up that lane yesterday and four people look up the lane before eight and none of them mentions it. **The rule of the forty-fourth morning is read out at about four by the man of fifty-six, who asks a question about it first and is given a line: a yard is a person, or more than one, standing where a thing can be heard by a person who did not say it.** The first person to use the rule is the woman of fifty-eight, who writes on the back of a page in her bag who was standing near the man who gave his name on the forty-third morning. **The fourth column is empty and the boy says why: he was nine feet above that bank and forty feet off, watching Adrian's hands.** The clerk enters that a person said a thing and that a second person said one word, and enters no name, and enters no word. The cart comes up late and does not stop.
- **762, the forty-sixth.** The man at the far end of the second table puts his spade down flat for the first time and walks to the low wall, and refuses Adrian the asking: *you gave me the right to say no before you asked on the forty-third morning and I am not going to spend that.* Adrian puts his pencil on the boards where it can be seen. **The man of about sixty-four answers: he goes out at about half past four and is back at first light, there is a turn and a culvert and about two miles of nothing and then a building, he does not know what is in it, he is paid nothing, he has been asked nothing in nine years, he has no line on any page, and he is not owed anything — the only reason the other man is standing at that table is that somebody with a pen wrote his down fifteen years ago.** He asks for one thing: that it not be written down. **The clerk writes it in the back of her own book on a page with nothing ruled on it, and enters that she did it against his wishes and would do it again, and that the lot book has five lines and no line for a man who is owed something by a person he cannot name.** For the first time in four hundred and one nights there are two men on that wall at about four, forty feet apart, and the man of fifty-six has never once looked to his right in nine years.
- **763, the forty-seventh.** **The boy has written the word *the man* in the first column of one of his own rows, about two years ago, because a blank is worse.** He tells Adrian on the boards and refuses to say whose row it is, because the man would tell him and the trouble is that the man would tell him. He goes up the bank and asks for the yard and announces the defect in front of about nineteen people without naming the row. The man at the end of the table says *aye*, once, and does not move. He comes down afterwards and says he wrote *the man* and that they are the same instrument twice, and tells the boy not to come and apologise. **The boy asks Adrian for the one thing he asked for on the fortieth morning: next time somebody comes down that lane with a page, stand in the yard and say nothing at all.** Adrian writes the request on his own paper and does not give it to him. The wire comes up at the door and is neither explained nor written down.
- **764, the forty-eighth.** **Tarin Kest, forty-three, keeper of every road in the basin, comes down that lane with a waybill and says Lot Seventeen is not on his sheet and neither is the bank, and that the turner who reads the wall twice a day is not one of his and never has been.** The lane count moves nineteen to twenty after the boy says it out loud, and a name goes in beside it for the first time in fifteen years and the man asked for it. **He says the eleventh mile is the one that fails: a turn, a culvert, a mile and a half with nothing on either side, and everything else on that run has somewhere to put a thing that went missing, and a thing lost on a road with nothing on it is not lost, it is finished. Eleven bundles in nine years, none reported, and he asked about one of them two years ago and stopped asking.** Adrian says the failure is in the room and Kest says the room is where pages are made. **The clerk then works out that her own rule of the forty-fourth morning forbids entering the only paper in this district that could find a thing lost on that mile, and says so in a yard, and says she did not think of it until a man said it.** Adrian tells Kest the cart driver's message on a road at about half past three without having asked him, and understands it about nine seconds too late. Kest writes a carrier's entry: a road, a direction, an hour and a fact about a wheel. *It does not say a man was there.*
- **765, the forty-ninth.** Adrian stands on the boards with his hands at his sides from about half past two because a boy of seventeen asked him to. **The cart comes up at about three and stops four feet below the top of the bank for the first time in nine years with a page in its driver's hand. The boy says twenty-one before the clerk can move, and the man says the rule is the rule and that he is not going to tell a yard that a boy of seventeen is wrong about arithmetic.** He then says one sentence: that Adrian told a stranger about him without asking, that the no is still the no, and that he was not stopped because he was asked to. **The page is the district's own letter, returned, with four lines on the back: a figure of forty coppers for one year, a term of one year, and the two words *as sent*.** The clerk turns it over without unfolding the second fold. The boy reads *as sent* out because Adrian refuses to. **The man at the end of the second table picks the lot book up and reads the fifth line out loud in a yard for the first time in fifteen years, and says the district's one unpaid man is the reason the district cannot send anybody, and that he is not the bearer, and that the name in the column is.**
- **766, the fiftieth.** **Nothing is on at ten for the first time in ten mornings because a boy of seventeen arranged it, going round nine people separately.** Adrian asks the woman of fifty-eight and tells her what he will do with the answer before she gives it. **The answer is a name: Nell Cray.** She went to Alder Reach four months back and came back and said *I've been* to a step, and the woman of fifty-eight did not ask, and has not asked since, and that has gone bad in the bag with the rest of it. *I have made her the person I do not say things to, and I have been proud of that for nine years.* The name is not in the lot book, not in the clerk's page, and on no page in the district. **Then the boy asks the man of fifty-six, in a yard, for the thing the man asked for on the forty-fourth morning, and gets it: the man says he wants nothing to happen, that a yard with a subject is a yard where the man being looked into cannot leave, and that he will be frightened for about a year. The boy writes it in the margin of a page that is not a record of anybody and reads it out.**
- **767, the fifty-first.** **The district must be able to name a figure and the only man who can is the man who keeps a tally, so Adrian goes and asks him, and says no for eleven seconds and then says yes and asks for the order to be done properly.** Seth Arlow says that a figure is not a payment until a market has said out loud that a person can be put in a room for a year and be found again afterwards. He says it at about eleven: forty coppers a year, and the term ends the day before the first sitting following and starts again only by fresh consent of the persons this yard represents. **About eleven people then thank him for the rail, unprompted, and he does not say one word for about eleven seconds.** The boy does not count it, for the first time in about four years. Seth works out that the rail is now a thing the district has made and that the count of fourteen is not going to move, and that being thanked is the only thing in this basin that attaches a number to a man without anybody writing a word. **The man of fifty-six, who said nothing for three and a half hours, spends the only instrument he has: nobody is to thank that man again, on pain of four figures read aloud in the ordinary voice. Nobody thanked him either.** The clerk enters four lines and says she wants a reason for the thanking and cannot write one, and enters the want instead, and asks whether that is allowed.
- **768, the fifty-second.** **A term is the bit where somebody stops, and the only thing in this district that can be stopped is the fifth line, and the man in it is the term.** Adrian goes to the far end of the table, which is four feet further on than where he stops, and says the whole of the trade before he gets an answer. The man tells him the fifth of the five is not money and not a form and is *a shortage of a line*, and that if somebody had written a line down fifteen years ago with a figure on it, the money would have been the easy half. Then he says no. **And this time he says yes to being written down, and asks the clerk to come down and take it, and gives the reason: a word that says *and the man* is a door in about nine rooms in Alder Reach, and you cannot close a door by standing in front of it being careful, because he spent nine years being careful in a yard, which is the one place careful does not work. A line that says a man was asked for an end and said no is a fact about a person, and a fact about a person can be asked about.** It goes in the margin and not on a line, and the count of the five stays five. **The woman of fifty-eight comes down the bank, which she does not do, and says it is not an answer, and that it is the first thing this district has had in fifteen years that is not an answer, and then she names the price: he is findable from this morning and he chose it.**
- **769, the fifty-third.** **A boy of seventeen asks a yard of about nineteen people who in that district has no interest in a page, and about forty seconds later the yard answers at once.** The woman who keeps a scale says yes, on three conditions, the third of which is that she is not going to be half in it. **She reads four lines out loud at about one and gets all of them and the yard is very quiet, because the fourth line is *Nell Cray, working list forty-one, ask the market that sent her and you will be told she was never there*.** The woman of fifty-eight was at the top of the bank four seconds before that line and says *read that one again*, and it is said twice in a yard and never a third time. The clerk enters that a note was read out and enters no name of the reader, and gives her reason: the column is for a thing this district reads out of its own wall, twice a morning, for years, and there has been one such man in nine years. **Orren Pike says the heading says *whoever read a thing out loud*, and that she is one and is not on a page, and the clerk says she has known it since a quarter to eight, and nobody does anything.** The reader's own sentence: *a thing weighed is a thing known, and it is worth more than everything else in this yard put together, and not because the weight is right, but because afterwards two people know it and neither of them has to trust the other one.* The page goes back up the lane in a cart at about half past four, and the woman of fifty-eight goes up the lane at about ten to six, alone, and tells the yard in advance that she is going and will not be grateful.
- **770, the fifty-fourth.** **The figure that has been going round that yard about Adrian's mornings is fifty, in nineteen mouths, and it is in no book, and nobody has ever checked it.** The boy checks it at about a quarter to four and says seventy, and says he is not going to take fifty off, because *fifty is not wrong, fifty is what was said, seventy is what there is.* **The man of fifty-six reads the four figures and then asks the boy to say the fifth one out loud so that he can read one too, and says that two figures about one person in a morning is the first time in his life he has not known what to do.** The clerk enters both and enters the old one at the old value and strikes nothing, and enters that one is a thing somebody said and one is a thing that happened. **The woman of fifty-eight comes back up the lane and says the woman she carries for was not angry and said *that is what not asking is for*, and then gives her own name in a yard — Mavis Dorr — and says the only protection anybody in this basin has had is being the one nobody can look up, and that she has been that for nine years by accident and it cost somebody four months of her life.** The clerk enters it on the page with nothing ruled on it. **Kest offers a place on a run over the eleventh mile and says the three words bed, wage and office out loud so that Adrian cannot say afterwards he was offered one.** Adrian reads out the folded paper the boy made him write. He takes the wire out, looks at it for about four seconds and puts it back. The books shut at six.

# VOLUME 16, BLOCK 0003 — CHAPTERS 771 TO 780, THE FIFTY-FIFTH THROUGH THE SIXTY-FOURTH MORNING AFTER THE THIRTIETH OF THE EIGHTH MONTH

> **THE BLOCK RECORD IS `state/volume-16-batch-0003-summary.md`. THE FIGURES WERE MEASURED CELL BY CELL IN EACH ROW'S OWN CARRIER PHRASE BY A CHECKER WRITTEN BEFORE THE PROSE: ZERO LADDER FAILURES, ROW 12 CONSTANT ON TEN OF TEN, ROW 18 ABSENT ON TEN OF TEN, ROWS 11 AND 12 SPLIT ON TEN OF TEN, ZERO DUPLICATED PARAGRAPHS, ONE DUPLICATED SENTENCE AND IT IS A CARRIER, 421 CROSS-FILE TWELVE-WORD RUNS, 36,999 WORDS OVER TEN FILES.**

- **771, the fifty-fifth.** **The boy counts a man for the second time in his life and gets seventy-six.** He says out loud in a yard that his own method was a box with a hole in it: he had been counting mornings he stood on the boards, and there are six mornings he was in the yard and not on them, one of them behind a counter holding a stranger's page flat. **The man of fifty-six refuses to read the figure** and says he has been frightened for about a year and is not going to be asked twice inside a fortnight, and adds that he did not say *no*. Adrian goes down the bank and asks the man at the end of the second table what instrument this district has for asking about nineteen people whether they consent to a year, and is told there is one and that he has been using it for a fortnight and does not like the shape of it. The clerk tells him a thing said in a yard at about eleven by a man is not going in her book.
- **772, the fifty-sixth.** **A man of sixty-one comes up that lane at about half past two with a leather case and no page in his hand, and the lane count does not move because of it.** He opens by naming the parties, the term and what he is taking. Nobody stops him, which has become a rule in that yard in about a week. Adrian does not recognise his face; he recognises the way the man said *lot*. **He puts his own name into the yard before anybody asks him for it.** He says he has not come to run anything or close anything; he says he holds an obligation with no end on it and would like to be asked about it in a yard by somebody who is not him. The clerk enters one line. He asks for the end of the second table and is refused and says he will sit on the stones. **The turner came through at about ten and did not stop at the wall.**
- **773, the fifty-seventh.** **He gives the clerk four things to enter and she enters two.** He says there is a clause, that it is held by a person, that it has no end on it, and — last, and having given her a chance to stop him at the first — that he wrote it. She enters that a man said those things and will not enter that it is held, because a clause that is held is a document this district does not own and a clause with no end on it would make her a woman who has entered a fifth condition into a district that has four. **He says he will send her one page she owns, in three or four days, and it has not come.** He goes down the bank at about half past three and sits four feet off a man of about sixty-four and is told *you will be the one that's been in the room — then I have nothing to say to you and I have said it.* They say nothing else that afternoon. He tells Adrian the name of the thing he wrote: **the Common Measure, to stop anybody owning a bus, a lamp, a ferry, a piece of road or a bell.** He stops before the rest of it. The man at the end of the second table tells Adrian that what the man wants is not a question but a hearing.
- **774, the fifty-eighth.** **THE NAMED COST OF THIS BLOCK, AND IT IS NOT ADRIAN.** Adrian asks the woman of fifty-eight to go into about nineteen kitchens. She tells him the price before she says yes: a thing said in a yard goes into about nine mouths and about nine people can be asked afterwards, and six people in this yard were findable that morning who had not been, and one of them is her, and she then names nine more, of whom one cannot be found by anybody who comes up that lane. **She names nine holders of the district's nine coppers out loud in a yard, and the ninth is Nell Cray, two miles away, on working list forty-one in a corridor nine miles up that hill, who has been said out loud three times in ten mornings and is on no page and cannot be found by anybody who comes up that lane.** She says *I am not a market, I am a list* in her own voice before anything else, because that is the only difference there is between a woman who is used and a woman who is doing it. The clerk enters no name and enters that she is entering a want against nobody but herself. **The cart driver says no.** He will take a man's page up that lane; he will not take the market's, because a figure needs a man and the man would be him and there is not room in the column for a second name. The page goes on the boards of the second table with a stone on the corner of it and is still there.
- **775, the fifty-ninth.** **THE MIDPOINT.** At about half past three, at the foot of a low wall, with a man of about sixty-four on the stones four feet off, a man of sixty-one tells a man of thirty-eight the truth about the Long Fracture. He wrote the Common Measure in about nine months to stop anybody owning a bus, a lamp, a ferry, a piece of road or a bell. It needed two fresh independent witnesses and a term, and he wrote both safeguards in himself in the ninth month. The close came with about nine hundred people in a hall and he could not find two he trusted more than himself and did not look long, **and he asks for the looking to be examined rather than the holding.** He took it into himself and left it open until there was somebody better; there has not been one in seventeen years and he has looked twice a year and speaks to about nine thousand people about a thing most of them have never heard of. The Measure sat open about eleven weeks with him in the gap where the witnesses should have been and the rights went over with nothing holding them. **That is the Long Fracture: not a machine, not a judgement, a settlement that did not close with one man in the hole in it.** He names one thing the gap does not excuse — in the ninth year he put his name on the roll of a ward market and about four hundred people went without bread the winter after — and refuses twice to be absolved, and tells Adrian that in about four years Adrian will be in a position to be the only one and will not be able to ask the yard. **In the middle of it Adrian says *then give it to a market* in the voice he uses for a good idea, and the man on the stones, who has never been asked anything in his life, says *Sit down*, and Adrian sits down.**
- **776, the sixtieth.** **The clerk does not enter any of the confession and says why: nine people heard it, one of them is seventeen, and in about four years somebody in a room will ask that boy.** She tells Adrian, on the lane, that a thing you did to somebody is not a thing about you, and that she has been getting that wrong since the fortieth morning and is nearly over. The woman who keeps a scale goes to the boards and says the district has been asking each other what instrument it has and has been told twice that the answer is a yard, and that the first thing the yard is used for is her. **The clerk asks the man of fifty-six for the column. He asks first what happens to the next one and is told nobody knows. He says yes anyway, on a condition: a rule about who may be entered, made out loud in a yard in about nineteen people's hearing, before anybody is refused, and not made afterwards, because a rule written after a refusal is a rule about that person.** She enters the condition before the answer because that is the order it happened in. He says the frightening part is not being asked; it is being the only thing in the yard that anybody is sure of.
- **777, the sixty-first.** **The keeper of every road in this basin would not accept a concession until Adrian said what followed from it.** Adrian concedes the cause — a thing is lost on the eleventh mile because there is nothing in this basin that can find it — and says that a carrier's entry is the only instrument here that can put a fact about a road into a book without a man's name at the top of it. **The room is not conceded and the line this summary used to put in Kest's mouth was never on a page: what Chapter 777 has, in the man at the end of the second table's mouth, is that what the man of sixty-one wants is not a question but a hearing, that it will look exactly like a question from the top of that bank and is not one, and that Adrian is the man standing on that bank who cannot tell the difference at the moment.** At about half past three he comes down the bank for the first time in two years and asks the man on the stones a question he already knew the answer to. The answer is new: a lamp in a window never seen out, a cart loaded from inside by between four and nine people, **and not one of them has ever come out to him, not once in nine years, which is why he stands in a hedge two hundred yards off.** **A blanket folded at a culvert on the eighth mile, put back on some nights and taken on others, watched for about eleven weeks.** Kest had it in his book and never connected it, and says the reason he never asked is that asking would have made him responsible. He writes the entry, reads it back to himself and not to the yard, and asks the boy of seventeen to be one end of the book and not a writer. **The man on the stones says no, and asks for the reason to be entered, both halves or neither, and says a half a reason is worse than none.** Then he says nobody asked him, and that every one of the people standing up that bank can now be asked about it, and there is not one thing in this basin that can be asked of him and refused.
- **778, the sixty-second.** **The boy connects the row.** He takes the six mornings he found in his own method, looks for them in the third column of every row on his sheet, finds exactly one, and the first column of that row says *the man*. The man at the end of the second table confirms it: **that is my row and I put it there through you.** He asks for it to be struck out. The boy says he does not strike anything. **He asks for it to be KEPT, and says it in a yard, because a thing asked to be kept is a favour and an instruction has a shape.** The boy reads it out and it is entered nowhere. **The column's real defect is then named on the page by three people in one morning: three people in that yard have read a thing out loud in three weeks and one of them is a page.** And the man of fifty-six says the thing the boy has not been able to say in nine days: **you have read four things out loud in that yard and you are not in that column, and the reason is that you asked.** The boy cannot answer it, and then rules a third box on the back of his own sheet — *mornings on which a thing was said out loud in this yard and was not entered anywhere* — copies four mornings into it out of the box he ruled on the fifty-fourth morning, and rules a fifth space he leaves empty.
- **779, the sixty-third.** **The man of sixty-one asks the clerk what she would enter if a district in this basin became the thing that holds the obligation, and she answers in about two seconds: the number of the five things this district does not have, and it would be five. And then she would enter his name, because she would have to.** He asks Adrian for a seat rather than a room — the clause says the Custodian is one person and does not say one man and does not say named in advance — and says he would rather be robbed than hold it. **The price is one private reading, in a room, entered afterwards whatever it says.** Adrian asks what; he will not say what it takes, because a man who knows what a reading costs cannot refuse it and a man who cannot refuse is not a witness. He tells Adrian about a reading he gave in the sixth year to a page a woman in a ward carried herself, which took her the reason she was travelling and which she did not find out about for a year. **He attaches a condition and refuses to name it, and asks for that to be entered.** **The clerk refuses: the rule of the forty-fourth morning protects people in this yard and does not reach up that hill, and one of the two of you is going to say something in the next ten minutes that ought to be entered and she is the only person who can enter it and has just told them she cannot.** Adrian does not say no, and says so out loud in front of about nineteen people, and says he is not going to pretend he did not hear the offer and is not saying yes either, and that a closing needs two.
- **780, the sixty-fourth.** **Adrian has been in that yard eighty-five mornings and has not been given a bed, a wage or an office, and has not asked for any of the three.** He writes four lines with no figure and no term on them and reads them out loud himself: *figure: none. Term: neither is sent. Ask instead, and answer in a yard if the answer can be made true in a yard: what is the founder's clause, who holds it, whether it has an end on it, and whether the person who holds it can be named in front of about nineteen people who have never held anything.* He adds that one of the people whose consent the term requires is not in this district and cannot be found, and says so first so that whoever reads it has it in the same breath as the question. **The clerk enters no part of it: a thing you read in that yard is a conversation.** **The cart driver asks for nine words' notice of what is on it so that anybody who asks him can ask the yard and not the man.** At about four a man of fifty-six says the four off the wall and then says a fifth figure with nobody having asked him: **eighty-five, the mornings Adrian had been in that yard.** He says he is not going to be asked twice inside a fortnight and that he was not going to be frightened about it, and that the frightening part is not being asked, it is being the only thing in the yard that anybody is sure of. **The boy writes it in pencil in the new box and nobody enters it anywhere.** Iven Tallow goes up the lane in the dark with the case under his arm and says, at the top of the bank, that Adrian must not put the two facts of their lives side by side — eighty-five mornings with no bed and no wage and no office, and seventeen years holding up every room in this basin with nowhere to sleep in one of them — because people will, and that he says so at the top of a bank in about nineteen people's hearing so that if it happens there is one morning on which somebody else said so first.

# VOLUME 16, BLOCK 0003 — CHAPTER SUMMARIES CORRECTED BY THE SECOND REVIEW

> **FIFTEEN CONTINUITY DEFECTS, ALL REPAIRED IN PLACE IN THE CHAPTER FILES, AND THE THREE THAT ALTERED WHAT A CHAPTER SAYS ARE GIVEN HERE WITH THE CHAPTER. EVERY OTHER ONE IS A DURATION OR A FIGURE IN A SPEECH AND IS RECORDED IN `state/volume-16-batch-0003-summary.md` SECTION 9 AND IN `state/continuity.md`.**

- **771, the fifty-fifth.** **The invented name is cut.** The boy of seventeen asked the man of fifty-six to say a figure and said, first and out loud, that he has been in that yard four years and has never had a name for the man. He then dated the cart that stopped four feet below the top of that bank as *about six mornings ago* instead of *eleven*, in a sentence that already said *the six mornings since*; he placed two of his own unaccounted mornings at the top of the bank looking down at a man's hands instead of at the foot of it looking up; and the man of fifty-six's statement that he would be frightened for about a year is now five mornings back, which is the fiftieth, which is Chapter 766.
- **772, the fifty-sixth.** **Iven Tallow arrives.** Sixty-one, a hip, a leather case, no page in his hand, and the lane count does not move because of him. He names the parties, the term and what he is taking before anybody has asked him a question, and gives his own name to a yard because if he is going to be findable he had better be the one who says it. **The margin is dated four mornings back, which is the fifty-second and is Chapter 768, and the seventeen years of the thing he was believed dead for is no longer sixteen.**
- **773, the fifty-seventh.** **He asks the clerk for the price and she gives it to him: one page she owns, in her own hand, not a fair copy, in three or four days. She enters that a clause exists and that it has no end on it and that he wrote it, and refuses to enter that it is held or by whom or where.** **The man of about sixty-four no longer says a road keeper has asked him what the run is, because on the sixty-first morning the road keeper comes down that bank and asks him.**
- **774, the fifty-eighth.** **Mavis Dorr, the named cost.** She says the price before she says yes — *I am not a market, I am a list*, in her own voice — and names nine holders of the district's nine coppers out loud in a yard, and the ninth is Nell Cray, who has never been asked about anything and cannot be found by anybody who comes up that lane. The clerk enters no name. The cart driver says no. The page goes on the boards of the second table with a stone on the corner of it and is still there. The count of findable people in that yard is now the same ladder the rest of the block uses.** **The one duration in the chapter that carries a date is seven mornings, which is the fifty-first, and the yard's age is eleven mornings, which is the figure about twenty uses of this block agree on; a pass of this block had it here at fourteen days so that it would agree with a different figure, and the third review of this block found the yard holding four different ages across the block and cut the day forms rather than renumbering them.**
- **775, the fifty-ninth. THE MIDPOINT, AND THE ONE REPAIR THAT CHANGED A SCENE.** A man of sixty-one tells a man of thirty-eight the truth about the Long Fracture at the foot of a low wall with a man of about sixty-four four feet off on the stones. **The roles in one exchange are put back: it was Iven who said *I have nothing to say to you and I have said it*, on the fifty-seventh morning, and the man on the stones now says so to him and invites the speech instead of claiming it, and the day is corrected to the day before yesterday.** Adrian interrupts with *then give it to a market* in the voice he uses for good ideas and hears himself about a second later. Iven refuses the sentence and not the man. The man on the stones says *sit down* and Adrian sits. Iven names the one thing the gap does not excuse, refuses absolution twice, and tells Adrian in advance what he will become in about four years. **The unfixable speech no longer says the boy said the gatepost figure means nothing nine mornings ago, which is on no page.**
- **776, the sixtieth.** **The clerk does not enter the confession and says why. She asks the man of fifty-six for the column in a yard and he says yes on a condition and the condition is a rule about who may be entered made before anybody is refused. The boy copies her entry onto the wall in chalk in her words, at about five, reads it out loud while he is doing it, and the turner reads it twice and goes on up the hill. The woman who keeps a scale claims the yard in her own first person: it is her reading of the four lines seven mornings back, not Adrian's, and the clerk would not put HER name in the column. Three durations in this chapter were wrong and two of them had been recorded as repaired while still on the page, one of them in the title: *four days* is *seven days*, and the woman's own reading of the corridor note is *seven mornings ago* in every speech that mentions it, including the one that said *two*.**
- **777, the sixty-first.** **Tarin Kest comes down that bank for the first time in two years and asks the man on the stones a question he has known the answer to for two years.** The answer is a lamp in a window never seen out, a cart at about half past five, between four and nine people in a doorway and **not one of them has ever come out to him**, and a blanket folded at a culvert and put back on some nights by somebody who has never once been there at half past four. The man asks for the reason to be written down, both halves or neither. **Four figures in this chapter were wrong and all four are now right: the road keeper's own tenure is thirteen mornings in every speech that gives it, the count going in at a quarter past nine is an hour and a half later than the quarter to eight the man who keeps a tally entered that morning and not nine hours earlier, the nothing on the eleventh mile is a mile and a half and not nine hundred yards, and the man of fifty-six's own reading of that wall is a count of mornings and not nine years. A third pass of this block found that the half-hour-and-a-half was still being measured against a habitual quarter to eight that no chapter gives the man, and that a *nine years* negative span had been left standing one line above the one that was repaired; the quarter to eight is now on the page as what he entered, which is not the time he said it, and the man of fifty-six now does something he does not remember doing.**
- **778, the sixty-second.** **The boy connects the row.** *There is a row on my page with a word in the first column where a name goes*, and the word is *the man*, and the man at the end of the second table confirms it and asks for it to be struck out and the boy says no and is then asked to keep it and say so in a yard and does, and it is entered nowhere. The man of fifty-six tells him the thing he has not been able to say in nine days. **The man at the end of the second table now gives his own tenure as fifteen years at that table, in his own mouth, and the vague-favour line no longer carries a second figure.** The boy rules a third box headed *mornings on which a thing was said out loud in this yard and was not entered anywhere*, copies four mornings in, and rules a fifth space and leaves it empty.
- **779, the sixty-third.** **Iven Tallow asks for a seat rather than a room, and for one private reading, and refuses to say what the reading would take, and attaches a condition he will not name and asks for the withholding to be entered.** Mara Quill stops both of them and tells them the trap their own rule built: if they go up that lane, about nine people will assume what was said in it and there is no column any of them can enter it in. Adrian does not say no and says so out loud in a yard. **Three figures in this chapter were wrong and all three are now right: the yard is about full and not a third full, the offer was made at about a quarter to eleven and not at ten, and he has been on those stones for the eighth afternoon running and not the third or the fourth.**
- **780, the sixty-fourth.** **Adrian Vale has been in that yard eighty-five mornings and has not been given a bed, a wage or an office and has not asked for any of the three.** He writes four lines with no figure and no term on them, reads them out himself, and the clerk enters no part of it because a thing you read in that yard is a conversation. The cart driver asks for nine words' notice of what is on a page and gets them, and carries it up the lane at about half past four. The man of fifty-six says the four figures and then says a fifth nobody asked him for. Iven Tallow walks up the lane in the dark. **Five things in this chapter were wrong and all five are now right: the gatepost figure does not care what morning it is, the fifth of the five was not paid on any morning this district has had a lane with a figure at the end of it, the man who fitted the rail came through that yard at about ten this morning, the apparatus is in the third person throughout, and the gap on the eleventh mile is a mile and a half and not the length of his two hands. A third pass of this block removed an unattributable *thirtieth morning* of a run of about fifty out of the carrier line, which is the same class of cut its own repair table names, and removed a prophecy from the same line that the chapter's own four o'clock scene contradicts; the man who fitted the rail is no longer said twice in four mornings to have come through and not stopped at the wall.**

# VOLUME 16, BLOCK 0003 — CHAPTER SUMMARIES CORRECTED BY THE THIRD REVIEW

> **A THIRD `novel-reviewer` WAS DISPATCHED AGAINST THE SAME TEN FILES AFTER THE SECOND REPAIR. IT RE-MEASURED THE FIGURE SYSTEM AND THE DUPLICATION COUNTS ITSELF, CONFIRMED THEM, AND THEN FOUND ELEVEN THINGS THAT NO FIGURE BLOCK CONTAINS: A SPEECH GIVEN TO THE WRONG PERSON, A THING THE APPARATUS SAID WAS ON A WALL THAT NOTHING PUT THERE, A FIGURE HELD AT FOUR VALUES ACROSS ONE CHAPTER, A REPAIRED LINE WITH ITS EXACT TWIN LEFT BEHIND ONE LINE ABOVE IT, A SENTENCE THAT POINTS THE OPPOSITE WAY FROM THE CLAUSE BEFORE IT, A COUNT NOBODY PUTS A NAME TO, A PROPHECY THE CHAPTER CONTRADICTS ON THE SAME MORNING, AN OFF-BY-ONE IN A FIGURE THE RECORD ITSELF WARNS ABOUT FOUR TIMES, A QUOTATION THAT A REPAIR HAD ALREADY MADE WRONG, A BEAT REPEATED FOUR MORNINGS APART INSIDE DIFFERENT CARRIER SENTENCES, AN INVENTED DISTRICT INSIDE A COUNT OF PEOPLE, AND A FIGURE IN THE ONE CHARACTER'S OWN ENUMERATION THAT NOBODY WOULD CHECK BECAUSE EVERYBODY ELSE SAYS *A FORTNIGHT* ALL OVER THIS MANUSCRIPT. ALL ELEVEN ARE REPAIRED IN PLACE. NO BEAT WAS MOVED AND NO CHAPTER WAS RESTARTED.**

1. **776 — the same woman reads the note and is addressed as though Adrian read it.** She says *what I read out loud in this yard seven mornings ago* and four lines later, in the same speech, *You read four lines seven mornings ago and you got all four and about nineteen people heard you, and the clerk would not put your name in the column*. The reading is hers, on the fifty-third morning, and the clerk's refusal of her name is the whole reason she is standing on her own step. The three pronouns are now hers, and the beat the chapter wanted — a woman claiming the instrument and then finding she is not on it — is the beat it now gets. **BOTH EARLIER PASSES EDITED THIS SPEECH AND MISSED IT, BECAUSE THE SEVEN-MORNINGS REPAIR WAS IN IT.**
2. **776 — an entry that was on the wall, by nobody.** Nothing puts the clerk's entry on that wall: she entered it in the lot book and read it out in the yard at about four, and she entered no name and refused twice. The sentence also slipped from *that night* to *that morning*. **The boy now copies the entry onto the wall himself in chalk, in her words and in her writing, at about five, and reads it out loud while he is doing it, because there was nowhere else in that yard to put a thing he had not been asked to enter** — which is the block's own thesis, done with a piece of chalk, by the one character in that yard whose whole function is reading things aloud. The turner reads it twice and goes on up the hill. Chapter 778's line about what is on that wall on the sixty-second morning is now true and is unchanged.
3. **The yard's age, which was holding four values.** *Fourteen days* on the fifty-eighth, *about eleven days* on the sixtieth, *a fortnight* on the sixtieth, and *thirteen mornings* four times in Chapter 777 — with *eleven days* twice in that same chapter. **All of the day forms are now mornings and the yard is eleven mornings, which is the figure about twenty uses of the block already agree on and which does not move across the ten mornings. The thirteen mornings in Chapter 777 are Tarin Kest's own span and are correct, and are now not confusable with the yard's age because the two are no longer written in different units in the same chapter.**
4. **777 — the repaired line's twin.** *A thing he has not done in nine years*, one line above a line that had just been rewritten because his own count is five hundred and two mornings and he may not be given a span. He now does a thing he does not remember doing.
5. **777 — a sentence that pointed the other way.** The count went in at a quarter past nine, *which is about an hour and a half later than it has ever gone in, and the man who keeps a tally entered that he had said it earlier*. Later, then earlier. And the half-hour-and-a-half was being measured against a quarter to eight that no chapter gives the man, because on the page a quarter to eight is only ever the man of fifty-six reading that wall. **The quarter to eight is now on the page as what the man entered that morning, which is not the time he said it, and the interval is measured inside the one morning.**
6. **780 — an unattributable count and a self-contradicted prophecy.** *The thirtieth morning of a run that was always going to be about fifty* is the same class of unattributable count this block's own repair table says was cut elsewhere rather than renumbered, and *it is the last of them at which anything gets said at that wall* is contradicted ninety lines later in the same chapter, at about four, on that wall, with four figures and a fifth and a paragraph of speech. **Both are cut and the line now says that nothing has been said at that wall except the four until this morning**, which is true, is a record of a fact, and costs nothing.
7. **780 — a beat said twice, four mornings apart.** *The man who fitted it was in that yard at about ten this morning and did not stop at the wall* stood in Chapter 776 and again in Chapter 780, inside different carrier sentences, which is why the block still measured zero duplicated paragraphs and two duplicated sentences. **Chapter 780's is now a different carrier** — he came through and did not stop — and the length of wood on the near side of that table still has nothing to do with anybody's kindness.
8. **771 — a figure inside the one character's own arithmetic.** The boy auditing his own method in public put one of his six unaccounted mornings at *a fortnight ago*; the sixth is the forty-ninth and it is six mornings back. **It is six mornings ago.** This one is small and it is last, and the block treats *a fortnight* as elastic everywhere else, which is exactly why nobody looks at it here.
9. **And the three that were not in the chapters at all** — the record's off-by-one on Adrian's own tenure, the record's stale quotation of the woman with a scale, and the invented district inside the continuity file's count of people who have read a thing out loud — **are recorded in `state/volume-16-batch-0003-summary.md` section 10 and in `state/continuity.md`, and the two that would have propagated into Chapters 781 to 790 were the first two.**

> **THE FIGURE SYSTEM CAME BACK CLEAN A FOURTH TIME. The reviewer re-derived all seventeen rows across ten mornings, re-measured the word counts, the duplication scope, the `about nine` count and the static carriers, and reproduced every figure this record gives.**

# VOLUME 16, BLOCK 0004 — CHAPTER SUMMARIES

> **CHAPTERS 781 TO 790, THE SIXTY-FIFTH THROUGH THE SEVENTY-FOURTH MORNING AFTER THE THIRTIETH OF THE EIGHTH MONTH. THE BLOCK CARRIES THE VOLUME'S ESCALATION: THE LOCK COUNCIL COMES DOWN THE LANE, AND ADRIAN AND MARA ARGUE OVER A SAFETY VALVE. THREE PASSES WERE DISPATCHED AGAINST THESE TEN FILES AND FIFTY-ONE PLUS THIRTY-ONE PLUS TWENTY-TWO REPAIRS WERE APPLIED IN PLACE ACROSS THE TEN, NO BEAT MOVED AND NO CHAPTER RESTARTED. NOT ONE BLOCKING DEFECT IN ANY OF THE THREE PASSES WAS A LADDER CELL. **THE THIRD PASS IS THE ONE THAT MATTERED AND IT IS THE ONLY ONE THAT FOUND A DEFECT IN THE RECORD RATHER THAN IN THE PROSE: ELEVEN FIGURES AND CLAIMS THAT `state/volume-16-batch-0004-summary.md` STATES AND ITS OWN CHAPTERS DO NOT SUPPORT, ALL ELEVEN CORRECTED IN PLACE. THE SUMMARIES BELOW ARE AS THEY STAND AFTER THAT PASS.**

- **781, the sixty-fifth.** **An appointed administrator comes down the lane with a page in her right hand and the boy says twenty-two before the clerk opens the book.** She is **Sabra Holt**, fifty-two, nineteen years an administrator, and she holds the third of the nine oldest keys, and a key is not a seat and a key is not a vote. **The sitting at Alder Reach is in nineteen days and this is the only figure in the block that is a distance to it; every later reference is *that room*.** She will accept either a named bearer it can pay or two fresh independent witnesses, she will not choose, and she will not leave before the sitting. She spends a whole morning asking nobody anything, and says why: a man who reads four figures to whoever stands there is a different man from the one she can get in an hour, and the second would be worth about four words in a room. The woman who keeps a scale says out loud that she is the first person down that lane in nine years who has not come for a person, and asks for it on a wall. The cart driver says the figure twenty-one is not room for a second name of his. The boy rules a space under the morning in his box and leaves it empty and says he would like everybody to know the day she asks him something.
- **782, the sixty-sixth.** **THE VALVE, AND ADRIAN LOSES IT.** He describes a settlement's safety valve in the third person and Mara Quill stops walking: *you have been in that yard eighty-seven mornings and every findable person in it became findable because of a thing you said out loud.* He says *then give it a term* and hears the shape of it a second afterwards and does not take it back. He offers to go up the lane alone to the room and read the terms and come back, and she refuses, and the reason is not distrust: *you have offered it to me, at nine in the morning, on a lane, which is a room with one person in it and a hedge.* She then gives him the half she had not said to anybody — that she is asking for a worse decade on purpose, because a worse decade is visible and a hand is not. **A woman with a page is standing at the top of the bank when they come down it and asks which of the two of them is the market, and Adrian says *nobody*, and she writes it down and says a district with no market cannot pay a bearer, cannot give fresh consent, and is not a district a sitting can put a clause in front of, and that the sitting does not care which of those three is true.**
- **783, the sixty-seventh.** **THE CLERK NAMES HERSELF.** *I am the market of Lot Seventeen*, in the ordinary voice, before she has been asked twice, and then the whole of what there is not: no figure, because a market without a figure has not decided anything since she was nineteen; no term, because a term is a length and this one has no end on it; and no person she can pay, and therefore **the fifth of the five things this district does not have is me**. Adrian is handed the move that would undo it — *say in a yard that you are a woman with a book and not a market and I will enter it and read it out at four o'clock* — **and does not say it**, and the woman who keeps a scale says one word from her own step at about forty feet and the word is *there*. The woman with a key refuses to enter the sentence, because a market that was asked in a market yard by an appointed administrator is a different sentence in about four years, and enters that a thing was said and gives the reason. Mara Quill then says, unasked, that she has made herself findable and that she did it to herself in a yard with a woman holding a key standing in the middle of the boards, and wants both halves written down or neither. **The findable count resolves as one ladder: six before the fifty-eighth morning, about fourteen now, and Mara Quill is the sixteenth.**
- **784, the sixty-eighth.** **THE RULE.** A woman with a key announces at about ten that she is going to ask the boy a question, and the man of fifty-six says out loud, in that yard, in front of about nineteen people, that the thing she came for is a rule about who may be entered made before anybody is refused. She refuses to make it: a rule about who may be entered in a public record, made by a person with a key, is a rule a room can take apart with a line of its own, and she has watched four districts learn that in three and be ruined by it in the fourth. **A man at the end of the second table, who has not said a whole sentence in that yard in a very long time, makes it at about half past ten: a person goes in that column on their own asking, out loud, in a yard, and not on somebody's, and what is written down is the asking and not the reading.** He then says the part that is his — that Adrian Vale is not in that column under the rule, that he made the rule and the rule does not stop him — and that **the man of fifty-six is not in it either, because he was asked and said yes, and saying yes to being asked is not asking.** He does not argue. He says he knew it would not go his way and asked for it anyway, because what he asked for was not the column. **The woman who keeps a scale tells him to his face that it is not the fright and not an accident and that wanting has not been one of the things on offer at that wall.** Sabra Holt enters the rule in her own book and says she will write down that it was made by a person whose name is on no page in this district, and then says the rule does not help the boy, because the rule is about a column and the boy is not going in a column.
- **785, the sixty-ninth.** **THE BOY SAYS NO, AND SAYS WHY NOBODY ASKED HIM FOR.** He cannot be a witness because he has understood the terms the way you understand the weather, and because there is a row on his own page with a word in the first column where a name goes, which he noticed on the forty-third morning, twenty-six mornings back, worked out what to do about, did not do, and walked away from up that lane, and has not been able to make himself do it since. **In about four years somebody will ask him what he saw, and he saw it, and he did not write it down, and that is the whole of why he cannot go up that hill as a witness.** The man whose row it is asks him to say the name so the row would stop being a question; the boy refuses, because the second you want it to be a name it is a name and it is settled and it is worth nothing to anybody nine miles up a hill. Then **he asks the woman with a key to enter his own refusal**, because if she does not, it goes up that hill without him. **Mara Quill refuses to enter it**, and her reason is the best thing any instrument in that district has done in the volume: *a person in two books cannot get out of either one, and my rule was never about what is good for this district. It was about what a person can refuse.* He is seventeen and he is the only person in that district a Council has asked to do a thing and who has refused one, and he says he worked that out while he was standing there. He goes down the eleven feet of that bank for the first time in eight mornings and says good afternoon to a man of about sixty-four and has no idea what he is doing there.
- **786, the seventieth.** **THE DEBT TO THE CREDITOR IS PAID.** Mara Quill comes down the bank with the lot book and says first that nobody asked her to and that there is a woman with a key in the yard. **It is not in the lot book. It is on a page of hers with nothing ruled on it: the lamp at about half past four, the cart loaded from inside by between four and nine people, the blanket at a culvert on the eighth mile, and his reason for standing two hundred yards off in a hedge for nine years — entered against his express wish, without being asked whether it was true or whether he wanted it there — and a figure of eleven seconds that has been in a book for nine years against a description of him, off a copy of a page in a corridor nine miles up a hill.** He asks one question and it is the only one he asks anybody that day: **does that book say that I asked to keep it.** It does not, because it was written before he said it. *Then it is a record of me and not of what I said, and I have been on a page for about nine mornings and nobody read it to me.* He says he has no complaint against her and wants that said first. **She enters the asking, at about half past four, in her own hand, in the ordinary voice, and reads it out before she writes it, and it is the first line anybody in that district has entered in a year at the word of the man it was about.** The number of documents this district does not own is four and did not move. **And the woman with a key says *I can find her*, and Mavis Dorr says *then find her*, and says do it as a Council and not as a kindness, and the woman with a key says she will ask Nell Cray herself and not the yard, and will carry the answer up the hill whether it is yes or no.** The man of about sixty-four goes out at about half past four and says that is the only thing about that morning he is able to say out loud.
- **787, the seventy-first.** **THE MAN AT THE END OF THE SECOND TABLE DESCRIBES THE THING HE HAS BEEN STANDING NEAR FOR FIFTEEN YEARS.** It is the fifth line and it is him and it is four words long and one of them is *until*, and it has been working every morning since, and it does not get paid and it does not get asked to. He says what it has cost — the ability to want anything, and about nine years of being careful in a yard which he did not notice losing — and then says the other half: **that is not the fifth of the five, the fifth of the five is a way to pay a person who is not in a household, the count is still five, and what he has got is worth exactly nothing to a room nine miles up a hill.** He is asked to be a witness and refuses, and the reason is the best thing in the block: *I dictated a line into a book at about twenty-two. I am not a person standing near a thing. I am the person who wrote one, and a man watching his own handwriting go down is a man with a reason, and a reason is the one thing a witness is not allowed to have.* **Iven Tallow says the same about himself from the second table.** The woman with a key says *there is nobody* out loud in a yard and then says the harder half: every person in that yard is already party to something, and the reason you cannot witness it is the reason you know what it costs, and the arrangement is that the only people who could stand in the room are the people it has already used — **and the reason it is so is that on the fifty-ninth morning a man told the truth in a yard with nine people above him instead of in a room, and the two he could not find in that hall were allowed to be two strangers off a road.** The chapter ends with a question asked and one sentence answered: *what is it like at half past four, on the road.* *Like being the only person who knows where he is going.*
- **788, the seventy-second. THE CLIMAX.** Adrian asks a man of sixty-one, in a yard, in front of about nineteen people, and he keeps the condition on the seat unnamed, says he will say no out loud if anybody asks him again, and says it is the only thing he has worth more than what was asked. Then he gives three reasons the page he promised cannot come: it is a page in Mara Quill's hand and he cannot write in her hand; it cannot go up that lane because the only carrier in this basin refused one and then, on the sixty-fourth morning, was asked again and said yes and carried a page in his cart at about half past four, and Iven is holding that very page; and there is no instrument in this district that can carry a page up a hill. The fourth reason is the one nobody expected: **for seventeen years he has held an obligation on the understanding that it was written down in a form that could be moved, and it is written down, and it cannot be moved, and a boy of seventeen made it legally immovable, and he did not know that until this morning.** The man at the end of the second table supplies the alternative and says it is not his idea: say it out loud, in a yard, in about nineteen people's hearing, because there is no instrument in this basin that can stop a thing said out loud in a yard. **The woman with a key says the reading is worse than the page and does not hide it.** Adrian asks anyway. A man of sixty-one takes out a sheet of paper that is not a page, and reads nine lines out loud, once, in the ordinary voice, without explaining any part of it, and about nine people in that yard recognise it and none of them says anything. **A conversation is not a thing four people made. The count did not move. The clerk could not enter it and gave the rule, and the rule is the reason the one document that could answer the notice is unenterable, uncarriable and unreadable.**
- **789, the seventy-third. TWO FIGURES AND A REFUSAL.** The man of fifty-six states his reading for a record — the four, in the order on the wall, five hundred and fourteenth morning — and is asked three things and not the fourth. **She says out loud, before she asks the first, that she is not going to ask him which of those four is wrong, and why.** Asked whether anybody in this district has ever been paid for reading, he says no, and adds that there has never once been a question put to him about it in five hundred and fourteen mornings, and the woman who keeps a scale's sentence from the sixty-eighth morning is what he has not been able to put down since. The finding she will carry: a person in a market yard states he has read four figures twice a morning for five hundred and fourteen mornings and has never been paid and has never asked. **The woman of fifty-eight says the words *that is the fifth of the five* out loud, and the woman with a key agrees, and it is the first time the fifth of the five has been said as a fact about one person instead of a sentence about a place.** Then the boy counts Adrian twice: **ninety-four on his own page, ninety-one on what a person in that yard would count, and he tells her which is wrong and refuses to give her the right one**, because the two are different decisions, one about what is true and one about what a person can be asked about afterwards, and he has never known which is better. She writes ninety-one and writes *on the word of a boy of seventeen, who states the correct figure and does not offer it*, and says it is the first entry in nineteen years in which the number and the person disagree and the person is more reliable. **Adrian says nothing at all, and it is the first fact in about ninety-four mornings he wanted to put a term on and could not.** Mara Quill refuses, in the ordinary voice, to say her own refusal in a yard, because the sentence would have him in the middle of it, and asks the boy of seventeen to hear her refuse it.
- **790, the seventy-fourth. THE BLOCK'S CLOSE.** **A clerk enters a sentence about a column.** The rule made on the sixty-eighth morning is entered six mornings after the fact, with the interval entered too. The column contains one name, that name does not satisfy the rule because it went in at the word of a boy asking for a place to put a name and not at the word of the person it belongs to, **and a figure in this district may not be corrected, struck, replaced, superseded or taken out, and she is therefore not able to take it out**, and in fifteen years she has never entered a thing about that column that was not a name. The man of fifty-six asks her to enter a sentence she has read out twice, and says he is not asking to be entered: he cannot tell whether he would be able to stand it, and the woman with the scale has already told him the difference between not asking and not wanting. **A woman with a key names four things she is carrying up that hill and the fifth she will not take, and the fifth is a woman on a working list forty-one, and the reason she is leaving it is that a room makes a decision about a person in the time it takes to write a figure down and it makes it about a list.** The man of about twenty-nine who drives the cart says the only unprotected thing anybody said in ten mornings: she is the first person to come up that lane in nine years who has left without a person, and he did not know until about a week ago that that was a thing that could be done. She says *I noticed too* and goes up the lane. **The lane count is twenty-two and did not move, and does not move for the other direction, and there is nobody in this district who has ever proposed that it should.** Adrian comes down the bank and asks his fourth question and **Iven Tallow answers it from the stones, and the answer is yes, and it is that he is the fifteenth person in that district that anybody can look up and he did that to himself on purpose and it has cost about nine other people their choice, and he has had eighteen mornings to work out whether that was a gift and he has not.** The man at the end of the second table says the other half of it into the middle of the yard, to nobody. **The boy writes the morning in his box, rules a space, leaves it empty, and writes ninety-five in the margin in pencil and does not say it out loud and does not offer it to anybody, and about four people at the top of that bank have already decided what they are going to do about the sitting and not one of them knew.**

---

# VOLUME 16, BLOCK 0005 — CHAPTERS 791 TO 800, THE SEVENTY-FIFTH TO THE EIGHTY-FOURTH MORNING AFTER THE THIRTIETH OF THE EIGHTH MONTH

> **THE BLOCK RECORD IS `state/volume-16-batch-0005-summary.md` AND IT IS THE MEASUREMENT. NOTHING IN THIS SECTION CORRECTS A FIGURE ON A LADDER. A RELATIVE DAY IS THE DIFFERENCE AND NOT THE COUNT: `c = 0` IS CHAPTER 750 AND THE MORNING NUMBER IS `c + 34`.**

**791 — THE SEVENTY-FIFTH MORNING. The man of about thirty-two who puts tables up puts his thumb in the hollow in the stone on the second table and says a hollow with nothing in it is a sentence, and carries the stone off that table in his coat and will not say where he puts it. Nobody says the word *page* until a woman with a scale says it from her step. The man at the end of the second table says four things out loud for the first time in about fifteen years: that he does not know where a page went, that he has had seventeen mornings to look for it, that he did not look for it because he did not want it found, and that a page which turns up afterwards is the worst kind there is because a room will believe it was made first. Mavis Dorr says she said a name out loud on the fifty-eighth morning and has had five mornings to think about whether that was a kindness and it was a way of being able to say it was not her fault. The man of fifty-six asks a woman with a key what happens to that wall, out loud, in a yard, for the first time in five hundred and sixteen mornings, and she answers *I do not know* and tells him a room will use him as a sentence. He asks for it to be entered, and she enters it, and he puts his hand on where the stone was and leaves it on bare board. The lane count is twenty-two and the count of the five things this district does not have is five and is not paid.**

**792 — THE SEVENTY-SIXTH MORNING. The woman who keeps a scale carries her beam scale up eleven feet of bank for the first time in nine years, in both hands, and lets nobody touch it, and says she has had nine years of other people's weight across that beam and has never once put a person on it, because there is no figure in this district a person can be weighed against, and that this is the fifth of the five, which she has *known* since she was forty and *been* for nine years, two figures she keeps apart at `chapter-0792.md:37` and `chapter-0792.md:75`, and that she never said it out loud because a thing said out loud in that yard gets used. She asks Adrian Vale what he is paid in and does not leave the answer alone. She names four people in that yard who can be named and cannot be paid and says she is the fifth. She refuses him her step in front of about nineteen people and says why: a man who comes to a step is a man being photographed giving something to a person who has nothing. She asks whether he will be here in ten mornings; he says he does not know; she says that has been the answer off that step every morning for nine years and has never once changed, and that this morning it was the same answer and she has got a different figure out of it and will not tell either of them what it was.**

**793 — THE SEVENTY-SEVENTH MORNING. The turner, who has come up that lane twice a day for nine years and read that wall off the wall in a lane with nobody in it, asks the man of fifty-six to say the four figures to him, and gives the reason out loud: he wants to know whether he has been wrong for six years and he is not going to find out. The man of fifty-six reads them, in the order on the wall, at the same speed, and they go into a man of forty-one standing at the foot of a bank. A woman with one of the oldest keys in this basin rules that it is not to happen twice, and gives the argument of the block: once is not a record, twice would be a practice, and a practice is what a district has instead of a person. He asks for it entered that it happened once and that he would do it again, and she enters it. The turner says not being asked is not the same as having nothing to say, and that he has been saying nothing for nine years because it was cheaper, and that he answered every question she asked and asked for none of them back. Adrian asks him one question and is told it will be answered in the lane on the way down and not in the yard.**

**794 — THE SEVENTY-EIGHTH MORNING. Sabra Holt announces that she will ask the man on the stones whether he will stand as the named bearer for the district of Lot Seventeen for a term, and says the question out loud before she asks it. He asks for one thing first and she lets him have it, and says he is not going to hold himself to the exact wording of what he said on the seventy-second morning because the person asking is not the person he had in mind, and that he is still going to say no, and that the condition is not named and is not going to be named, and that if she asks him what it is he will say no to that as well and would like both entered. She asks. He says no, faster than the question. He asks for the asking to be entered before the clerk has written anything. Mara Quill enters it on the page at the back with nothing ruled on it and reads it out before she writes it, and says she has put a description of a man on stones and every one of them is him. Sabra Holt promises, in a yard, never to ask him twice. He says thank you, and Adrian Vale, unasked, says it is the first time anybody in that yard has said thank you to him in fourteen mornings. The man of the second table says two men have been refused out of that room inside seven mornings and neither refusal was made by a person.**

**795 — THE SEVENTY-NINTH MORNING. A boy of seventeen says the word *one hundred* out loud in a yard in front of about nineteen people when nobody has asked him for it, and gives the reason: on the seventy-third morning he gave a woman with a key ninety-one, told her his own page said ninety-four, and would not give her the right one, and he has had six mornings to find out whether that was about what is true or about what a person can be asked about afterwards, and this morning he has decided to find out. Sabra Holt writes it and reads out what she is writing under it. Mara Quill says she cannot strike the ninety-one and cannot replace the hundred, because a figure in this district may not be corrected, struck, replaced or taken out, and because there is a heading over that column that says whoever read a thing out loud and a column filled according to the asking does not answer to its heading. Sabra Holt says she is carrying the wrong one up the hill. The count of things this district has made did not move. Adrian says he is not going to correct either figure and says so out loud. The boy writes Adrian's refusal to let him write. Nobody was corrected and nothing was struck.**

**796 — THE EIGHTIETH MORNING. Nell Cray walks two miles down a lane and up eleven feet of bank, on foot, having been asked in writing by a woman with a key and not by the yard, and having told the yard nobody obliged her and she wanted that said first. The boy says the lane figure out loud and then says *twenty-two does not move*, because there is no stranger and there is no page and nothing came down that lane. She says she holds the sixth of the nine coppers and has been on working list forty-one for nine years and has never been asked what she is worth.
