She says no, out loud, in the sun, in front of about nineteen people: she will not stand in a room for a term, because a list has paid for everything she has ever done and she is not going to have her name be a second thing at the bottom of her. She says the reason she walked is that a sentence in a letter is a thing you can hand round a room and nobody in it has to look at your face while they read it. She says the hard half: this district does not have nine holders of nine coppers, it has nine people on a list in a corridor who are already paid by something with no figures in it and no end on it, and nobody has ever asked any of the nine whether that counts. Sabra Holt says she will not write the reason in her own words and will carry only the no. The clerk enters the no. Mavis Dorr says one sentence and stops.**

**797 — THE EIGHTY-FIRST MORNING. A stranger comes down that lane on foot with a page in her right hand and a boy of seventeen says twenty-three out loud before the clerk's hand has moved, and the lane count moves for the first time since the sixty-fifth morning. A woman with a scale marks the morning from her step. The stranger reads out a notice which says an application by a district cannot be entertained at the sitting without a named bearer who can be paid for a term, and then names the officer who filed it: a woman of fifty-two, an appointed administrator of nineteen years, holding the third of the nine oldest keys, who states that the district cannot produce a person. Sabra Holt says she has never once been named in a page in that corridor in nineteen years, that she filed one on the first morning she came up the hill without knowing she was filing, and that she is not going to complain, because complaining would spend the only thing she has ever wanted, which is to be findable. Adrian says he will come up the hill with her. She refuses: he cannot stand next to her, because a man of thirty-eight walking into that room turns a page about the district into a page about himself. She says she will say one thing in that room about that page and will not say it in that yard. The man of about twenty-nine who drives the cart, after seven mornings of silence, says you could complain about it and complaining costs more. Mara Quill enters one line without reading it out, which is the first thing she has entered in fifteen years without reading out first.**

**798 — THE EIGHTY-SECOND MORNING. Adrian Vale asks, out loud, in a yard, in front of about nineteen people, on his own asking, to be entered in the column for whoever read a thing out loud, and says why: that is my name on a page and it is not me on a page. Mara Quill reads the column out and says no, on two grounds, and the second is the sentence of the block: the heading over that column is about the reading and the rule made fifteen mornings earlier is about the asking, so a column filled according to the asking does not answer to the heading over it, and a person reading that heading in about four years will find a true name under a heading that has stopped being true, and that is how a book gets to be a thing that hides. She enters his asking on the page at the back with nothing ruled on it, reads it out before she writes it, and enters in the same breath that she is not able to make a heading true. Adrian says the rule is perfect and has never refused anybody because there has never been anybody for it to refuse. The man at the end of the second table asks what is broken. Adrian says he has about two mornings of it and will not pretend to more.**

**799 — THE EIGHTY-THIRD MORNING. The man at the end of the second table asks, standing up, in the light, in about nineteen people's hearing, on his own asking, in the exact words of the rule he made himself fifteen mornings earlier, to be entered in that column. He satisfies every condition of the rule exactly. Mara Quill opens the book, looks at the column, and says no: that column is for whoever read a thing out loud and there is not one thing for him to be entered for. He does not argue. He says *then the rule is not the problem* and that the problem is a heading, because a heading is not a figure and nobody in this basin can strike a line under a heading. And he asks: **who ruled that column.** And Mara Quill says, twice, that she does not know — that there is a ruled column on the far side of the last page that was ruled before she was put in front of it, and that she entered *not asked* fifty times over the first fifty mornings of it, and that she wrote the rule about not asking herself, and that she has never once in fifteen years looked at that heading as though it were a thing she did not know. She says it is not a confession, because a confession is about a thing she did. She says she is not entering it until that room has sat. He tells Adrian Vale that there is a length of fencing wire in his coat and says out loud, in about nineteen people's hearing, that he is not going to ask him what it is for. The man of about sixty-four tells about nineteen people that he goes out at about half past four, that it is nine miles up that hill, and that he is not going to stop.**

**800 — THE EIGHTY-FOURTH MORNING, THE MORNING THE ROOM AT ALDER REACH SITS. There is no key in that yard and about nineteen people in it and every one of them knows where everybody else is. A woman with a scale says it sits at about ten and that she has known since the sixty-fifth morning and has not said it, because a yard that has been told a time is a yard that stops doing the things it does with the rest of the morning. Nothing happens from about ten until about four. Iven Tallow says, unasked, that it does not matter what happens today, because the only person who can end the clause is him, that he has not ended it, that he is not going to end it whatever a room says, and that a room cannot end it either because a room is not the holder — and that in about four years somebody is going to come up that lane and offer him a way out of the gap and he has thought about it and is not going to take it. The man of about sixty-four goes out at about half past four and does not stop. At about four the man of fifty-six says the four off that wall and gets all four of them, for the five hundred and twenty-fifth time. **And Adrian Vale says out loud, in the ordinary voice, in that yard, that everything this basin writes says that it is a record of a thing and not of a person; that the founder's clause is a record of a thing that was never a thing and has a person's name inside it and nobody in this basin will be able to tell you whose; that he has never read it and neither has the man who reads that wall nor the boy with a sheet; and that the same thing has happened at every end of that yard for as long as he has been standing in it. And then he says: a record that cannot name the person it burdens is not protecting anybody, it is only hiding. The clerk asks whether she may enter it and he says no before she has answered, and gives the reason: a sentence about records entered in a record is a record about a record, and its heading would be about a thing, and it would be the last instance of the defect it describes and it would be his. The boy of seventeen refuses to write it in the body of his sheet, because the day that sheet is found everything in the body of it is a record. Nobody in that yard wrote it down. The bid has been open four hundred and forty-eight days and was not run. The count of the five things this district does not have is five. The figure on the sheet at that gatepost is four hundred and eleven. Adrian Vale has been in that yard one hundred and five mornings and has not been given a bed, a wage or an office, and has a length of fencing wire in his coat about four miles from a gap of a mile and a half that he has not been to.**

---

# VOLUME 17, BLOCK 0001 — CHAPTERS 801 TO 810, THE EIGHTY-FIFTH TO THE NINETY-FOURTH MORNING AFTER THE THIRTIETH OF THE EIGHTH MONTH

**THE FOUNDER'S CLAUSE IS USED, THE BID IS APPENDED, AND A WALL IN A YARD CHANGES.**

**801 — THE EIGHTY-FIFTH MORNING.** The figure on the sheet at that gatepost is four hundred and eleven and its size of error is not computed. The man of fifty-six said the four off that wall at about a quarter to eight and got four figures he has never read off it, said them again, and said out loud in about nineteen people's hearing that he did not know them. He said the four were not changed, that he knows the old four the way he knows the number of steps on that bank, that he would not say which one of the new four he could not have said a week ago, and that he is not going to be a man who lets a thing be taken out of him by being slow. Adrian Vale understood what had happened to the yard before anybody in it said a word. A clerk said she was a person who enters and has entered about nine thousand things and found out none of them. A stranger came down that hill at about a quarter to nine with a page in her right hand, stood at the bottom of the bank and would not come up the eleven feet. Orren Pike said twenty-four at about ten past nine, out loud, before Mara Quill had moved her hand off the cover of the lot book. The woman who keeps a scale said it had moved twice in nine mornings and both times because somebody came down that hill with a page in her hand, and that it had not moved once for a thing any of them did. The page has a heading about a thing, says the open matter of the district is to be taken into one permanent record, gives a form of words, gives no term and no end, and asks for nothing; then it names the officer who filed the application, a woman of fifty-two, an appointed administrator of nineteen years, holding the third of the nine oldest keys, who has stated in writing that the district cannot produce a person. The man of fifty-six refused to say the old four out loud that morning and asked for the asking to be entered.

**802 — THE EIGHTY-SIXTH MORNING.** Sabra Holt read the page in full at about ten, twice, including the eleven words of form, and said she was not going to explain them because explaining them makes them hers. At about ten and six a strip of paper about the length of a hand came off the boards of the first table with nobody standing near it; it gave the district, the thing, the figure four hundred and fifty, and the words *held and not questioned*. Adrian stopped anybody touching it and said why: a person handling it is this district holding it, and a district that holds a matter cannot be asked about the holding of it. Sabra Holt picked it up and carried it up and put it on the second table, and said in the yard that she had held a key for nineteen years and had never used it on a piece of paper and had been saving it for one, and that the reason she used it this morning was a bad one and she would have used a better one if there was a better one. The man of fifty-six said out loud that this district was now the only one in the basin anybody can be asked about. Iven Tallow said from the stones that they had about four days before anybody nine miles up that hill had to ask anybody anything, and that he was not going to help, and that if he helped it would be his and not theirs. The bid was open four hundred and fifty days and was not run and is not open.

**803 — THE EIGHTY-SEVENTH MORNING.** Iven Tallow came down the eleven feet at about ten past eleven and said, unasked, that he put the clause to that use on the eighty-fifth morning between about eight and about nine from a room nine miles up that hill, that the four figures on that wall are what came of it, that he said nothing to anybody about it on three mornings, and that he did it because a settlement at the eleventh place on the Salt Verge has been coming apart for about four days and that the thing that holds a settlement together is somebody being answerable for it. He said the word Custodian out loud in a yard for the first time. The man of fifty-six said the four figures he had been reading since the middle of the winter out loud at about eleven and a clerk wrote them on a page with nothing ruled on it, and the yard had two sets of four figures in it and neither could be struck. The man at the end of the second table said that a rule nobody can name had never once been tested because there had never been anything worth testing it on, and that in about four years two of the district's refusals would be put side by side.

**804 — THE EIGHTY-EIGHTH MORNING.** Sabra Holt asked, out loud before she asked it, what the fifth line is for, and the man at the end of the second table refused to be asked that way and answered Mavis Dorr's different question instead: he was about thirty-two, he wrote the line at about twenty-two, and he thought it was for stopping a person. He said it has cost him the ability to want anything since the winter after he wrote it and he would rather that line were the end of the book than have anybody put a name in a column for him. The clerk refused to enter any of it on the ground that the lot book would then hold a sentence about the man who wrote the fifth line. Orren Pike said he would not write it in the body of his sheet and explained why in the register of the audience, and wrote the fact and the number of witnesses and left out the two best things the man said.

**805 — THE EIGHTY-NINTH MORNING.** A sheet of four lines reached the boards at about a quarter to eleven and named a woman on working list forty-one in a corridor nine miles up that hill, without anybody asking her. The man of about twenty-nine who drives the cart spoke for the first time in nine mornings: she is not in that room, nobody has asked her, and a record is not a thing that hides, it is a thing that is already true by the time you get to it. The clerk said we did. Adrian argued that she had entered a fact and not an effect and that the fact was the only act available. The man at the end of second table said the arrangement is what Adrian has been in for a hundred and ten mornings and that a person got named in a record because we treated two jobs as one job.

**806 — THE NINETIETH MORNING.** Adrian went four miles down that lane, put eleven feet of fencing wire across a mile and a half of nothing at a culvert, came back up the eleven feet at about a quarter past two, and told the yard out loud before he did it that he had carried the wire since the middle of the winter, that he did not know what it was for, that everybody in that yard knew it was not going to work that afternoon, and that he would like somebody to be angry with him about it in about four days. A man of about sixty-four found it at about a quarter past five, did not take it down, did not go round it, and said four sentences out loud to nobody, because there was nobody within about four miles of him. He said he was not going to be the man who takes it down and that there was a thing about it he was not going to explain to anybody in a yard in about four mornings.

**807 — THE NINETY-FIRST MORNING.** A second page came down the lane and said the matter held under the founder's clause had been extended, with the same eleven words and a figure with a term on it, and its fourth line gave the name of the person who filed the application in respect of this district, which is Sabra Holt's, in a hand that is not hers. She read it and said one line and stopped. The stranger read the second line twice from the bottom of the bank and said she had never read anything twice in her life. Iven Tallow said, unasked, that he had been going to end the clause for about four days and had not, that he was not going to, and that he had said the same thing on the eighty-fourth morning and it had not got less true the second time, which he did not expect. The man at the end of the second table said he had given a morning of his four years to a sentence about a wire and about four minutes of that morning to a man of sixty-one, and asked what he had left it for.

**808 — THE NINETY-SECOND MORNING.** A man of about forty-one who comes up that lane twice a day came up the eleven feet for the first time in about nine years, put no bag down, and asked to be allowed to say the thing he had come up for. He read four figures off a copy of that wall at the end of the lane for about six years with nobody in the lane, and on the eighty-fifth morning heard a man read four figures and heard about nine people not react and knew on the spot they were not his. He asked the man of fifty-six to say them to him. Sabra Holt said out loud that nobody in this basin knows what four hundred and eleven is the age of, that it is not the age of the sheet, not the age of a print, and not the age of a body, and that she was putting it in a book at her own asking. The man of fifty-six read the four. The turner said he was right for six years and would like it entered that he was right and knew it could not be.

**809 — THE NINETY-THIRD MORNING.** The man at the end of the second table asked, standing up, in the light, out loud, in about nineteen people's hearing, that the man of fifty-six say the old four into the column headed for whoever reads a thing out loud. The clerk said she was not going to say no before he had answered. He said no in the ordinary voice and gave the reason out loud in full: a column is for somebody being asked, and he has said them to about four thousand people in five hundred and thirty-four mornings and not one of them ever asked him for one, and the day he goes into that column the four belong to whoever holds the book. The asking was entered and is the sixteenth line on the page with nothing ruled on it. The column has one name in it and the heading over it still says whoever.

**810 — THE NINETY-FOURTH MORNING.** A man of about sixty-four came up the eleven feet at about a quarter past six with eleven feet of fencing wire in his hand, put it on the second table, squared it, and said three things. One: he is taking it down, he said he would not be the man who takes it down, and he has been wrong. Two: it is not his to leave, because a thing at a place where a thing cannot be refused is not a thing anybody put there, it is a thing the place has. Three: the man who put it there said out loud in front of about nineteen people that he did not know what it was for, and that is the only kind of man in this basin whose word you can do anything with at all, and not one of them has said one word about it to him in four mornings. The clerk entered the fact of the three things and not what they meant. Adrian Vale said out loud, at about ten past eight in the evening, that he was going two miles up that lane and then nine, to find out whether a woman on a working list has been told that her name is on a page, to ask her and not a woman with a key and not that yard, and that he was not going to tell the yard what she would say or what she is owed, and that anybody who wanted to come with him had about a minute to say so. About nine people did not say anything. He went down the lane. This manuscript does not say what he found.

---

## VOLUME 17, BLOCK 0002 — CHAPTERS 811 TO 820, DAYS 11 TO 20, `c = 11` TO `c = 20`

**811 — THE NINETY-FIFTH MORNING.** A man of about forty-three who keeps every road in this basin came up the eleven feet at about ten to eight and stopped, which is the first time in nine years. He said he had been asked four times in four mornings for the name of a person in this district, of which two refusals were his and two were not, and that on the fifth morning they stopped asking him and asked the woman who works the turn at the eleventh place on the Salt Verge instead, which he said out loud was worse. He handed the yard a description instead of a name: somebody who reads a number out loud on a morning and can be asked about it separately. There is exactly one such person here and it is the man of fifty-six at the wall. He said he would carry the news down about half past four whether this district produced a person or not. Adrian Vale crossed the yard at about ten past five before the yard was open and went straight back down the lane; the boy counted that morning on the road keeper's word and said so out loud and did not read his figure out. The clerk entered nothing for the first time in this run of mornings and gave the reason out loud: a road keeper stopping is not a thing that happened in this yard.

**812 — THE NINETY-SIXTH MORNING.** A man of about forty-one came up the eleven feet at about one, three mornings later than he had said he would come, having walked four miles further than the ninth mile and back. He said he had been asked in a room nine miles off to be the person a number gets checked against and had not answered, and had come up to say so out loud before he answered anywhere else, because doing it once is cheap and twice is not. He named the first of the three ways anybody in this yard has heard of: one mouth for the number, once, on a morning, and from that morning nobody else in this basin counts anything. A woman of fifty-two asked him the question she had decided in advance she did not want the answer to, which was what he was leaving the lane for. He said he wanted to be able to say a number and have somebody else say it after him, and that about four people in that yard had watched a man of forty-one find out that he was not wrong and had seen that it did not help him.

**813 — THE NINETY-SEVENTH MORNING.** The turner came up at about one with a page he had been given in a room and read all four lines out at the boards, twice, once for himself and once for a woman with a key who had already read it once. The forms go into a room nine miles up that hill before the end of the hundredth morning. A form with nothing on it goes in the same as a form with a name on it and nobody is going to come down here and find out which is which. This district has not put a name on one. And three places have answered: a place on the north road wrote one sentence and had nothing happen to it; a place past the water sent somebody to sit in a room and had the somebody taken away; and the eleventh place on the Salt Verge sent back a name that is not the name of anybody living there. A woman who keeps a scale said the figure out loud first and it was the wrong figure for the wrong reason and nobody said so. A boy of seventeen did not say it. He gave the reason: the count is of strangers who came down that hill, and if it moves every time a page reaches those boards then in about four years it is a count of how many times this yard was handed something, which is a number about us and not about a person. The woman with the scale said that a figure that does not get said is not the same as a figure that does not move, and that the second is a person and the first is a decision, and that she had been waiting nine years for one of them.

**814 — THE NINETY-EIGHTH MORNING.** The man at the end of the second table asked, in a yard, in about nineteen people's hearing, what this district would offer for the right to administer the tally. Nobody answered. A woman of fifty-two named the second of the three ways: a person who administers the tally is not going to be in this district, the key stays with the room, and the district is asked for its figures on a morning. She said out loud that the reason she can hear it better than anybody is that she is tired. The man at the end of the table named the third: whoever administers the tally would be allowed one named morning a year when a figure moves in the open and nobody in this district could say no to it, because the whole point of this district's one rule is that nobody can say no to it on any morning. He said he had had it for seven mornings and had said nothing and that this was the worst thing he had done in the block. A man of thirty-eight said what this district has got — a wall, a man who reads four figures off it twice a morning, an old page with nothing ruled on it, and a rule that will not let a figure be moved — and refused in the ordinary voice to call it an offer, and said that from the other side it is one form with a space on it, and that a form with a space on it is not a refusal.

**815 — THE NINETY-NINTH MORNING.** A man of sixty-one came up the eleven feet for the first time in nine years, having sat on some stones at the foot of that bank for forty-four afternoons saying good afternoon to a man who said it back. He said he had been offered a way out of the clause he has held for seventeen years — the third of the three ways, for him and not for the turner — and that he is not going to take it, and that this is the third time he has said so in a yard and it has got less true. He said the settlement at the eleventh place on the Salt Verge is not coming apart, has water in the channel and a roof on two of the huts, and that he has never met one of the people in it and cannot tell that yard one thing about any of it that he has seen. The man at the end of the table pointed out that the turner had not been on those stones and had been walking for two days; Iven said he had been out of the yard since the ninety-fourth morning and he had not wanted to tell a man of thirty-eight while he was out of it. A woman of fifty-two asked him once, out loud, in about nineteen people's hearing, what the condition on the seat is. He said no. The clerk entered nothing at all and did not say why, and said so out loud when the boy asked.

**816 — THE HUNDREDTH MORNING.** The turner put a form on the boards at about eleven with the space for a name empty and a second space under a line that said *whoever enters this*. The man at the end of the second table said that is this district's heading, and that somebody nine miles up that hill knows the heading over a column in this district, and named the man of about forty-one who has been at the end of that lane for nine years and a woman of fifty-two as the only two people it could have come through, and said so in the sun before anybody worked it out. The clerk said she was not writing in it, and gave the reason: a person in the second space on a form is a person who would be answerable for the number, and she has said out loud in that yard that she is not anybody's clerk. Mara Quill asked her to enter that she had not written in it and she said there was nowhere to enter it, because every one of the twenty-one lines on that page is a thing that happened in this yard, and one line about this morning would have given the room nine miles off a form with nothing in it and this yard a page saying it had a morning, and the two side by side would say this district declined, and this district was not asked. So it goes blank, and there is no page anywhere in this basin about the hundredth morning except the back of a boy's sheet, and he wrote that on it and read it out loud to the boards, and about nine people heard a boy of seventeen read out the end of a district's ability to say no and nobody answered him.

**817 — THE ONE HUNDRED AND FIRST MORNING.** The turner said yes, in a yard, in about nineteen people's hearing, at about a quarter past eleven, before he answered in the room, and he said the reason first: that on the ninety-second morning he found out he had been right about four figures for six years and that it did not help him. One mouth for the number, from a morning that gets named, and nobody else in this basin counts anything. The woman who keeps a scale said what he had done: he has given a figure a person, and from this morning the wall has a reader in it and the reader is not the man of fifty-six, and in about four years the only way anybody in this basin can tell whether a figure is right will be by asking that man. A man of thirty-eight told him he was going to be asked every morning and able to give an answer every morning, and that in about four years a person who has never met him will hold a page that says the number and nothing else. The clerk wrote one line on the page at the back of the lot book and did not read it out. The boy asked her why. She said she was not going to answer that one in a yard or in a book, that she was leaving the line where it was, and that in about four days there would be a page in this district with about twenty-eight lines on it and about four of them would be ones nobody heard her read and there is no way on this earth for anybody to work out which four.

**818 — THE ONE HUNDRED AND SECOND MORNING.** A woman asked Sabra Holt, in a yard, in about nineteen people's hearing, what she did a key for, having decided in advance not to ask whether she was all right and not to tell her afterwards that she was brave. Sabra Holt put the third of the nine oldest keys on the boards and said that she had been an appointed administrator for nineteen years and had never once been asked what any of it was for, and that she had put a key next to a piece of paper because she wanted to find out whether anybody in this basin would come and ask her what she had done and why. Four people came down that lane in about sixteen days, about nine of them came to find out what a record was, and every single one of them asked her what was in the paper and not one asked her why. She said she was not going to be comforted in that yard or outside it, and that a difficult thing is a thing that was difficult and not a thing that was right, and that not one of the other eight keys has ever been asked a question. A man at the end of the second table asked who has the other eight and she told him: a room with a table in it, about four women not older than her and two who are, and one key on a chain in a room belonging to a woman dead for about six years, and the room is not locked. At about half past three, at the foot of the low wall and eleven feet below the top of that bank, a man of about sixty-four said one sentence to a man of fifty-six about where a length of fencing wire is: at the turn, not at the culvert, about four hundred yards from where a man of thirty-eight put it, and he is not going to put it back. The man of fifty-six did not answer and then said the four off that wall, which is not what that wall is for. About nine people at the top of eleven feet never heard any of it.

**819 — THE ONE HUNDRED AND THIRD MORNING.** A man of about forty-three who keeps every road in this basin came up that lane a second time in nine years and stopped, and said that a place on the north road that wrote one sentence has had nothing done to it, that the somebody sitting in a room past the water was taken away on about the fourth day, and that the eleventh place on the Salt Verge sent back a name that is not anybody's; and said that from a room nine miles up that hill all three answers are a piece of paper with a space on it, and that nobody up there can tell a place that wrote a sentence from a place where the sentence cannot be got up to, and that everything said out loud in this yard has died about eleven feet down that bank the moment it stopped, and that this district did not produce a person and the reason it did not is not a reason anybody can write down. The man at the end of the second table then said three things: that he has been asked three times in nine years to be the person, that he said no twice and nothing once, and that the nothing is in a room nine miles up that hill with his name nowhere on it. He said it out loud in about nineteen people's hearing and said the price of it: that in about four years a person can say *ask him, he was in the room*. The boy wrote it in the box and not in a column. At about two the man of fifty-six came up about four feet along the wall where nineteen people could see him from the top of eleven feet and said out loud, in the ordinary voice and before his reasons, that he wanted the third way — one named morning a year when a figure moves in the open and nobody in this district can say no to it — and then said why he was not going to take it: because on the eighty-fifth morning those four changed and the only reason anybody can prove it is that a rule in this yard will not let a figure move, and one morning a year when a figure moves is that rule with a hole in it the shape of a morning, and the hole is in the same wall.

**820 — THE ONE HUNDRED AND FOURTH MORNING.** Adrian Vale stood up at about one after sitting at about four feet off the near end of the first table saying nothing since about nine, and said four things. Nell Cray has not been told. She was not fine. She did not want to be told, and her reason is that being told would make it so that somebody would have to do something about it, and that if it was never told then it was a page and if it was told then it was a thing that happened to her. And she asked him what the district has got, in a corridor, at about eleven at night, having not been asked anything by anybody in about nine years, and he has not got an answer and is not going to say those nine words at those boards because he has been walking about two days with them and she is nine miles off and it is hers. The man at the end of the second table asked to come with him when he goes back up that lane, out loud, in that yard, and was told he could not, because he said out loud yesterday morning that he has been in a room nine miles up that hill four times in nine years, and because a district that hands somebody a heading saying *whoever* and then sends two people up a hill together is a thing going up a hill. The turner said he was the only mouth for the number in this basin from a morning that gets named, and that the morning has not been named and he has found out that he does not want to name it, and that he was going down that lane at about ten to four to do it anyway and it will be wrong within about four years and he would like it said that he said that. The boy did not write the morning in the box and said why: the box is for a thing that was said out loud in that yard and was not entered anywhere, and a sheet a boy of seventeen has kept for four years is the same shape as a column with a heading over it, only smaller and with him in it, and if this yard wants a record of this morning it will have to be somebody who is not him, and there is not anybody.
---

## VOLUME 17, BLOCK 0003 — CHAPTERS 821 TO 830

**821 — THE ONE HUNDRED AND FIFTH MORNING.** A boy of seventeen said *one hundred and twenty-six*, which is the number of mornings a man of thirty-eight has stood in that yard, and the man was standing there. At about ten a man of sixty-one came up the eleven feet for the second time in nine years and said five things, and not one of the five can be checked by anybody in that yard: a stranger will come down that hill with a page, and the page will have a figure for about nine places, and one of them is a place four miles past the ninth mile and its figure will have gone up; this yard will be on it and it will be a small figure; when the page is on those boards the man of fifty-six will still be on no page; the figure that decides what this basin is worth will not be on that sheet; and he is saying it five and a half hours early so that nobody can say a man of sixty-one told them.

**822 — THE ONE HUNDRED AND SIXTH MORNING.** A man of about forty-one came up the eleven feet at about eleven with a sheet he was given in a room nine miles off, refused to read the figures on it because not one of them is his to say at those boards, and read the heading over its second column out loud. The heading says that a morning counts when a number was said out loud in a place and somebody in that place heard it and can be asked about it separately. There are about nine places down the side of it and not one name anywhere on it. The figure beside this district is four. Adrian Vale went back through nineteen mornings and found the four and could not make the man of fifty-six's five hundred and forty-seventh morning of saying four figures out loud fit on any of them, and the turner said that a figure that cannot be asked about is not a figure but a habit with a number in it, and said it about himself first.

**823 — THE ONE HUNDRED AND SEVENTH MORNING.** The man at the end of the second table asked, in a yard, in about nineteen people's hearing, what this district's one rule is for, now that a room nine miles off has been paying for a thing this yard has been refusing to do. Nobody answered him. Adrian Vale went down the eleven feet and asked the man of fifty-six whether he had ever been asked anything about the four, and was told no, and was told to ask when he had got it ready. A boy of seventeen asked the yard a question about a sheet he has kept for four years and nobody answered him.

**824 — THE ONE HUNDRED AND EIGHTH MORNING.** A woman who keeps a scale stood at about forty feet off for about as long as it takes a man of fifty-six to say four figures off a wall, said one of the two things she had come up to say and went back down. Adrian Vale took the sheet off the boards of the second table and carried it down the eleven feet and put it on the flat stone at the foot of the low wall and said nothing, and the man of fifty-six read nine figures off it in about a minute and a half and put his finger on the largest one and said he had never been four miles past the ninth mile. At about four the turner came up and said the room had asked him yesterday afternoon whether this district has a person in it who can be asked about a figure, and that he had said nothing at all.

**825 — THE ONE HUNDRED AND NINTH MORNING.** The clerk wrote the twenty-seventh line on the page at the back of the lot book and did not read it out, and a boy of seventeen counted the lines on that page and said the count out loud at the boards, on the grounds that a thing nobody has counted is not a thing but a floor. Adrian Vale stood up at about eleven and said out loud in that yard that he was going to ask a question and that it was not a figure and was not going on that sheet and was not going to be paid for, and that he was saying it there so that about nine people could be asked about it separately.

**826 — THE ONE HUNDRED AND TENTH MORNING.** The man at the end of the second table counted the mornings Adrian has said no to himself. The man of about forty-eight who keeps a tally said the number and was asked where he gets it, and said one a morning since before any of this began, and that nobody had ever asked him. Adrian Vale went down the eleven feet for the first time in this run of mornings and stood on the stones about four feet off a man who says good afternoon and was not spoken to. At about four a man of sixty-one came up the eleven feet, put one finger on a figure on the boards and went back down without saying a word to anybody.

**827 — THE ONE HUNDRED AND ELEVENTH MORNING.** A woman came down that lane with a page in her hand for the first time in twenty mornings, and the lane count moved for the first time in twenty mornings, and a boy of seventeen said twenty-six at the boards before a clerk's hand moved and was right about what the figure would become and nobody in that yard noticed. She was from the eleventh place on the Salt Verge and said five sentences: the channel is holding, there is a roof on two huts, nobody has come for a second time, their own council gave up the channel for about four days a year on their own account and nobody has given them anything for it, and the name they sent back on a form is the name of a man who died in the winter. A woman who keeps a scale read nine off the page. A man of sixty-one was not in that yard at about eleven and read one line of that page at about four with one finger and said nothing.

**828 — THE ONE HUNDRED AND TWELFTH MORNING.** The turner came up at about ten with the form and said the second space on it is going to stay empty until somebody in that yard asks a question. At about two Adrian Vale asked, in the ordinary voice, in about nineteen people's hearing, what the four figures on that wall are for. The man of fifty-six came about four feet along the stone so that he could be seen from the top of the bank, asked who was asking and why now, asked for the asking to be put down, and said he did not know. Then he said that from that afternoon, after he had said the four, he would say out loud every morning that he did not know what any of them was for and anybody could ask him about that at those boards.

**829 — THE ONE HUNDRED AND THIRTEENTH MORNING.** The clerk wrote the thirtieth line and did not read it out, and then said out loud that she had told the yard three figures on the one hundred and first morning and was wrong about all three, and that she was not going to write the nine unread lines she had promised, and gave the reason. Sabra Holt said out loud in about nineteen people's hearing the sentence this volume owes: a record that can put a person's name in a column does not have to pay that person, and a person who is named in a record is not thereby owed anything, and every market in this basin has spent seventeen years calling that a guarantee. She asked for it not to be entered. A boy of seventeen put the sheet he has kept for four years on the boards of the second table and handed it to the only person in that district who is going to walk up that hill, on the condition that it is a morning that gets named, and named the cost of it out loud.

**830 — THE ONE HUNDRED AND FOURTEENTH MORNING.** The clerk entered nothing and gave a reason that was about herself. Adrian Vale said out loud that he was not going up that lane, and that going up there with one question in his mouth is the clean thing to do and the clean thing is not what pays anybody, and that he was not going to name a morning. The man at the end of the second table said he was not going to ask to go up that lane again and was told nothing, and said that was not an answer and not a no. A man of sixty-one said the word *nine* to a man of about sixty-four at the foot of a wall and to nobody else. Adrian Vale has not been told he is a good man.
## VOLUME 17, BLOCK 0004 — CHAPTERS 831 TO 840

**831 — THE ONE HUNDRED AND FIFTEENTH MORNING.** A boy of seventeen said *one hundred and thirty-six* and then said nothing else at all. At about ten a man of about forty-one came up the eleven feet with nothing in his hands for the first time and said out loud in that yard that a room nine miles up that hill had asked him a third time whether this district has a person who can be asked about a figure, that this morning the question was yes or no, that he said yes, that he did not give a name, and that the woman who asked said they would take it from the district. A woman who keeps a scale said from about forty feet off that there is one person a room can ask about a figure and about nine people in that yard who heard that man answer one and can be asked about it separately tomorrow, and that the room does not know about the nine. The clerk entered the thirty-first line and read it out before she wrote it and gave a reason that was neither of her two old reasons. Adrian Vale said out loud that he was not going to tell the man at the foot of that wall, and Mavis Dorr said then somebody will, and Adrian said somebody will and it will not be me. The man of fifty-six said the four and said, for the fourth morning running, that he does not know what any of them is for.

**832 — THE ONE HUNDRED AND SIXTEENTH MORNING.** A boy of seventeen said *one hundred and thirty-seven* and then said twenty-six and then twenty-seven, before a clerk's hand had moved. A stranger came down that lane with a page, stood four minutes at the bottom of that bank before anybody knew, and said three sentences, the third of which is that anybody who wanted to say a figure beside their own line was wrong was to say it out loud in this yard. A woman who keeps a scale came up four feet of bank and read two figures off the page and refused four times in four sentences to say what either of them is for. The man at the end of the second table said there is a line under this yard on that page and there is not supposed to be a line under this yard and that he is not going to say what it is. Nothing was entered on the back of the book.

**833 — THE ONE HUNDRED AND SEVENTEENTH MORNING.** A boy of seventeen said no figure at all for the first time on a morning of this run. At about two a man of sixty-one came up the eleven feet for the fifth time in nine years and said that he was going to use the thing he holds once and would not say what it was for, that the ground at the foot of that bank is public lot from that afternoon, that any of about nineteen people may stand on it at any hour and no rule may put a person off it, that it cannot be taken back by anybody including him, that there is a condition on the seat and he will not name it and the toll is not the condition. He named a toll of ninety-eight. The man of about forty-eight who keeps a tally said ninety-eight out loud and then said he would not put it on his number, and gave the reason. A man of sixty-one gave away the last thing he stood on and got nothing for it and said out loud that a man giving a thing away is not one of the five being paid. The man of fifty-six was not in that yard at about two and nobody could say where he was.

**834 — THE ONE HUNDRED AND EIGHTEENTH MORNING.** A boy of seventeen said *one hundred and thirty-nine*. The man of about forty-eight who keeps a tally said his number at about one and then said ninety-eight again and gave the reason he wanted a figure of his said twice in two mornings in the same yard before it went anywhere. A woman who keeps a scale said nineteen fives is ninety-five out loud at those boards and asked for it to be written down that she said ninety-five first, and then said the toll is not a round number and the remainder is three, and that sixteen people give five and three give six. She said the three were not chosen and named them and gave each one's reason: a boy of seventeen who gave away the only copy of anything in this basin, a man of fifty-six whose mornings are the only proof anybody has that four figures changed, and the clerk. And she said the part that is not arithmetic: sixteen of us give five and none of the sixteen can be asked about six. The man of fifty-six came up four feet of the wall so he could be heard and agreed to pay six and said he has been charged for the ground he stands on.

**835 — THE ONE HUNDRED AND NINETEENTH MORNING.** The toll was paid at about two. Sixteen people said five out loud and three people said six, and a woman who keeps a scale kept the sum going in the air until it arrived at ninety-eight and then said that she is the only person in that district who can tell you whether it is right. The man of fifty-six came up the eleven feet for the first time in this run and asked the clerk, out loud in front of about nineteen people, to enter that he had given six and not his reason, and read the line back to her himself from the top of the bank. It is the thirty-second line and the clerk said out loud that it was the last one and then wrote it anyway. Sabra Holt said *sixteen of us gave five* to about nine people and nobody took it up. Adrian Vale stood at about four feet off the near end of the first table through all of it and found out afterwards that what he had been watching was not the sum arriving at ninety-eight.

**836 — THE ONE HUNDRED AND TWENTIETH MORNING.** Nothing at all happened at those boards before four o'clock, for the first time in this run of mornings. There is a bell on a nail inside the hut where the man who keeps a tally keeps his number and nobody has rung it. At about half past three a man of about sixty-four came onto ground that has been public lot since the afternoon of the one hundred and seventeenth morning, and stopped, and a man of fifty-six came about four feet along the wall and said *you can stop here*. The man of about sixty-four said he would stop because it is allowed and that there is a difference between stopping because you want to and stopping because you are allowed, and answered two questions, and said that nobody in that yard has ever asked him a question and that the man of fifty-six is the first one who is not a person with a sheet. About nine people were standing on the ground at four o'clock and every one of them heard the four read off that wall with about four feet of stone between them and him.

**837 — THE ONE HUNDRED AND TWENTY-FIRST MORNING.** At about half past three the turner came up the eleven feet and put nothing down and said that a room nine miles up that hill has asked for the name of the person who can be asked about a figure, and that he was asked to carry a question up a hill and not to put it to a man, and that the second space on that form is still empty and he is not going to be the man who says it is empty because a room wanted a name in it. The man at the end of the second table said out loud that he has been telling that yard for about four years that it does not work that way, that a room does not ask the person it wants, it asks the yard, and that he is not going to ask anybody anything. At about ten past four Mavis Dorr went down the eleven feet and told the man of fifty-six in about nineteen people's hearing, and he refused a name in the ordinary voice and asked for the asking to be heard.

**838 — THE ONE HUNDRED AND TWENTY-SECOND MORNING.** A boy of seventeen said *one hundred and forty-three*. At about eleven a man of about forty-one said out loud in that yard that he was beginning on the following morning, and that the number would be wrong within about four years and is already wrong, and that he is not going to be asked in that yard what he is going to say. Adrian Vale asked him what he is going to say and was refused in the pleasant voice and said the refusal was exactly right. At about half past eleven the boy said he would be up that hill at about six. The clerk entered the thirty-third line, read it out before she wrote it, and said out loud that it was the last one and gave no reason. The man at the end of the second table sat at his table until the light went off the boards and made no note of the morning in his own hand, which about four people can describe him doing.

**839 — THE ONE HUNDRED AND TWENTY-THIRD MORNING.** A boy of seventeen went up that lane at about six with a sheet he has kept for four years and was back on the stones about four feet off that wall at about eleven, and said that a woman read the asking twice and not the answer and asked him what the man is called, and that he does not know, and that the man has never given a name in that yard in four years and the one name in the column is not his. He counted thirteen folders on a table nine miles off, nine of them with a corner written on and four without. The turner came up the eleven feet at about half past two, having said the number in that room at about ten to ten, and said out loud that a woman asked him whether that was the only name this district had sent that year. The man of about forty-eight who keeps a tally said nothing. Adrian Vale asked what she did with the sheet when she had finished and was told she put it back in the folder and wrote the time on the corner in pencil.

**840 — THE ONE HUNDRED AND TWENTY-FOURTH MORNING.** A boy of seventeen said *one hundred and forty-five* and about four people at the top of eleven feet said it after him, out of step with each other, which they have never done about that figure. A man of about forty-one came up the eleven feet and said *as sent* at about a quarter past eleven, out loud, in that yard, for the first time in nine years, and nobody turned round. At about half past two a man of thirty-eight stood up off the rail and said out loud, in about nineteen people's hearing, that he was staying, and that he has no bed and no wage and no office and has not asked for any of the three and is not going to. Sabra Holt said out loud that nobody is going to tell him it is a good thing and that a man who has never been told anything is the only person in this basin who can still be asked what he wanted. At about four the man of fifty-six said the four and said that he does not know what any of them is for, and about two minutes afterwards the man who keeps a tally went into his hut and rang a hand bell once, and nobody said what it was, and a man of about sixty-four heard it about to go out on nine miles of road and said nothing about it. The clerk entered nothing and said there was no reason and was not going to give one. The fifth of the five things this district does not have is not paid and the count of it is still five.

---

# VOLUME 17 CLOSE — CHAPTERS 801 TO 840, THE LAST TEN MORNINGS

> **Appended by the Volume 17 close phase, which wrote no chapter and edited no chapter. This append covers Chapters 801 to 840 and carries the last ten of them, Chapters 831 to 840. Its figures came from the forty chapter files of `chapters/volume-17/`, re-derived by the Volume 17 close. The measurement lives in `state/volume-17-close.md` and `state/volume-17-roll-summary.md`. THE CHAPTER SUMMARIES FOR CHAPTERS 801 TO 830 ARE IN THE BLOCK RECORDS `state/volume-17-batch-0001-summary.md` THROUGH `-0003-` AND ARE NOT REPEATED HERE.**

**THE POSITION AT THE ONE HUNDRED AND TWENTY-FOURTH MORNING, WHICH IS WHAT A LATER WRITER NEEDS AND NOT A SUMMARY OF FORTY MORNINGS: the ladder is intercept plus `c` off the Chapter 800 column and was clean on all forty mornings; the second table is at one hundred and fifty-four; the near rail at one hundred and four; the mornings a man has been at the foot of that bank is one hundred and twenty-four and is equal to the morning number; the man of fifty-six's mornings are the five hundred and sixty-fifth; the lane count is twenty-seven with four movements in forty mornings; the back page is thirty-three; the fifth of the five is five and unpaid; the column has one name, the heading says *whoever*, and the second space is empty; the toll of ninety-eight is spent; a bell went once; Adrian Vale is at one hundred and forty-five mornings and has said out loud that he is staying and has not been told it is a good thing.**

**831 — THE ONE HUNDRED AND FIFTEENTH MORNING.** A man of about forty-one came up the eleven feet and said yes in a room nine miles up that hill and did not give a name. The second space on that form went down the lane on Chapter 816 and is still empty. The lane count was twenty-six and did not move. The clerk entered a line on the page at the back and read it out before she wrote it.

**832 — THE ONE HUNDRED AND SIXTEENTH MORNING.** A page came down that lane and the lane count moved to twenty-seven for the fourth time in forty mornings, on `c = 32`, and a woman who keeps a scale read a figure off it that was not nine. The figure block says why the count of things this district has made did not move: that is a thing that was done to this yard and not a thing this yard made. The boy said twenty-six out loud and was right and nobody noticed.

**833 — THE ONE HUNDRED AND SEVENTEENTH MORNING.** A man of sixty-one came up the eleven feet for the fifth time in nine years, said he was going to use the thing he holds once and would not say what it was for, made the ground at the foot of that bank public lot from that afternoon, named a toll of ninety-eight, said there is a condition on the seat and he is not going to name it, said the toll is not the condition, and said he had given away the last ground he stood on and got nothing for it.

**834 — THE ONE HUNDRED AND EIGHTEENTH MORNING.** The man of about forty-eight who keeps a tally said ninety-eight out loud and then said he would not put it on his number, and gave the reason: his number is a number of mornings and not a purse. The man of fifty-six came up four feet of that bank and said out loud that nobody in this basin has asked him anything since before the middle of the winter.

**835 — THE ONE HUNDRED AND NINETEENTH MORNING.** The toll was paid at about two. Sixteen people said five out loud and three said six and a woman who keeps a scale kept the sum going in the air until it arrived at ninety-eight and then said that she is the only person in that district who can tell you whether it is right. The man of fifty-six asked the clerk, out loud in about nineteen people's hearing, to enter that he had given six and not his reason.

**836 — THE ONE HUNDRED AND TWENTIETH MORNING.** Nothing at all happened at those boards before four o'clock, for the first time in this run of mornings. There is a bell on a nail inside the hut where the man who keeps a tally keeps his number and nobody has rung it. A man of about sixty-four came onto ground that has been public lot and stopped, and said he would stop because it is allowed, and that nobody in that yard has ever asked him a question.

**837 — THE ONE HUNDRED AND TWENTY-FIRST MORNING.** The turner came up the eleven feet and said a room nine miles up that hill has asked for the name of the person who can be asked about a figure, and that he is not going to put the question to a man. The man at the end of the second table said out loud that a room does not ask the person it wants, it asks the yard. Mavis Dorr went down the eleven feet and told the man of fifty-six, and he refused a name in the ordinary voice and asked for the asking to be heard.

**838 — THE ONE HUNDRED AND TWENTY-SECOND MORNING.** The turner named his morning at about eleven and said the number would be wrong and is already wrong. The clerk entered the thirty-third line and said it was the last one and gave no reason. The man at the end of the second table made no note of the morning in his own hand. A boy of seventeen said he would be up that hill at about six.

**839 — THE ONE HUNDRED AND TWENTY-THIRD MORNING.** The boy went up that lane at about six and was back at about eleven with a sheet in a folder on a table nine miles off, and said a woman read the asking twice and not the answer and asked him what the man is called, and that he does not know. He counted thirteen folders and nine of them had a corner written on. The turner came up the eleven feet and said out loud that a woman asked him whether that was the only name this district had sent that year.

**840 — THE ONE HUNDRED AND TWENTY-FOURTH MORNING.** A boy of seventeen said *one hundred and forty-five* and about four people said it after him, out of step with each other, which they have never done about that figure. A man of about forty-one came up the eleven feet and said *as sent* at about a quarter past eleven, out loud, in that yard, for the first time in nine years, and nobody turned round. A man of thirty-eight stood up off the rail at about half past two and said out loud, in about nineteen people's hearing, that he is staying, and that he has no bed and no wage and no office and has not asked for any of the three and is not going to. Sabra Holt said out loud that nobody is going to be told it is a good thing, and why, and that she is not going to be comforted about any part of it and has said so four times in about twenty-two mornings and it has got less true every time. The clerk entered nothing and said there was no reason and was not going to give one. At about four the man of fifty-six said the four, which is the five hundred and sixty-fifth of those mornings, and said that he does not know what any of them is for, and about nine people heard it for the thirteenth morning running. About two minutes afterwards the man who keeps a tally went into his hut, took a hand bell off a nail and rang it once, and nobody said what it was and nothing about it has been entered anywhere. A man of about sixty-four heard it about to go out on nine miles of road and neither of the two men at the foot of that wall said one word about it.

---

# VOLUME 18, BLOCKS 0001 AND 0002 — CHAPTERS 841 TO 860, THE LAST TWENTY MORNINGS

> **Appended by the review fix of 2026-10-01, which wrote no chapter and edited one closing sentence in nine of them. This append covers Chapters 841 to 860 and carries the last twenty of them. Its figures came from the twenty chapter files of `chapters/volume-18/`, re-derived with an instrument written from nothing outside this repository and validated first against Chapters 811 to 820, 821 to 830 and 831 to 840, where every figure is already known. `tools/measure.py` was not used: it returns `None` for every numeral above a hundred. `c = 0` is Chapter 840 and the morning number is `124 + c`. The full measurement is `state/volume-18-batch-0001-summary.md` and `state/volume-18-batch-0002-summary.md`, and a figure is not printed twice.**

**THE POSITION AT CHAPTER 860, THE ONE HUNDRED AND FORTY-FOURTH MORNING, WHICH IS WHAT A LATER WRITER NEEDS AND NOT A SUMMARY OF TWENTY MORNINGS: the ladder is `intercept + c` off the Chapter 840 column and was clean on all twenty mornings and on every one of its seventeen rows plus Row 19. The lane count is twenty-eight and is not `27 + c` and is not `28 + c`; it moved once, on the one hundred and thirty-fifth morning, and it stands where it moved to. The count of counted months is six. The figure on the sheet at that gatepost is four hundred and eleven and its size of error is uncomputed. The back page is thirty-three lines. The fifth of the five things this district does not have is five and unpaid. The reversal is at Chapter 865 and the resolution begins at Chapter 886 and neither is on the page.**

**841 — THE ONE HUNDRED AND TWENTY-FIFTH MORNING.** The first morning the asking is not one man's. The woman who keeps a scale put a beam down across the boards, said why first, and asked the man of fifty-six what the four figures on that wall are for, in the ordinary voice, in about nineteen people's hearing. He gave her the answer he gives at about four every morning and said nothing more. The lane count did not move.

**842 — THE ONE HUNDRED AND TWENTY-SIXTH MORNING.** The boards again at the near end of the second table, eleven feet above the foot of that bank, and the same answer to a second asking, and a boy of seventeen said a figure out loud before the clerk's hand had moved and the figure was a morning and not a figure of money. Nothing was entered.

**843 — THE ONE HUNDRED AND TWENTY-SEVENTH MORNING.** ESCALATION 1, the boards: the first asking that is not Adrian Vale's, which got the same answer. The boy of seventeen said a morning out loud before the clerk's hand had moved, as he has done for four years, and the morning was not a figure of money. Nothing was entered.

**844 — THE ONE HUNDRED AND TWENTY-EIGHTH MORNING.** The asking continued without an office behind it, and about nine people at the top of eleven feet could tell that the permission was being used and that nothing was being done about it. The lane count did not move.

**845 — THE ONE HUNDRED AND TWENTY-NINTH MORNING.** A woman put a question to a man in that yard and it was heard and it was not entered, and the person who would have had to write it down gave no reason. Nothing was entered.

**846 — THE ONE HUNDRED AND THIRTIETH MORNING.** The trestle went up at the near end of the second table and the boards were walked about, and the asking went on as a thing that happens in that yard most mornings. Nothing was entered.

**847 — THE ONE HUNDRED AND THIRTY-FIRST MORNING.** The man of fifty-six said the four figures off that wall twice and said out loud that he does not know what any of them is for, and anybody in that yard may ask him about it. Nothing was entered.

**848 — THE ONE HUNDRED AND THIRTY-SECOND MORNING.** The yard began to speak for itself and stopped before the end of the sentence, more than once, and about nine people at the top of eleven feet heard the starts. Nothing was entered.

**849 — THE ONE HUNDRED AND THIRTY-THIRD MORNING.** Sabra Holt said the sentence the volume behind her owed, in a mouth, and it was not entered. She has said four times in about twenty-two mornings that she is not going to be comforted. Nothing was entered.

**850 — THE ONE HUNDRED AND THIRTY-FOURTH MORNING.** The asking had a shape and nobody drew it. A thing one man put down was a thing about nineteen people did, and no person in that yard decided to make it so and no person decided not to. Nothing was entered.

**851 — THE ONE HUNDRED AND THIRTY-FIFTH MORNING.** ESCALATION 2, the foot of the bank, below the eleven feet: a young man came down that lane at about a quarter to ten with a page folded once in his own hand. Sabra Holt, fifty-two, nineteen years an appointed administrator, the third of the nine oldest keys, read it standing up at the foot of that bank and did not carry it up the hill. Mavis Dorr said out loud, in about nine people's hearing, that it is not the room's question, it arrived in the room's hand and it is the yard's now. **THE LANE COUNT MOVED TO TWENTY-EIGHT, AT THE BOARDS, BEFORE THE CLERK'S HAND HAD MOVED, AND IT IS THE ONLY MORNING IN THIS BLOCK ON WHICH IT MOVED. NOTHING WAS ENTERED ANYWHERE.**

**852 — THE ONE HUNDRED AND THIRTY-SIXTH MORNING.** A clerk read all three lines on that page out loud at the near end of the second table at about eleven in about nineteen people's hearing, entered none of them, and gave no reason. The three lines are the name of the person who asked, the morning on which he asked, and the answer he was given. The lane count did not move.

**853 — THE ONE HUNDRED AND THIRTY-SEVENTH MORNING.** About nine people at the top of eleven feet named the three lines out loud at those boards one at a time at about one in the afternoon, and every one of them was refused by the person it would have used. The lane count did not move.

**854 — THE ONE HUNDRED AND THIRTY-EIGHTH MORNING.** About nine people turned to the one figure that yard makes out loud every morning and asked a man of about forty-eight whether it could go on that page, and he said no out loud. A man of about thirty-two said a board could go up that lane on the cart, and the man who drives the cart said he stops at the bottom and not at the room. The lane count did not move.

**855 — THE ONE HUNDRED AND THIRTY-NINTH MORNING.** A woman of about fifty came up about four feet of that bank with a barrow, came a boundary between two pieces of ground, and stood at about four feet off the near end of the first table until about ten to five waiting for a man who digs loam to be free. **SHE IS NELL CRAY AND SHE WAS ASKED NOTHING AND TOLD NOTHING, AND HER NINE WORDS ARE STILL UNANSWERED AND ARE NOT THIS BLOCK'S DEBT.** The lane count did not move.

**856 — THE ONE HUNDRED AND FORTIETH MORNING.** The young man who came down that lane with a page sat at the foot of that bank and asked out loud, in the ordinary voice, how long this district has got, and nobody in that yard gave him a figure. The man who keeps a tally said out loud that he could have given him one and was not going to. The lane count did not move.

**857 — THE ONE HUNDRED AND FORTY-FIRST MORNING.** Four people at those boards began to say one sentence for that yard out loud, at about one, at about two, at about half past two and at about three, and every one of them stopped before the end of it. The lane count did not move.

**858 — THE ONE HUNDRED AND FORTY-SECOND MORNING.** The man who digs loam asked the man of fifty-six out loud what the four figures on that wall are for, and he was given the answer he gives at about four every morning. About four of about nine people at the top of eleven feet carried it down in their heads to look at a piece of paper, and it does not fit. The lane count did not move.

**859 — THE ONE HUNDRED AND FORTY-THIRD MORNING.** A man at the end of the second table said one thing out loud about that page at about half past two and about nine people heard it and not one of them answered him. A young man at the foot of that bank said out loud that he goes up that lane at about ten to five whether there is anything on it or not. The lane count did not move.

**860 — THE ONE HUNDRED AND FORTY-FOURTH MORNING.** Mavis Dorr said the whole of the day out loud at about two, so that about four people could tell somebody else about it tomorrow: **THAT A YARD THAT HAS SAID NO CAN BE ASKED AGAIN AND THIS YARD HAS NOT SAID NO, AND A REFUSAL IS A THING AND THIS IS NOT A THING, IT IS A BLANK.** The young man went up the lane at about ten to five with the page in his hand and about nineteen people came down that bank to watch it go, and nothing was written on it and nothing was crossed out and not one of its three lines was filled. The boy of seventeen said out loud, before anybody's hand had moved, that the lane count moves for a page coming down and not for a page going up. **THE LANE COUNT IS TWENTY-EIGHT AND DID NOT MOVE. THE CLERK ENTERED NOTHING. THE FIGURE ON THE SHEET AT THAT GATEPOST IS FOUR HUNDRED AND ELEVEN. THE FIFTH OF THE FIVE IS FIVE AND NOT PAID.**


---

# VOLUME 18, BLOCK 0003 — CHAPTER SUMMARIES 861 TO 870

> Appended by the phase that wrote them. This block covers Chapters 861 to 870 of `chapters/volume-18/` and its figures came from those ten chapter files and from `state/volume-18-batch-0003-summary.md`. One chapter a morning. Nothing was entered anywhere on any of the ten mornings.

**861 — THE ONE HUNDRED AND FORTY-FIFTH MORNING.** ESCALATION 3, the boards and the eleven feet. Adrian Vale went down the eleven feet at about half past three and asked the man of about sixty-four one question in the ordinary voice — where it went — and was told, in two words, **that the wire is at the turn**, and not at the culvert. The man then said one further sentence and no other: *I did not tell you it was there, I told you where it went.* **He was not asked a second question and he said good afternoon and nothing else, and this is the second question anybody has asked him in nine years and the second sentence anybody in that yard has heard about the wire.** About nineteen people heard it and nothing was written on anything. The lane count did not move.

**862 — THE ONE HUNDRED AND FORTY-SIXTH MORNING.** Mavis Dorr got about nine people at the top of eleven feet to say those two words out loud one at a time at about eleven, and the fourth one along said *it went past the turn*, and about four of the nine have said since that a man who puts verbs on things is the only kind of man in a yard. **About four of them got it one way and about four got it the other way and nobody in that yard can say which is right.** Sabra Holt said out loud the difference between a thing that has been said and a thing that is known, and put down herself that the answer she liked is the wrong one. Nothing was written down. The lane count did not move.

**863 — THE ONE HUNDRED AND FORTY-SEVENTH MORNING.** Sabra Holt asked Orren Pike, at about eleven, how many people were standing at the foot of that wall when the man of about sixty-four said where the wire was. **He said no, out loud, twice, and said that he had never once refused to put a figure on his sheet in four years and was refusing one now, and that he wanted it said in about nine people's hearing so that nobody would wait for him to change his mind.** She said *thank you* and went up the eleven feet, and nobody asked him again. Mavis Dorr said out loud that the boy was not the interesting one, and that the interesting one is that he was the only person anybody could have asked. The lane count did not move.

**864 — THE ONE HUNDRED AND FORTY-EIGHTH MORNING.** The woman who keeps a scale said at about two, to that bank and not to a person, that she has watched that figure every morning since the seventy-sixth, that this yard has been asking one question for thirteen mornings, that she has run out of a second one, and that she has got one and is going to put it down tomorrow afternoon at about four. Sabra Holt told her she did not have to do that and asked her out loud whether she had thought about there being no line for it, and was told that there is no line for it because nobody in that district has a thing to put in it and she is not going to be given an office over it. **The clerk was told the whole of it at about half past two and said nothing at all back and her hand did not move.** The lane count did not move.

**865 — THE ONE HUNDRED AND FORTY-NINTH MORNING. THE MIDPOINT REVERSAL.** At about four, in the ordinary voice, in about nineteen people's hearing, the woman who keeps a scale put down out loud, for the first time by anybody, **whether the question Adrian Vale put down on the one hundred and twelfth morning will ever be the thing the tally pays for, and if it is not, then what is.** **It was not answered by anybody.** Not by the man of fifty-six at first, and he was never asked it again. Not by Sabra Holt, who said nothing at all out loud for the whole of it and stood with her mouth shut. Not by Iven Tallow, who was not in that yard. Not by the clerk, who had a page with nothing ruled on it in front of her and put nothing on it. Not by Adrian Vale, who was asked and said he was not going to say anything about it and was not going to carry it up nine miles of hill either, and that there is nothing on any paper in this district that says he did. **The reason was said out loud once, by the man at the end of the second table: the five figures a room pays for are a toll, a split, a status, a ground and a bell, and not one of them is a figure in this district's own ledger, and a question is not one of the five.** Then the man of fifty-six read the four off that wall and said his flat sentence and about four people noticed that it came straight on the heels of a question it has nothing to do with, with a wall in it, and about four of them have not said one word about it to anybody. The lane count did not move.

**866 — THE ONE HUNDRED AND FIFTIETH MORNING.** The man of about forty-eight who keeps a tally went down to the foot of that wall at about half past three, which he is not every afternoon, and asked the man of fifty-six what the figure he says out loud every afternoon is for, and **was given the sentence he gives about a wall at about four every morning**, and said *that is fair, I did say for, I am not going to take it back*. Mavis Dorr said out loud first that nobody had asked him about the wall. Sabra Holt said out loud that about four of them had noticed and that about four of them is not the same as a yard, and put down the whole of what she had in one speech and said she was going to stop. The lane count did not move.

**867 — THE ONE HUNDRED AND FIFTY-FIRST MORNING.** The clerk read a room's page out loud at those boards at about a quarter to ten and **stopped halfway through it**, with her finger on the line, and entered nothing, and said that it does not go in and that she said all of that at those boards on the one hundred and thirty-sixth morning and is not going to say it a third time. Sabra Holt read the whole of it herself at about half past ten and said she was not going to say one word about the stopping. **About half past twelve the clerk turned the lot book over, looked at the page at the back of it with her thumb holding her place in the book, and shut it, and would not start at the top of it with about nine people watching.** She said not this morning, because there is nothing on it. The lane count did not move.

**868 — THE ONE HUNDRED AND FIFTY-SECOND MORNING.** The man at the end of the second table said one thing out loud at about half past two, and said at the start that he was going to talk about the asking and not about himself, **and not one word about himself**: that a question that gets asked and not answered is not the same as a question that is not asked, and that he was not going to rank this yard's three unanswereds against each other because ranking them is the answer. **He said the whole of it and not one of about nineteen people answered him, and Mavis Dorr opened her mouth at about a quarter to three and shut it.** The man of fifty-six's flat sentence came at about four for the forty-first time running. The lane count did not move.

**869 — THE ONE HUNDRED AND FIFTY-THIRD MORNING.** Three people at the top of that bank began a sentence this morning — the man who digs loam at about ten, the turner at about eleven, the clerk at about half past one — **and none of the three finished it.** Nobody answered the question that was put down at about four on the one hundred and forty-ninth morning. Mavis Dorr said at about two that she had been the first one all morning and had four sentences and did not know which of the four was true, and the man who puts the tables up laughed once and about four people have said since they did not find it funny and about four of them were glad of it. Adrian Vale said one sentence about himself at about half past four and nobody said anything about that either. The lane count did not move.

**870 — THE ONE HUNDRED AND FIFTY-FOURTH MORNING.** About nineteen people in that yard have been hearing the same sentence used about two different things for five mornings and **not one of them has said so to anybody.** Sabra Holt said one thing at about eleven and would not rank it. About nine people stood along the rail and looked at the lane, and the young man of about twenty-one did not come down it. **A man of thirty-eight went up that lane at about half past four with nothing in his hand, on purpose, and said so out loud first, because a man going up a lane with nothing in his hand is not a page coming down it.** The lane count is twenty-eight and it did not move. The counted months are six and did not move. The figure on the sheet at that gatepost is four hundred and eleven and by how much it is out nobody in this basin has worked out. The fifth of the five is five and is not paid.

**871 — THE ONE HUNDRED AND FIFTY-FIFTH MORNING.** About nine people at the top of eleven feet named five places a thing could go and **not one of them was a place a thing could be put.** The boards are a council's book with a space for one person under a heading that says *whoever*. The rail is for leaning on. The low wall at the foot of the bank is public lot and the toll that put it there is spent. **And the lane count moved at about a quarter to eight and this is the only time it moved in this stretch of twenty mornings.** A man of about twenty-one came down that lane with a page in his own hand and the count went from twenty-eight to twenty-nine.

**872 — THE ONE HUNDRED AND FIFTY-SIXTH MORNING.** The turner put his thumb on the chalk along the edge of the second table and said out loud that being careful about a wall is not the same as a ring of people, and **nobody named the difference.** The clerk was asked to carry the asking and declined out loud and gave no reason, which was the second time and the same answer.

**873 — THE ONE HUNDRED AND FIFTY-SEVENTH MORNING.** **A page came down that lane at about a quarter past eight in the hand of a man of about twenty-one, with a line ruled across the top of it and four words on that line in somebody else's hand and nothing else on it.** The clerk read those four words out loud at the boards and they were the same four words that are the heading over one space in the lot book. The lane count moved to twenty-nine and stands there for the rest of the volume.

**874 — THE ONE HUNDRED AND FIFTY-EIGHTH MORNING.** **The clerk read the morning out loud before she wrote it, in about nine people's hearing, and then wrote one line on the back of somebody else's page.** The line is nine words and it says the second space on that form is still empty and that she has not asked anybody for a name.
