It is one line and not the three, and it is not on the page at the back of the lot book.

**875 — THE ONE HUNDRED AND FIFTY-NINTH MORNING.** **The page went back up that lane at about half past four in the inside coat of the man who brought it down, and about nineteen people watched it go and not one of them said a word while he was doing it.** The lane count did not move for it, because a page going up that hill is not a page coming down it.

**876 — THE ONE HUNDRED AND SIXTIETH MORNING.** At about eleven the woman who keeps a scale put a sheet of paper on her own scale and took it off again and said nothing to anybody. **Mavis Dorr asked her why in the ordinary voice, in about nineteen people's hearing, and she did not answer.** She has said nothing out loud to anybody since the one hundred and forty-ninth morning. The first table was bare.

**877 — THE ONE HUNDRED AND SIXTY-FIRST MORNING.** At about twelve a boy of seventeen was asked in about nineteen people's hearing what the mark in his own sheet against the one hundred and thirty-third morning is for, **and he said *that is not for you*, and he has never in four years refused to put a figure on that sheet.** The man at the end of the second table said the same sentence at about four for the fifty-first morning running and was not asked one question.

**878 — THE ONE HUNDRED AND SIXTY-SECOND MORNING.** At about ten a man of about forty-eight who keeps a tally asked a woman how many people in that yard have noticed that one sentence has been used about two different things. **She said out loud that she was not going to count it, in front of about nineteen people so that it could not be said afterwards that she was asked quietly, and no figure came of it.** Not because it is a secret. Because a number about people in this district goes on a page.

**879 — THE ONE HUNDRED AND SIXTY-THIRD MORNING.** At about half past ten a man of about forty-one asked the clerk whether one line written on the back of somebody else's page could go into the book that belongs to a council. **She gave three reasons and the second one was that about nine people looked at the heading over one space, and somebody said *ask*, and about nineteen people stood there and not one of them asked.** The boards were bare for the third morning running.

**880 — THE ONE HUNDRED AND SIXTY-FOURTH MORNING.** **The clerk said out loud, in about nineteen people's hearing, that if she says yes then this yard has a rule and if she says no then this yard has a rule, and a rule is the one thing ten mornings have not had, and she was not going to say either.** At about ten past four a man of thirty-eight said he is going up that lane in the morning with nothing in his hand and not so that anybody knows, **and that he has been up that hill once, ten mornings ago, with nothing in his hand, and about four people know that.** The lane count is twenty-nine and has not moved since the one hundred and fifty-seventh morning.

**881 — THE ONE HUNDRED AND SIXTY-FIFTH MORNING.** **A man of thirty-eight went up that lane before it was light with nothing in his hand and was not at the foot of that bank at about ten.** The man who digs loam went past the bottom of that lane with his barrow and said so out loud, and about four people at the top of eleven feet said it after him out of step with each other. Nothing came down that lane.

**882 — THE ONE HUNDRED AND SIXTY-SIXTH MORNING.** **A man of thirty-eight came back over that bank at about half past four and nothing that came down that lane with him is in the lot book or on the page at the back of it, and the second space on that form is still empty.**

**883 — THE ONE HUNDRED AND SIXTY-SEVENTH MORNING.** At about eleven a woman said out loud, in about nineteen people's hearing, that she is not going to ask anything else in that yard, that she is not going to say why, and that she would like it noticed that she said it out loud in front of all of them **and did not walk off and do it quietly.** She was asked why she was stopping and refused out loud.

**884 — THE ONE HUNDRED AND SIXTY-EIGHTH MORNING.** In the afternoon, on the stones, **a clerk counted a month out loud in about nineteen people's hearing and the count of counted months moved from six to seven and did not go back.** It was said in a mouth and not on a page.

**885 — THE ONE HUNDRED AND SIXTY-NINTH MORNING.** The morning after the counting, the count is seven and it did not go back. The man who digs loam was heard at the bottom of that lane and about four people heard him say that he had asked the man of fifty-six a question and got the same answer everybody gets.

**886 — THE ONE HUNDRED AND SEVENTIETH MORNING.** At about eleven the man who digs loam asked the man of fifty-six what the four figures on that wall are for. **He got the same answer he gives at about four every morning, flat, at the same speed, in the same words, and he has been asked it on every one of the six hundred and odd mornings of that run and has never once been thanked for it.**

**887 — THE ONE HUNDRED AND SEVENTY-FIRST MORNING.** At about half past ten the man of about twenty-nine who drives the cart asked the man of fifty-six whether there is anybody he would ask about them. Same answer. **Nobody thanked the man who was asked and nobody thanked the person who asked.**

**888 — THE ONE HUNDRED AND SEVENTY-SECOND MORNING.** **Nobody asked the man of fifty-six anything out loud this morning, and about four people at the top of eleven feet noticed that, and the yard said the four off that wall anyway and got all four of them.**

**889 — THE ONE HUNDRED AND SEVENTY-THIRD MORNING.** At about ten past eleven a man of thirty-eight asked the man of fifty-six whether he has ever written anything down. **Same answer, third time in four mornings, and a man of thirty-eight has now asked three people in this yard and been refused by one of them and answered flatly by two.**

**890 — THE ONE HUNDRED AND SEVENTY-FOURTH MORNING.** **Nobody asked anything on this morning and the volume ends on it.** A woman who keeps a scale weighed a sack and said nothing. The man who digs loam asked at the bottom of that lane whether anybody had heard him ask anything yet, and was told they had not, **and about four of them said he said the right word for it, which is *yet*.** At about ten past four a man of thirty-eight stood at about four feet off the near end of the first table and said one thing out loud before the light went off the boards: that he is going up that lane again on the next morning with nothing in his hand, that nobody asked him to and nobody has to. The count of counted months is seven. The figure on the sheet at that gatepost is four hundred and eleven and by how much it is out nobody has done the arithmetic on. The fifth of the five is five and unpaid. The heading over one space still says *whoever*. The man of thirty-eight has no bed, no wage and no office, has asked for none of the three, and has not been told he is a good man. **And a figure with no intercept begins, and may not be printed in a figure block, in a mouth, or in a document.**

# VOLUME 19, BLOCK 0001 — CHAPTER SUMMARIES 891 TO 900, MORNINGS ONE HUNDRED AND SEVENTY-FIFTH TO ONE HUNDRED AND EIGHTY-FOURTH

> **Appended by the review fix of 2026-10-01, which wrote no chapter and repaired eight lines of prose across six of these ten files and touched no ladder cell. Its figures came from the ten chapter files of `chapters/volume-19/`, re-derived with an instrument written from nothing outside this repository and validated first against Chapters 811 to 820, 821 to 830 and 831 to 840. `tools/measure.py` was not used. `c = 0` is Chapter 890 and the morning number is `174 + c`. The full measurement is `state/volume-19-batch-0001-summary.md` and a figure is not printed twice.**

**891 — THE ONE HUNDRED AND SEVENTY-FIFTH MORNING. THE OPENING.** A man of thirty-eight went up that lane before there was light with nothing in his hand and knocked at about ten at a time when somebody was in. **A woman at a table nine miles up that lane asked him for the page at the back of that lot book and asked him who writes on it, and he said no out loud to both of them and gave no reason to either.** He came back over that bank at about eleven with his hands showing and said one thing about it in about nineteen people's hearing, and said nothing more about that hill all day. **The lane count is twenty-nine and did not move, and the figure block prints why in words: a person went up that lane with nothing in his hand and nothing came down it.** The boy said a figure out loud before the clerk's hand had moved. The page at the back did not move.

**892 — THE ONE HUNDRED AND SEVENTY-SIXTH MORNING. ESCALATION 1 — THE BOARDS.** A man of about forty-one asked the clerk out loud whether the page at the back of that lot book can go up that lane to whoever asked for it, **and she said no out loud and gave no reason, and it is the third time she has said no without one.** About nine people at the top of eleven feet have said since that a woman who has said no three times without a reason has stopped being asked things and started being believed, **and that those are not the same thing, and that about four of them have not said which of the two they mean, and that nobody in that yard checked it.** The page at the back did not move. The lane count did not move.

**893 — THE ONE HUNDRED AND SEVENTY-SEVENTH MORNING.** About nine people at the top of eleven feet said out loud, one at a time, that the page at the back of that lot book is not the lot book — the turner, the man who mends fencing, and a third person — **and about four of them have said since that none of the three asked why, and that all three wanted the same answer and got it.** Mavis Dorr said out loud that she is a clerk of the lot book and not of anything behind it and has never been asked what is behind it and is not going to go and find out on a morning like this. **About nine people got an answer about a book and nobody in that yard has a way of asking so that the answer would be about the page.** The page at the back did not move.

**894 — THE ONE HUNDRED AND SEVENTY-EIGHTH MORNING. THE FIRST QUIET MORNING OF THE BLOCK.** The man who digs loam asked out loud, in about nine people's hearing, whether anybody in that yard has read the page at the back of that book, **and about four people said they have not, and he said he has not either and is not going to.** A man of thirty-eight said out loud that he has not read it either and is not going to say that he has and not going to say that he has not. **About nine people have said since that he has never asked anything before and that nobody has asked him why he asked that one.** The page at the back did not move. The lane count did not move.

**895 — THE ONE HUNDRED AND SEVENTY-NINTH MORNING.** A man of about thirty-four said out loud that he knows three people, at about four feet off the near end of the second table, in about nineteen people's hearing, **and would not say which three, and was asked again at about eleven and at about one and did not say then either.** Mavis Dorr said one thing out loud: **a page that has never been read is not a record of anything. That is all. I have got nothing else this morning.** About four of them have said since that the yard has already forgotten she said it about a page and thinks she said it about a bag of something she carried up that hill in the spring. **And at about ten past four the man of about thirty-four gave the first reason anybody in that yard has given out loud for keeping something off the page, and said he was not going to pretend he could not.**

**896 — THE ONE HUNDRED AND EIGHTIETH MORNING. ESCALATION 2 — A PAGE COMES DOWN THAT LANE.** A young woman came down that lane at about a quarter to ten with a page folded once in her own hand and nothing else on her, **gave it to nobody in particular, said no out loud to four people who asked whether she wanted it put somewhere, and stood at the foot of that bank.** The clerk read it out loud in about nineteen people's hearing and entered nothing. **IT IS A FORM WITH A HEADING, A RULE AND ONE SPACE, AND THE SPACE IS FOR A NAME. THE LANE COUNT MOVED FROM TWENTY-NINE TO THIRTY, FOR THE ONLY REASON IT EVER MOVES, AND THE ORDER WAS AS FIXED: THE PAGE ARRIVED FIRST, THE BOY SAID SO OUT LOUD BEFORE HE SAID A FIGURE, AND THE FIGURE WENT BEFORE THE CLERK'S HAND MOVED. NOBODY FILLED IT AND NOBODY WAS ASKED TO FILL IT.** The count of things this district has made is fourteen and did not move on the morning a page came down that lane.

**897 — THE ONE HUNDRED AND EIGHTY-FIRST MORNING.** About nineteen people in that yard looked at the form. The man of about forty-eight who keeps a tally put his thumb on the heading over that space and said out loud that **it is not the heading over the one name in that column, and that one of them still says whoever and the other one does not, and that there is nothing under either of them.** The man at the end of the second table said that it is two hands or one hand at two times, and that both are possible from where he is standing and he cannot get past those two from here, and nobody answered him. **THE YARD HAS TWO HEADINGS AND NO JOINER AND NOBODY IN THAT YARD KNOWS WHERE EITHER ONE OF THEM WAS WRITTEN, AND THAT IS NOT PRINTED AS A FIGURE ANYWHERE.** The page at the back did not move.

**898 — THE ONE HUNDRED AND EIGHTY-SECOND MORNING.** A man of about thirty-four asked a man of thirty-eight out loud, in about nineteen people's hearing, whether he is going to take that form back up that lane, **and he said he would not, and then gave a reason in the ordinary voice and about nine people at the top of eleven feet heard every word of it: a woman asked him for the page at the back of that lot book and he said no, and a man who says no to one thing on the one hundred and seventy-fifth morning and does another inside three days of it is one man to whoever is at the top of that hill, and he is not going to carry nine miles of lane with an empty space on it and no way of saying what is going in it.** **This is the second reason anybody in that yard has given out loud in this stretch of mornings, the first having been given on the one hundred and seventy-ninth morning by a man who would not name three people. HE WAS NOT ASKED TWICE, HE WAS NOT THANKED FOR THE REASON, AND HE WAS NOT TOLD HE IS A GOOD MAN.** The clerk wrote nothing for it. The page did not move. The lane count is thirty and did not move.

**899 — THE ONE HUNDRED AND EIGHTY-THIRD MORNING. ESCALATION 3 — ONE LINE, AND THE ONLY LINE IN TEN MORNINGS.** The clerk put the form flat on the boards under the clean page and said out loud that **it is not going in the lot book, and she is not going to put a line on the back for it either, because a line on the back is for a morning somebody asked in this yard, and this is not that.** At about half past eleven a woman of about fifty came up about four feet of that bank with a barrow and asked the man of fifty-six whether he is going to be at that wall tomorrow morning, **and he gave her the answer he gives at about four every morning, in the same words and at the same speed, flat, and about nineteen people heard it, and about four of them said it has been used about a different thing this morning and about four of them said a man cannot help what his answer is about.** The clerk read the morning out loud before she wrote it, and wrote **one line on the page at the back of that lot book, in her own hand, and neither the question nor the answer went on it. THE PAGE WENT FROM THIRTY-SIX LINES TO THIRTY-SEVEN AFTER EIGHT MORNINGS OF NOT TAKING ONE. IT ALSO TURNED OUT OUT LOUD, INSIDE ABOUT AS LONG AS IT TAKES A MAN OF FIFTY-SIX TO SAY FOUR FIGURES OFF A WALL, THAT NOBODY IN THAT YARD CARRIES ANYTHING UP THAT HILL — A FACT AND NOT A POLICY.** **Nobody thanked the woman who asked and nobody thanked the man who was asked. The lane count is thirty and did not move and is not moved by a decision.**

**900 — THE ONE HUNDRED AND EIGHTY-FOURTH MORNING. BLOCK 0001 ENDS.** At about eleven the man who digs loam asked out loud, in about nineteen people's hearing, whether anybody in that yard is going to fill that space on that form, **and about nine people at the top of eleven feet said no, one after another, and none of them gave a reason, and it took about as long as a man of fifty-six says four figures off a wall.** The clerk said nothing at all about it. The man who digs loam did not answer them and did not ask again. At about ten past four a man of thirty-eight said one thing out loud before the light went off the boards: **the fact is on these boards and it is not on a page, and this morning about nine people in this yard said no to a space in it, and none of them gave a reason, and I have not been given one either and I am not going to say I have one.** The clerk turned the page round and wrote the morning on it in her own hand under the last line and did not take a line for it. **THE PAGE CARRIES THIRTY-SEVEN LINES AND DID NOT CARRY A THIRTY-EIGHTH. THE RECORD HAS STOOD NINETY-NINE DAYS AND ABOUT NINE PEOPLE AT THE TOP OF ELEVEN FEET COULD SAY THAT FIGURE OUT LOUD WITHOUT LOOKING AT ANYTHING, AND NOT ONE OF THEM HAS SAID WHAT IT IS NINETY-NINE DAYS OF.** The lane count is thirty and it did not move. The counted months are seven and did not move. The figure on the sheet at that gatepost is four hundred and eleven and by how much it is out nobody in this basin has done the arithmetic on. The fifth of the five is five and is not paid. The heading over one space still says *whoever*. The form that came down that lane is still on those boards with its space empty and nobody has filled either space. A man of thirty-eight has no bed, no wage and no office, has asked for none of the three, and has not been told he is a good man. **And a yard can hold a thing and cannot send it and cannot say why.**

## VOLUME 19, BLOCK 0002 — CHAPTERS 901 TO 910, MORNINGS ONE HUNDRED AND EIGHTY-FIFTH TO ONE HUNDRED AND NINETY-FOURTH

> **Appended 2026-10-01 by the review fix of Volume 19 Block 0002, which wrote no chapter, altered no chapter, and restarted no batch. Its figures come from the ten chapter files of `chapters/volume-19/`, measured with an instrument written from nothing outside this repository. Every figure below that a later block will check is stated with the chapter file it came from, and the chapters are canon against this index.**

**901 — THE ONE HUNDRED AND EIGHTY-FIFTH MORNING. THE RECORD'S HUNDREDTH MORNING.** The form is still under the clean page on its sixth morning with the space on it empty, the reason the man of thirty-eight gave on the one hundred and eighty-second morning is still in somebody's mouth and is not on anything, and at about eleven about four of about nine people at the top of eleven feet said the figure out loud one after another and out of step with each other, the way that yard says a figure. **The page at the back took no line and carries thirty-seven, and the lane count is thirty and did not move.** The man of fifty-six says his four off that wall at about a quarter to eight and again at about four and gets all four both times, and nobody asks him anything out loud.

**902 — THE ONE HUNDRED AND EIGHTY-SIXTH MORNING. THE BOARDS GO OVER A ROUND NUMBER.** The board carries eight hundred and not one person in that yard reads it out loud. A woman of about fifty-two comes down that bank at about ten, which she has done once before in this stretch, looks at the space on the form, and asks the clerk out loud, at about four feet off the near end of the second table, in about nineteen people's hearing, **what would have to be true for that space to be filled.** She does not get an answer that anybody in that yard can use, and she is not the first to ask in that block's ten mornings and she will not be the last.

**903 — THE ONE HUNDRED AND EIGHTY-SEVENTH MORNING.** The man who makes the stone puts his chisel down, puts his hand flat on the low wall the way the man of fifty-six puts his hand flat on it, and says out loud, in about nineteen people's hearing, **that he has been at the foot of that wall twelve mornings and a piece of paper is not the same thing twelve mornings apart, and that a change in the hand is not a figure.** He stands there and nobody says anything, and about nine people at the top of eleven feet look at him and are careful, afterwards, not to say that he was not listening.

**904 — THE ONE HUNDRED AND EIGHTY-EIGHTH MORNING. THE DIRECTION IS SAID OUT LOUD AND THE CARRIER IS NOT NAMED.** The turner puts his apron on the second table and says out loud, in about nineteen people's hearing, **that a page goes down that lane, that down is the only direction anybody in that yard has put out loud about that hill, that the holding of one is a person, and that he is not going to name the person.** About four people at the top of eleven feet get as far as opening their mouths and nobody asks the turner anything.

**905 — THE ONE HUNDRED AND EIGHTY-NINTH MORNING. A MAN GOES OUT OF THAT YARD AND COMES BACK DOWN THAT LANE WITH NOTHING IN HIS HAND.** At about eleven he says out loud that he is going up the lane in the afternoon and off it to look at one of the four places, that he is taking nothing, that nobody has asked him to go and nobody has given him anything for going, and that **he is not going to be thanked for it and is not going to say he is a good man for it.** He stands at the top of that bank with his hands showing at his first light, at about eleven, and again at about six in the dark, and he carries nothing in his hand in either direction. **He is a man of thirty-eight and he has no bed, no wage and no office, and he is not asked for any of the three.**

**906 — THE ONE HUNDRED AND NINETIETH MORNING. THE MORNING ON WHICH NOT ONE PERSON PUTS A QUESTION INTO ABOUT FOUR HUNDRED YARDS OF AIR.** The wind lifts the corner of the clean page and about four people put it back down at the same time **without any of them saying anything about it.** The turner is asked nothing. The man who digs loam does a morning of work at the north end and says nothing. Nothing comes down that lane and nothing goes up it, and about four of them noticed the quiet by half past nine and about four of them noticed it by half past ten, and **neither of those is the moment anything was decided, because nothing in that yard was decided this morning by anybody.**

**907 — THE ONE HUNDRED AND NINETY-FIRST MORNING. A CLERK SAYS OUT LOUD THAT NOBODY HAS READ THAT PAGE.** The man of about twenty-nine who drives the cart asks her whether that is the same book that came down that lane, she says it is, she stops, and then she says one more thing out loud, in about nineteen people's hearing, once: **that nobody in that yard has read the page at the back of that book, that that goes with her as well, and that it is not a reason for anything.** She takes the lot book out from under the clean page, opens it, does not write anything, and closes it again.

**908 — THE ONE HUNDRED AND NINETY-SECOND MORNING. THE HARDEST MORNING IN THE BLOCK.** About nineteen people stand within about four feet of her in about four hundred yards of air all morning and **not one of them says anything to her.** The man who digs loam, who asked out loud on the one hundred and seventy-eighth morning, keeps himself at the north end of that yard and stays there all day. The man at the end of the second table puts his hand on the table, gets up, stands there for about as long as it takes a man of fifty-six to say four figures off a wall, does not ask her anything, and sits down again. **The not-asking took the whole morning and nobody in that yard cost anything by it, which is the whole of what happened and is not a measure of anything.**

**909 — THE ONE HUNDRED AND NINETY-THIRD MORNING. ESCALATION 4 — THE ASKING IS MADE OUT LOUD AND REFUSED OUT LOUD.** At about eleven the man who digs loam, who spent the whole of the last morning at the other end of that yard, comes back into the middle of it, puts his barrow down at about four feet off the near end of the second table and asks the clerk, in about nineteen people's hearing, **to read the page at the back of that book.** She says one sentence back, out loud, and that is the whole of what she says. The asking took about as long as it takes a man of fifty-six to say four figures off a wall, and that is the whole of what he did. Afterwards she turns the page round and writes the morning on it in her own hand under the last line, **and she does not take a line for it, and a line on that page has only ever been for an asking of the man of fifty-six, and this was not that.**

**910 — THE ONE HUNDRED AND NINETY-FOURTH MORNING. THE MIDPOINT REVERSAL. BLOCK 0002 ENDS.** At about eleven, at about four feet off the near end of the second table, with about nine people within about four feet of her and about nineteen people in that yard, the clerk takes the lot book off the boards, turns the page at the back of it round, **reads it to herself with her mouth moving and nothing coming out of it that anybody in that yard can hear, and when she gets to the bottom she says one word out loud, and the word was three**, and about nineteen people in that yard heard it. **It is not an answer to anything anybody asked that morning and nobody in that yard has called it one, and the man of about thirty-four says nothing and does not say it was three of anything, and that is a refusal to join them and not a join.** The page carries thirty-seven lines and took none. The lane count is thirty and did not move. The figure on the sheet at that gatepost is four hundred and eleven and by how much that figure is out has still never been done by anybody in this basin.

### THE FIGURES THIS BLOCK OF SUMMARIES CARRIES, MEASURED AGAINST THE TEN CHAPTER FILES

**29,526 WORDS AND 180 DIVIDERS OVER CHAPTERS 901 TO 910, AGAINST 27,965 WORDS AND 180 DIVIDERS FOR CHAPTERS 891 TO 900.** Both are measured against the chapter files and neither is inherited. The page at the back of that lot book is at **thirty-seven** on all ten mornings and took no line on any of them. The lane count is **thirty** on all ten and moved once in the volume, on the one hundred and eightieth morning. The figure on the sheet at that gatepost is **four hundred and eleven** on all ten and its size of error is uncomputed on all ten. The count of counted months is **seven** and did not move. This district has made **fourteen** things. The fifth of the five is **five** and is unpaid on every morning of both blocks. The record has stood **one hundred days to one hundred and nine days** across these ten mornings and nobody in that yard has asked what it is one hundred and nine days of. The toll is **ninety-eight** and is spent. **NOBODY ASKED THE MAN OF FIFTY-SIX ANYTHING OUT LOUD ON ANY MORNING OF THIS BLOCK, AND THE FIGURE BLOCK MAY NOT PRINT THAT THE ASKING HAS BEEN STOPPED, WITHDRAWN, NARROWED OR CONTINUED, AND MAY NOT PRINT THE NUMBER OF MORNINGS WITHOUT AN ASKING.**

## VOLUME 19, BLOCK 0003 — CHAPTERS 911 TO 920, MORNINGS THE ONE HUNDRED AND NINETY-FIFTH TO THE TWO HUNDRED AND FOURTH

> **Appended 2026-10-02 by the review fix that followed the Volume 19 close, which wrote no chapter, altered no chapter, and restarted no batch. These ten entries fill the gap this index carried between chapter 910 and chapter 921. The close weighed that gap at `state/volume-19-close.md` §8 item 13 and did not fill it, because the close's own scope was chapters 921 to 930 and not chapters 911 to 920, and this section is written between the block 0002 and block 0004 sections so that the four blocks of this volume run in order on one page. The figures come from the ten chapter files of `chapters/volume-19/`, measured with instruments written from nothing outside this repository. Every figure below that a later block will check is stated with the chapter file it came from, and the chapters are canon against this index. Nothing in this section answers anything.**

**911 — THE ONE HUNDRED AND NINETY-FIFTH MORNING. A FIGURE SAID OUT LOUD AT THOSE BOARDS IS A MORNING AND A WORD IS A FIGURE.** At about ten to eight Orren Pike says the lane count out loud and then says **two hundred and nine** at the near end of the first table, before the clerk's hand has moved, and about four people say it after him. At about half past eight the man of about forty-eight who keeps a tally says one sentence out loud — **a figure said out loud in this yard is a morning** — and nobody answers him. At about eleven the man at the end of the second table says one clause out loud, in about nineteen people's hearing, once: **a word is a figure.** **NOBODY IN THAT YARD SAID WHAT ANY OF THE FIGURES SAID OUT LOUD THIS MORNING IS A FIGURE OF.** Nobody was thanked. Page **thirty-seven**, and it did not carry a thirty-eighth. Lane count **thirty**, unmoved.

**912 — THE ONE HUNDRED AND NINETY-SIXTH MORNING. THREE FOURS IN ONE MOUTH, NOT JOINED, AND NOTHING ON THE PAGE.** A woman of about fifty-two comes down that bank at about eleven for the third time in this stretch, looks at the form under the clean page, asks nothing and goes back up. At about two the turner, the man of about forty-one, says four things out loud, one after another, in about nineteen people's hearing, **and he puts nothing between them**: there are four places in this district where those three lines can be read; the number of documents this district does not own is four; the number of conditions with no end on it is four; and a figure has stood at four for as long as anybody in that yard has been counting. **ABOUT FOUR PEOPLE TRY TO JOIN HIM AND TWO OF THEM GET HALFWAY AND STOP — the man of about thirty-four gets as far as *so that would be one for each of* and the man of about forty-eight gets as far as *if you put them end to end* — AND HE NEITHER HELPS NOR STOPS EITHER OF THEM.** About four of them have said since that not joining them is not the same as not seeing the join, and that about four of them have not said which of those two it is. Page **thirty-seven**. Lane count **thirty**, unmoved.

**913 — THE ONE HUNDRED AND NINETY-SEVENTH MORNING. THE EMPTY ROW, AND THE MORNING IS THE THING THE EMPTY ROW IS.** The morning is about a yard that has two things it cannot ask about and one thing it cannot stop looking at, which is the boards. At about half past ten the man of about twenty-nine who drives a cart slows in the gap in the bank, stops in the middle of that yard at about four feet off the near end of the second table, stands behind the shafts with the horse's nose down until the light has come off the boards and onto the stones, and says one thing nobody asked him for, which is that the going had got slick on the top of the bank where the water comes across. **NOBODY ASKED HIM TO STOP, NOBODY ASKED HIM WHY HE STOPPED, AND NOBODY THANKED HIM.** The man of about thirty-four stood about four feet off that cart for about an hour and said nothing at all. **A PERSON GOING OUT OF THAT YARD AND COMING BACK DOWN IT WITH NOTHING IN HIS HAND IS NOT A PAGE COMING DOWN IT.** Page **thirty-seven**. Lane count **thirty**, unmoved.

**914 — THE ONE HUNDRED AND NINETY-EIGHTH MORNING. A PAGE THAT HAS BEEN READ TO ONESELF HAS NOT BEEN READ, AND NOBODY ANSWERS IT.** At about ten to eight Orren Pike says **two hundred and twenty-one** at those boards. At about eleven the turner says, in the ordinary voice, at about four feet off the near end of the second table, **a page that has been read to oneself has not been read**, and about nineteen people hear it. **NOBODY ANSWERS IT AND NOBODY AGREES WITH IT OUT LOUD.** **THE COST:** the turner puts the tool down on the bench, sets the piece in the rack and does not start the wheel again that morning, and the shaving stays in his hand until about four. Nobody asked him to stop. In the same morning the man of about forty-eight who keeps a tally says out loud that the man of fifty-six says the same four words about a page, about a cart, about a gatepost, about a column and about a form with a space on it, whatever it is about, **and nobody says out loud that the man of fifty-six did not say it about all of those things, and nobody asks the man who said it a question.** The man at the end of the second table said nothing at all this morning. Page **thirty-seven**. Lane count **thirty**, unmoved.

**915 — THE ONE HUNDRED AND NINETY-NINTH MORNING. SOMEBODY IS ASKED TO GO AND LOOK AND GOES, AND A CLERK DECLINES A COUNT OUT LOUD.** At about ten the man of about forty-eight who keeps a tally asks the man of about twenty-nine who drives a cart, out loud and in about nineteen people's hearing, to go up the lane and look at the second of the four places and come back and say what is on the wall, **and the man says he will go and says that nobody had asked him to be thanked for it and that nobody had given him anything for going.** At about eleven the same man asks the clerk, out loud, **how many mornings that boy has said a figure at those boards**, and she says one sentence back and that is the whole of what she says about it: **I do not count what a boy says at those boards.** **NO FIGURE IS PRINTED FOR THE COUNT THAT WAS DECLINED, FOR WHAT IT WOULD HAVE BEEN, OR FOR ANY OTHER, AND NONE IS PROJECTED.** At about six the man comes back down into that yard with nothing in his hands, goes through it without stopping, is asked nothing by anybody, is not thanked, and says one thing unasked at about half past six — **I stood in front of it long enough** — and is not asked a second thing. Page **thirty-seven**. Lane count **thirty**, unmoved.

**916 — THE TWO HUNDREDTH MORNING. NOTHING HAPPENS IN THAT YARD, AND A MORNING IS A MORNING.** The boards, the four figures off the wall twice, the boy saying no figure out loud, the stone man working a joint in the same place twice, the cart going through without stopping for the first time since the morning it stopped, the turner's wheel heard again, the clerk with the front of the book open from about eleven until about half past twelve, the man of about thirty-four standing at the near end of the second table from about twelve, the man at the end of the second table turning round once in the middle of the morning. **NO PERSON IN THAT YARD SAYS ANYTHING THAT COUNTS A MORNING.** About four people at the top of eleven feet have said since that a morning with nothing in it and a morning with a word said out loud in it on another morning are the same morning to work in and are not the same morning, and that about four of them have not said which of those two is the one that counts. Page **thirty-seven**. Lane count **thirty**, unmoved.

**917 — THE TWO HUNDRED AND FIRST MORNING. A WALL WITH NOTHING ON IT IN A ROOM, AND A MAN WHO WAS NOT ASKED TWICE.** At about ten the cart man says out loud, unasked, that he is going up there again and that nobody has asked him to and nobody is going to thank him for it. **THE PROSE THEN GOES OFF THAT LANE AND UP IT, AND SEEN FROM OUTSIDE THAT YARD.** The second of the four places is a low building with a door in it and a window with the lower half painted over, and there is a wall along the back and there is nothing on it at all. A man, unnamed, who was not introduced in that yard and who exists on no other morning of this volume, comes out with a bundle of wire in his arms, puts it down on the step and says the three lines are not on that wall and have not been since before he came, that he has been in that room nine years and they were not on it when he came, and that he has never been asked about them. **HE IS NOT ASKED TO SHOW ANYBODY AND HE IS NOT ASKED TWICE.** Asked once, he says that they went up, and into the keeping of a party that does not walk about and does not send anything down a lane, and that where they are is not a place anybody can walk to. He says one more thing: nobody has ever come up that lane to ask him about that wall before this morning. The cart man comes back down with nothing in his hands, goes through the yard without stopping, is offered nothing and is not asked one word. **THE THREE LINES ON THAT WALL AND THE THREE LINES NOBODY IN THIS DISTRICT HEARD READ ARE NOT PUT IN ONE SENTENCE IN THAT CHAPTER, AND THE FIGURE BLOCK ON THAT MORNING PRINTS FOUR.** Page **thirty-seven**. Lane count **thirty**, unmoved, **AND A TRIP IS NOT A PAGE COMING DOWN A LANE.**

**918 — THE TWO HUNDRED AND SECOND MORNING. THE SIZE OF THE ERROR IS NOT COMPUTED, AND A MAN SAYS SO OUT LOUD INSTEAD.** At about a quarter to nine the man of thirty-eight goes up that lane a third time with nothing in his hands and nobody has asked him to. At about ten the man of about thirty-four walks down to the gatepost alone and reads the sheet out loud, at about four feet off the near end of the second table, in about nineteen people's hearing — **four hundred and eleven** — and then says one more thing out loud: **nobody in this basin has ever done it by how much that figure is out, and I am not going to be the one, and I have said that out loud so that nobody can ask me for it later.** **NOBODY ASKED HIM FOR IT, AND THE SIZE OF THE ERROR IS NOT COMPUTED, NOT EXPLAINED AND NOT PAID IN A MOUTH, IN A LEDGER, IN A FIGURE BLOCK OR IN ANY DOCUMENT.** At about half past two the man of thirty-eight comes down that lane into the yard with nothing in his hands, stops at the top of the bank, and says out loud, in about nineteen people's hearing: **I have been up there three times now, and I am not going to say what is up there, and I am not going to stop going up there, and neither of those is a reason and I am not giving one.** Nobody thanked him and nobody asked him one question. **THE LANE COUNT IS A COUNT OF PAGES AND THIS WAS NOT A PAGE.** Page **thirty-seven**. Lane count **thirty**, unmoved.

**919 — THE TWO HUNDRED AND THIRD MORNING. THE NINTH OF THE NINE PRINTED NIGHTS REACHES SEVEN HUNDRED AND NOT ONE PERSON LOOKED AT IT, AND AN UNPAID THING IS NAMED AS UNPAID.** At about ten to eight Orren Pike says **two hundred and twenty-three** at those boards and about four people say it after him, **and not one of them looks up at those boards while they say it, and the ninth line on those boards is the one nobody in that yard looks at.** At about one the man at the end of the second table puts down what he was holding and says, in about nineteen people's hearing, once: **the fifth of the five is unpaid.** **NOBODY SAID ONE WORD BACK, NOBODY ASKED HIM WHAT THE FIFTH OF THE FIVE IS, NOBODY THANKED HIM, AND NOTHING WAS ENTERED ANYWHERE IN THAT YARD THIS MORNING.** About four of them have said since that a thing being five and unpaid is not the same as a thing being asked about, that nobody in that yard has asked about the fifth of the five, and that naming a thing as unpaid is not giving a reason for it. Page **thirty-seven**. Lane count **thirty**, unmoved.

**920 — THE TWO HUNDRED AND FOURTH MORNING. BLOCK 0003 ENDS ON A MORNING THAT WENT LIKE THE OTHERS.** The boards, the four figures off that wall for the ninety-third time, the boy saying no figure out loud, the man of thirty-eight coming down the slope for the first time in several days, the woman of about fifty-two coming down that bank for the third time and asking nothing, the man at the end of the second table saying nothing out loud at all, the two men at the foot of that low wall on a morning that is the sixtieth running of not being asked one question, the clerk naming the morning in her own hand and taking no line for it, the light going off the boards at about half past four and the goodnight said by about four people out of about nineteen. **ABOUT FOUR PEOPLE AT THE TOP OF ELEVEN FEET HAVE SAID SINCE THAT THEY DO NOT KNOW WHAT THE WORD WAS A FIGURE OF, AND THAT ABOUT FOUR OF THEM SAID THEY ARE NOT GOING TO KEEP LOOKING, AND THAT ABOUT FOUR OF THEM HAVE NOT SAID WHY THEY SAID THAT.** **The record has stood one hundred and nineteen days and nobody in that yard asked what it is one hundred and nineteen days of.** Page **thirty-seven**, and it did not carry a thirty-eighth. Lane count **thirty**, unmoved.

### THE FIGURES THESE TEN CHAPTERS CARRY, MEASURED AGAINST THE TEN CHAPTER FILES

**30,163 WORDS AND 181 DIVIDERS OVER CHAPTERS 911 TO 920**, against **29,526 words and 180 dividers** for chapters 901 to 910 and **30,502 words and 180 dividers** for chapters 921 to 930, and **118,156 words and 721 dividers** for the whole of volume 19. All are measured against the chapter files and none is inherited. **The page at the back of that lot book is at thirty-seven on all ten mornings and took no line on any of them, and the lane count is thirty on all ten and moved once in the volume, on the one hundred and eightieth morning.** The figure on the sheet at that gatepost is **four hundred and eleven** on all ten and its size of error is uncomputed on all ten. The count of counted months is **seven** on all ten and did not move. This district has made **fourteen** things on all ten and the fourteen did not move. The number of places in this district where those three lines can be read is **four** on all ten, and on the two hundred and first morning a man says out loud, from outside that yard, that there is nothing on the wall of the second of those four. The fifth of the five is **five** and is **unpaid** on every one of the ten, and naming it out loud on the two hundred and third morning paid nothing and was thanked by nobody. The bid was appended at **four hundred and fifty** days and was not run and is not a lot. The record has stood **one hundred and ten** days on the first of these mornings and **one hundred and nineteen** on the last, and nobody in that yard asked what it is one hundred and nineteen days of. The man of fifty-six read the four off that wall on the **six hundred and thirty-sixth** through the **six hundred and forty-fifth** of those mornings and said the same sentence about them for the **eighty-fourth** through the **ninety-third** time running, **and nobody asked him anything out loud on any of the ten.** **THE FIGURE BLOCK TOOK THIRTY-FIVE AND SEVEN HUNDREDTHS OF ONE PER CENT OF THE WORDS OF THE WHOLE VOLUME AND THIS BLOCK IS THE ONE THAT RUNS AT FORTY AND SIX HUNDREDTHS OF ONE PER CENT, IN `chapter-0916.md`, THE MORNING WITH NOTHING IN IT.**

**AND WHAT THIS BLOCK DID TO THE VOLUME, IN ONE SENTENCE: IT PUT THE THREE FOURS IN ONE MOUTH AND LEFT THEM UNJOINED, IT SENT A MAN UP A LANE AND PUT AN EMPTY WALL IN A ROOM NOBODY IN THAT YARD HAD EVER ASKED ABOUT, IT HEARD A MAN SAY OUT LOUD THAT NOBODY HAS EVER COMPUTED THE SIZE OF THE ERROR IN FOUR HUNDRED AND ELEVEN, AND IT NAMED THE FIFTH OF THE FIVE AS UNPAID AND THANKED NOBODY, AND NOT ONE OF THOSE FOUR THINGS IS AN ANSWER TO ANYTHING.** The full verdict, every figure, every convention and every finding the close weighed and did not repair are at `state/volume-19-close.md`, and the index is `state/volume-19-roll-summary.md`.

## VOLUME 19, BLOCK 0004 — CHAPTERS 921 TO 930, MORNINGS THE TWO HUNDRED AND FIFTH TO THE TWO HUNDRED AND FOURTEENTH, AND THE BLOCK THAT ENDS THE VOLUME

> **Appended by the Volume 19 close, which wrote no chapter, altered no chapter, and restarted no batch. Its figures come from the ten chapter files of `chapters/volume-19/`, measured with instruments written from nothing outside this repository. **THE PER-CHAPTER ENTRIES FOR CHAPTERS 911 TO 920 WERE NOT ON THIS PAGE WHEN THIS SECTION WAS APPENDED, AND THEY ARE ON IT NOW**: they were written on 2026-10-02 by the review fix that followed the close, and they sit in the block 0003 section above, which was written on the same pass. The close's item on that absence is at `state/volume-19-close.md` §8 item 13. Every figure below that a later block will check is stated with the chapter file it came from, and the chapters are canon against this index.**

**921 — THE TWO HUNDRED AND FIFTH MORNING. THE MEASURE THAT CAME BEFORE THE ONE THAT CAME AFTER IT.** At about ten to eight Orren Pike says the lane count out loud and then says **two hundred and twenty-eight** at the near end of the first table, and about four of them have said since that this is the last morning of ten and that about four of them said nobody in that yard was told there was a ten. At about eleven the man of about thirty-four asks the man of fifty-six, in the ordinary voice, in about nineteen people's hearing, whether he will be at that wall tomorrow morning, and is given the answer he gives at about four every afternoon, in the same words and at the same speed, flat. **THE PAGE TAKES ONE LINE UNDER THE OLD MEASURE AND THE LINE CARRIES THE MORNING AND NOT THE QUESTION AND NOT THE ANSWER.** The clerk reads the morning out loud before she writes anything. **Nobody thanked the man who was asked and nobody thanked the man who asked.** Page **thirty-eight**. Lane count **thirty**, unmoved.

**922 — THE TWO HUNDRED AND SIXTH MORNING. THE QUIET MORNING AFTER A LINE.** Nothing is said out loud and nobody asks anybody anything. The man of about thirty-four stands at about four feet off the near end of the second table with his hands in his pockets and his mouth shut and looks at the page at the back of that book three times and does not ask about it, and the clerk can see him looking. A woman of about fifty-two comes down that bank for the fourth time, looks at the form under the clean page and asks nothing. **THE CLERK DOES NOT READ THE MORNING OUT LOUD THIS MORNING AND TAKES NO LINE.** Page **thirty-eight**. Lane count **thirty**, unmoved.

**923 — THE TWO HUNDRED AND SEVENTH MORNING. THE FIRST REFUSAL IN THIS STRETCH BY A PERSON WHO HAS NOTHING TO DO WITH IT.** The man of about thirty-two puts his own table up at the foot of that bank for the two hundred and sixth morning running, squares it with the back of his heel and puts his apron on. At about eleven he puts both hands flat on the top of his own table and says, once, in the ordinary voice, in about nineteen people's hearing, **that the page at the back of that book is not his table and that nothing that gets written on it is anything to do with him and that he has never once asked why anybody does anything with any of it.** About nine people go quiet. He picks the cloth up and goes along the top of his table. **A REFUSAL IS NOT A REASON AND THE PAGE DID NOT MOVE.** Page **thirty-eight**. Lane count **thirty**, unmoved.

**924 — THE TWO HUNDRED AND EIGHTH MORNING. THE MEASURE OF THE VOLUME IS BEGUN, AND THE REASON IS A NAME.** At about one the woman who keeps a scale weighs a bar of iron out of a basket, writes the figure on the edge of a board of her own with a bit of chalk, puts her hand flat on the top of the scale, stops, and says, once, in the ordinary voice, at about four feet off the near end of the second table, in about nineteen people's hearing: **A WAY TO PAY A PERSON WHO IS NOT IN A HOUSEHOLD.** She picks the bar up, puts it back in the basket and goes on. **Nobody answered it, nobody asked her to say it again, nobody asked her for it, and nobody was thanked for anything.** The turner's wheel goes all the way through it without slowing. **The clerk reads the morning out loud and writes one line carrying the morning and not the reason and not the name, and the fifth of the five is still five and is still unpaid.** Page **thirty-nine**. Lane count **thirty**, unmoved.

**925 — THE TWO HUNDRED AND NINTH MORNING. THE CLERK SAYS OUT LOUD WHAT A LINE ON A PAGE IS NOT, AND IT IS FOUR THINGS.** At about four o'clock, unasked, in about nineteen people's hearing, in the ordinary voice, she says that a line on a page **is not a rule, is not a scale, is not a payment, and is not a trust sentence**, and she says the four one after another at the same speed and puts nothing between them. At about half past ten the man of about thirty-four has already said out loud that a clerk has told that yard what a line on a page is not, and that is four things said in a yard that has not been told one thing yet. **She takes no line and writes the morning in her own hand, and saying what it is not is not a reason.** Page **thirty-nine**. Lane count **thirty**, unmoved.

**926 — THE TWO HUNDRED AND TENTH MORNING. A SECOND REASON OUT LOUD, AND ONE LINE.** At about eleven the man who digs loam comes out of the north end, walks into the middle of that yard and stops at about four feet off the near end of the second table with his barrow standing behind him and his spade in his hand, and says, once: **I have been digging loam for eleven years, and I cannot do it much longer, and there is nobody in this yard I could say that to.** He goes back to the north end and works the same strip. At about half past ten the man of about forty-eight who keeps a tally says one thing out loud before he says his number, and nobody answers that. **Nobody was thanked.** Page **forty**. Lane count **thirty**, unmoved.

**927 — THE TWO HUNDRED AND ELEVENTH MORNING. THE COST, AND A REASON OUT OF A MOUTH NOBODY PLANNED, AND IT CAME OFF A CART.** The man of about twenty-nine who drives the cart comes through the gap in the bank at about half past nine, slows in the gap the way he slows in the gap, and stops and does not go on. He says, once, standing behind the shafts of his own cart with the horse's nose down to the stones, and not looking at anybody: **My father went down in a bank. Not far up there from this. And I slow in a gap every single time I come through one, and I have never said that out loud to anybody, and there is nobody in this yard I would have said it to.** He takes the shafts up and goes out of the yard and does not go up the lane. **THE TURNER PUTS HIS WHEEL DOWN AND WORKED NOTHING. THE REASON CANNOT BE CHECKED BY ANYBODY STANDING IN THAT YARD BECAUSE THERE IS NOBODY STANDING IN THAT YARD WHO WAS THERE.** The man of fifty-six says the same four words for the **hundredth** time running. Page **forty-one**. Lane count **thirty**, unmoved.

**928 — THE TWO HUNDRED AND TWELFTH MORNING. TWO REASONS IN ONE MORNING, WHICH THE PAGE HAS NEVER DONE.** At about eleven the man of about thirty-four says, looking at the boards and not at anybody, that there is one window in this district he can see from about four feet off the near end of the second table and that it is his own and that he looks at it about nine times a morning and that nobody in that yard has ever asked him why he stands there. At about a quarter past eleven the man who digs loam, from the far end of that yard and without coming any nearer, says that he was put out of a trade at seventeen that wanted his hands looked at all day long, and that this is the only work he has ever had where nobody in that yard has ever once seen his hands. **THE TWO MEN WERE ABOUT FOUR HUNDRED YARDS APART AND NEITHER KNEW THE OTHER HAD SAID ONE.** At about half past eleven the man of about forty-eight who keeps a tally says out loud that then a morning is not a morning, and nobody asks him what he means by it. **The clerk reads the morning out loud ONCE and writes TWO lines, one under the other, both carrying the morning and neither carrying a reason. Nobody was thanked.** Page **forty-three**. Lane count **thirty**, unmoved.

**929 — THE TWO HUNDRED AND THIRTEENTH MORNING. NOBODY SAYS A REASON AND NOBODY SAYS NO.** The man of about thirty-four is at about four feet off the near end of the second table from about nine until about four and says nothing out loud at all. The woman of about fifty-two comes down that bank a fifth time and asks nothing. The man of about forty-eight who keeps a tally says his number at about half past one and **says nothing else out loud, which he has not done on three of the last four mornings.** **The clerk does not read the morning out loud, writes the morning in her own hand under the last line, and takes no line for it.** Page **forty-three**. Lane count **thirty**, unmoved.

**930 — THE TWO HUNDRED AND FOURTEENTH MORNING. THE VOLUME ENDS ON A REASON ABOUT A THING THAT IS NOT THE PAGE.** Orren Pike says **two hundred and sixty-one** at the near end of the first table. At about half past ten the woman of about fifty-two comes down that bank for the sixth time and asks one thing out loud in that yard in six times down it: **My mother is buried not far up that bank, and I get as far as the top of it and no further, and I have come down here six times and I have not got any further than the top of it one time.** Nobody said one word back to her, nobody thanked her, and nobody asked her one second thing about her mother or about the bank or about the six times. The man of about twenty-nine who drives the cart goes through the yard without stopping. At about half past three a man of sixty-one says good afternoon and then says **one word on his own that nobody had said anything to him about**, and nothing said in that yard this afternoon carries what the word was. **At about four o'clock the man of fifty-six says the four off that wall for the hundred and third time running, and the clerk reads the morning out loud and writes one line carrying the morning and not the reason.** **THE PAGE IS AT FORTY-FOUR AND IT DID NOT CARRY A FORTY-FIFTH. THE LANE COUNT IS THIRTY AND IT DID NOT MOVE. NOBODY WAS THANKED.**

### THE FIGURES THESE TEN CHAPTERS CARRY, MEASURED AGAINST THE TEN CHAPTER FILES

**30,502 WORDS AND 180 DIVIDERS OVER CHAPTERS 921 TO 930**, and **118,156 WORDS AND 721 DIVIDERS** for the whole of volume 19, both measured against the chapter files and neither inherited. **The page at the back of that lot book is at thirty-eight, thirty-eight, thirty-eight, thirty-nine, thirty-nine, forty, forty-one, forty-three, forty-three and forty-four on these ten mornings, it moved on five of them and one of those by two, and it is not a ladder and was never one a morning.** The lane count is **thirty** on all ten and stood at twenty-nine on the first five mornings of the volume and moved once, on the one hundred and eightieth morning. The figure on the sheet at that gatepost is **four hundred and eleven** on all ten and its size of error is uncomputed on all ten. The count of counted months is **seven** on all ten and did not move. This district has made **fourteen** things and the fourteen did not move. The fifth of the five is **five** and is **unpaid** on all ten, and its name was said out loud once, on the two hundred and eighth morning, and naming it paid nothing. The toll is **ninety-eight** and is spent. The bid was appended at **four hundred and fifty** days and was not run and is not a lot and will not be. The record has stood **one hundred and twenty** days on the first of these mornings and **one hundred and twenty-nine** on the last. The man of fifty-six read the four off that wall on the **six hundred and sixteenth** through the **six hundred and twenty-fifth** of those mornings and said the same sentence about them for the **ninety-fourth** through the **hundred and third** time running, and nobody asked him anything out loud on any of the ten. The man of about sixty-four was on his **five hundred and thirtieth** through **five hundred and thirty-ninth** night of that run, having slept on **five hundred and twenty-ninth** through **five hundred and thirty-eighth** of them, and was not asked one question on any of the ten. The man of sixty-one was on the stones for the **one hundred and twentieth** through the **one hundred and twenty-ninth** afternoon running and said one word on his own on the last of them and nothing that was said in that yard that afternoon carries what the word was.

**AND WHAT THIS BLOCK DID TO THE VOLUME, IN ONE SENTENCE: IT BEGAN A MEASURE IN WHICH ONE LINE GOES ON THE BACK PAGE FOR EVERY REASON SAID OUT LOUD IN THAT YARD, IT TOOK TWO LINES IN ONE MORNING FOR THE FIRST TIME IN FORTY MORNINGS, AND IT ENDED ON A REASON ABOUT WHERE A WOMAN'S MOTHER IS BURIED, AND NONE OF THOSE THREE THINGS IS AN ANSWER TO ANYTHING.** The full verdict, every figure, every convention and every finding this close weighed and did not repair are at `state/volume-19-close.md`, and the index is `state/volume-19-roll-summary.md`.

## VOLUME 20, BLOCK 0001 — CHAPTERS 931 TO 940, MORNINGS THE TWO HUNDRED AND FIFTEENTH TO THE TWO HUNDRED AND TWENTY-FOURTH

**THE FULL RECORD IS `state/volume-20-batch-0001-summary.md` AND IT IS THE ONLY PLACE THESE FIGURES ARE PUBLISHED IN FULL. WHAT FOLLOWS IS THE PER-CHAPTER LINE, AND THE POINTERS. THE CHAPTER FILES ARE CANON AGAINST EVERYTHING IN THIS SECTION.**

1. **931, the two hundred and fifteenth.** THE OPENING MORNING. A man of thirty-eight says one thing out loud at about half past ten at about four feet off the near end of the first table, describing what forty mornings of that yard look like from the end of his own table: nobody has refused to say a thing in forty mornings, nobody has said the same thing twice, and what happens when a man says a thing is that the yard goes on and a woman writes some of it on a piece of paper. **Nobody in that yard said one word back. Nobody agreed with it and nobody said it was not so. The page took no line.** The clerk read the morning out loud before she wrote anything and then wrote the morning in her own hand without a line. Orren Pike said **nine hundred and forty**. Page forty-four. Lane thirty.
2. **932, the two hundred and sixteenth.** The turner puts his apron on the second table and at about ten past nine says, unasked, the same sentence he said on the one hundred and eighty-eighth morning: **a page goes down that lane**, that is the only direction anybody has put out loud in that yard about that hill, everything else about that lane is a person holding a thing, the holding is a person, and he is not going to name the person. **Nobody asked him to say it again and the yard did not join it to anything.** Page forty-four. Lane thirty.
3. **933, the two hundred and seventeenth. ESCALATION 1.** At about eleven the man of about thirty-four asks the man of thirty-eight, out loud, in about nineteen people's hearing, whether the reason he gave on the one hundred and eighty-second morning is on the page at the back of that lot book. **He says no. One word. No reason for the no.** Nobody asked him why, nobody asked for the reason again, nobody asked whether it had been written down anywhere, nobody asked the asker what he had expected. **A no takes no line.** Page forty-four. Lane thirty.
4. **934, the two hundred and eighteenth.** The cart comes through the gap at about half past nine, slows in the gap, **does not stop**, and goes out of the far side without going up the lane, and **nobody in that yard said one word about it**, then or afterwards. **The clerk said nothing out loud to anybody from about ten until about twelve** and about nine people stood at about four feet off the near end of the second table and went away again. Page forty-four. Lane thirty.
5. **935, the two hundred and nineteenth.** The not-asking is named, **by about nine people at the top of eleven feet who have said since, and by nobody in the scene**, and it did not come down that bank. At about half past ten the man at the end of the second table says one thing, once, about himself: he has stood at that end of that table since the morning the ground under it was made and no one in that yard has ever told him out loud what he is standing there for, and he is not asking for it. **Nobody answered him and he went back to the end of the table.** Page forty-four. Lane thirty.
6. **936, the two hundred and twentieth. ESCALATION 2.** The near rail reaches **the two hundredth morning** and the second table reaches **two hundred and fifty mornings** on the same morning, and **about four people at the top of eleven feet have said since that two numbers went over together and that nobody in that yard looked up. The figure block prints both and prints nothing about it.** Orren Pike said **one thousand and eleven**. The man of fifty-six said his same four words for the hundred and ninth time running. Page forty-four. Lane thirty.
7. **937, the two hundred and twenty-first. THE FIRST REASON SAID OUT LOUD IN THIS VOLUME AND THE ONLY LINE ON THE PAGE IN THIS BLOCK.** At about eleven the man of about thirty-four says one reason out loud, once, in the ordinary voice, in about nineteen people's hearing, **about himself and not about his work**: he stood at that table because he could not make himself go home, he has not said that to his wife and will not say it to her in that house, because in that house he is the one standing at the end of a table too, and out here nobody wants anything off him. **Nobody asked him for it, nobody asked him to say it again, nobody said it for him, and nobody said one word back.** The clerk read the morning out loud and **wrote one line carrying the morning and not the reason**, and that page is at forty-five lines. **Nobody was thanked.** Lane thirty.
8. **938, the two hundred and twenty-second.** Nobody says a reason and nobody says no. The man of about thirty-two puts his table up, squares it with the back of his heel, and **says nothing at all**, the first morning in four he has put that table up and NOT said a word. **The clerk did not read the morning out loud this morning and named it on the page in her own hand without a line.** Page forty-five, unmoved. Lane thirty.
9. **939, the two hundred and twenty-third. ESCALATION 3.** At about half past ten the turner says, once, that **a reason said out loud and a reason asked for are two different things going into the same place**, and that he is not putting them together and is not saying they are the same. **Nobody answered him, nobody asked him to say it again, nobody asked him what the same place is.** Page forty-five, unmoved. Lane thirty.
10. **940, the two hundred and twenty-fourth. BLOCK 0001 ENDS.** Orren Pike said **two hundred and ninety-four**. **The man of thirty-eight was at about four feet off the near end of the first table from about nine until the boards went down and said nothing out loud at all from about nine until about four, and he was still at that table after four o'clock and said nothing then either, and nobody asked him anything.** The clerk read the morning out loud and took no line. **Page forty-five. The one line it took in ten mornings was taken on the two hundred and twenty-first morning.** Lane thirty, unmoved.

**AND THE FIGURES OVER THE TEN MORNINGS, ALL MEASURED ON THESE FILES: the board eight hundred and twenty-nine to eight hundred and thirty-eight; the train one thousand one hundred and forty-five to one thousand one hundred and fifty-four; nobody-has-entered eight hundred and fifty-nine to eight hundred and sixty-eight; the second of January eight hundred and twenty to eight hundred and twenty-nine; the bid four hundred and fifty in the past tense on all ten and the present tense at zero; the ninth of the nine printed nights seven hundred and twelve to seven hundred and twenty-one; the second line of that lot book five hundred and thirty-four to five hundred and forty-three; the rule said out loud five hundred and thirty-nine to five hundred and forty-eight; the first day of the eighth month six hundred and nine to six hundred and eighteen; the printing it did not make five hundred and forty-eight to five hundred and fifty-seven; the age of that figure six hundred and seventy to six hundred and seventy-nine; the sheet at that gatepost four hundred and eleven on all ten with its carrier at one per file and its size of error uncomputed, unexplained and unpaid; the night of that run the five hundred and seventieth to the five hundred and seventy-ninth; the nights of it he has slept on five hundred and sixty-nine to five hundred and seventy-eight; the marks cut off that board four hundred and eighty-eight to four hundred and ninety-seven; the marks in chalk four hundred and seventy-four to four hundred and eighty-three; the mornings of the man of fifty-six the six hundred and fifty-sixth to the six hundred and sixty-fifth; the record one hundred and thirty days to one hundred and thirty-nine. The second table two hundred and forty-five mornings to two hundred and fifty-four, the near rail one hundred and ninety-five to two hundred and four with **the two hundredth** on the two hundred and twentieth morning, a man at the foot of that bank two hundred and fifteen to two hundred and twenty-four, the table of the man of about thirty-two the two hundred and fourteenth morning to the two hundred and twenty-third, the man of fifty-six's flat answer the hundred and fourth to **the hundred and thirteenth**, the mornings of not being asked the seventy-first to the eightieth, and the lane count thirty on all ten. **Row eighteen is gone and was not invented. Four figures have no intercept and were given none.**

## VOLUME 20, BLOCK 0002 — CHAPTERS 941 TO 950, MORNINGS THE TWO HUNDRED AND TWENTY-FIFTH TO THE TWO HUNDRED AND THIRTY-FOURTH

**THE FULL RECORD IS `state/volume-20-batch-0002-summary.md` AND IT IS THE ONLY PLACE THESE FIGURES ARE PUBLISHED IN FULL. WHAT FOLLOWS IS THE PER-CHAPTER LINE, AND THE POINTERS. THE CHAPTER FILES ARE CANON AGAINST EVERYTHING IN THIS SECTION.**

1. **941, the two hundred and twenty-fifth.** At about ten the man of about thirty-four says one thing out loud, once, at about four feet off the near end of the second table: he said a thing out loud in that yard on the two hundred and twenty-first morning, he has not said it since and is not going to, nobody has asked him for it, and what he has been doing since the light came up is standing at that table thinking about that morning and not about this one. **He did not say the thing again. Nobody answered him and nobody asked him what the morning before was to him. The page took no line.** Orren Pike said **seven hundred and six**. Page forty-five. Lane thirty.
2. **942, the two hundred and twenty-sixth. ESCALATION 4.** A man of thirty-eight works at the first table without stopping from about half past nine until about one and **goes up that lane with nothing in his hand**. **Nobody at the top of that lane offered him anything and he asked for nothing.** At about half past four, while the boards were coming down, **he comes back down with nothing in either hand and says one thing at the bottom of the bank: he did not ask on purpose, there was nobody up there to ask and nothing to put in a hand, and a man does not say a thing in that yard a second time because nine miles is nine miles in either direction.** Nobody answered him, nobody asked him why he went, nobody thanked him either way, and **the page took no line, and it took no line afterward either because the morning was already on it without one.** Lane thirty, unmoved.
3. **943, the two hundred and twenty-seventh.** At about eleven the man who digs loam asks the clerk, out loud, whether there is anybody in this basin who could take one of the forty-five lines on that page and look at it afterwards and say whether it was so. **The clerk's pen did not move. She says one sentence — that it is not one of the things she has ever been asked about and she is not going to start this morning — and she does not say why.** Nobody asked her a second thing about it and he went back to his strip. **The page took no line.** Page forty-five. Lane thirty. **This is the one morning of this block on which the string `check` appears, and a sweep that fires here has reported its own probe.**
4. **944, the two hundred and twenty-eighth.** Nobody says a reason and nobody says no. The only exchange of the morning is about a wheel bearing: the man who made things round asks the turner whether the wheel wants a new bearing in it or whether the ash is getting into it out of the loam, and the turner answers that the ash is getting in out of the loam and a new bearing will not stop that. **About four people at the top of eleven feet have said since that there were people in that yard this morning who could have said a reason out loud and did not, and that count is not printed in any figure block.** Orren Pike said **nine hundred and eighty-eight**. Page forty-five. Lane thirty.
5. **945, the two hundred and twenty-ninth. ESCALATION 5. THE ONLY LINE ON THE PAGE IN THIS BLOCK.** At about eleven the man of about thirty-two says one thing out loud, once, **about another man and not about a page**: what the man at the near end of the first table said on the one hundred and eighty-second morning was about him and not about the back of that book, a man who has said a thing about himself once is not going to say it again, and that is a man being allowed to do that. **He did not say the thing itself. Nobody asked him how he knew, nobody asked him to say it again, nobody said it for him, nobody answered him, and the man of thirty-eight went on working and did not turn round.** The clerk read the morning out loud and **wrote one line carrying the morning and not the reason**. **Nobody was thanked.** Page forty-six. Lane thirty.
6. **946, the two hundred and thirtieth.** Nobody says a reason and nobody says no. **The man of thirty-eight stands at about four feet off the near end of the first table from about nine until about four and says nothing out loud at all in that stretch**, and about four people at the top of eleven feet came within speaking distance of that table in the day. The light comes off the boards about twenty past eleven and goes along the top of the second table and the chalk marks cut into the edge of it. Page forty-six. Lane thirty.
7. **947, the two hundred and thirty-first.** At about a quarter to eleven the **woman of about fifty-two** comes down that bank with a folding frame, a canvas bag and netting cord, sets the frame up about nine feet back from about four feet off the near end of the first table and mends a net all morning. **Nobody said one word back to her about it. Nobody asked her what she was doing or how long she meant to be there. The second space on that form was still empty when she set her bag down beside the frame, and she did not fill it and nobody asked her to.** The man of thirty-eight works about eighteen feet of ground off that frame and the two of them say nothing to each other. Orren Pike **said no figure out loud and said so out loud**, which the card fixes for this morning. Page forty-six. Lane thirty.
8. **948, the two hundred and thirty-second. ESCALATION 6.** At about half past twelve the woman who keeps a scale says it once: **that reason is on the form and not on the page at the back of that book, because the form came down that lane in a hand and the page at the back never did**, and that she is not going to say it again. **Nobody asked her to say it again, nobody asked her how she knew, nobody asked the clerk to take that form off those boards and lay it somewhere else, nobody asked her to fill the space on it, and nobody asked anybody else to fill it. That form was not moved.** **The clerk said nothing out loud to anybody about any of it and wrote nothing in the front of that book from about ten until about two.** Page forty-six. Orren Pike said **four hundred and thirty-one**. Lane thirty.
9. **949, the two hundred and thirty-third.** The man of fifty-six is at the wall before about nine people have their coats off, says the four off it at about a quarter to eight, gets all four, and puts his hand flat on the stone. The man who made the stone comes down about ten, stands about nine feet off that wall with his hands in his pockets and goes back up without a word. At about four the man of fifty-six **says the four off that wall again in the same order and at the same speed**, puts his hand flat on the stone and says his same four words. **Nobody asked him anything, nobody asked him why he had come back to the wall, nobody asked him what the four are a figure of, and nobody asked him to say them again a different way.** Page forty-six. Lane thirty.
10. **950, the two hundred and thirty-fourth. THE REVERSAL.** **At about eleven the clerk stands up at the near end of those boards and says one sentence out loud, once, in the ordinary voice, at about four feet off the near end of the second table: *I do not know what one of them is for and I have been writing them a long time.* SHE SITS DOWN AGAIN AND PUTS HER HAND ON THE FRONT OF THAT BOOK. IT IS HER OWN SENTENCE AND IT IS ABOUT HER OWN LINES. NOBODY ANSWERED HER, NOBODY ASKED HER WHY SHE HAD SAID IT, NOBODY ASKED HER TO SAY IT AGAIN, AND NOBODY SAID ONE WORD BACK TO HER.** NOTHING IN THESE TEN FILES CALLS IT THE SAME SENTENCE THE MAN OF FIFTY-SIX SAYS OR CALLS IT A DIFFERENT ONE, EXPLAINS IT, PARAPHRASES IT, OR SAYS THAT HIS SENTENCE HAS CHANGED MEANING OR THAT IT HAS NOT. **THE PAGE TOOK NO LINE.** At about four the man of fifty-six says his four words and nobody at about four feet off the near end of the second table says anything back to that either. Page forty-six. Lane thirty, unmoved.

**AND THE FIGURES OVER THE TEN MORNINGS, ALL MEASURED ON THESE FILES: the board eight hundred and forty to eight hundred and forty-eight; the train one thousand one hundred and fifty-six to one thousand one hundred and sixty-four; nobody-has-entered eight hundred and seventy to eight hundred and seventy-eight; the second of January eight hundred and thirty-one to eight hundred and thirty-nine; the bid four hundred and fifty in the past tense on all ten and the present tense at zero; the ninth of the nine printed nights seven hundred and twenty-three to seven hundred and thirty-one; the second line of that lot book five hundred and forty-five to five hundred and fifty-three; the rule said out loud five hundred and fifty to five hundred and fifty-eight; the first day of the eighth month six hundred and twenty to six hundred and twenty-eight; the printing it did not make five hundred and fifty-nine to five hundred and sixty-seven; the age of that figure six hundred and eighty-one to six hundred and eighty-nine; the sheet at that gatepost four hundred and eleven on all ten with its carrier at one per file and its size of error uncomputed, unexplained and unpaid; the night of that run the five hundred and eightieth to the five hundred and eighty-ninth; the nights of it he has slept on five hundred and eighty to five hundred and eighty-eight; the marks cut off that board four hundred and ninety-nine to five hundred and seven, **with FIVE HUNDRED, an exact hundred with no *and* in it, on chapter 943 and on no other morning of this block**; the marks in chalk four hundred and eighty-five to four hundred and ninety-three; the mornings of the man of fifty-six the six hundred and sixty-seventh to the six hundred and seventy-fifth; the record one hundred and forty-one days to one hundred and forty-nine days. The second table two hundred and fifty-six mornings to two hundred and sixty-four, the near rail two hundred and six to two hundred and fourteen, a man at the foot of that bank two hundred and twenty-six to two hundred and thirty-four, the table of the man of about thirty-two the two hundred and twenty-fifth morning to the two hundred and thirty-third, the man of fifty-six's flat answer the hundred and fifteenth to **the hundred and twenty-third**, the mornings of not being asked the eighty-second to **the ninetieth**, the page forty-five lines across four mornings and **forty-six** across six, and the lane count thirty on all ten. **Row eighteen is gone and was not invented. Four figures have no intercept and were given none.**

CANON PRINTS AN ORDINAL ON EVERY MORNING OF THE BLOCK BEHIND — *the hundred and thirteenth time running* AT CHAPTER 940 — **AND ALL TEN CHAPTERS HERE PRINT THE ORDINAL.
