## VOLUME 20, BLOCK 0003 — CHAPTERS 951 TO 960, MORNINGS THE TWO HUNDRED AND THIRTY-FIFTH TO THE TWO HUNDRED AND FORTY-FOURTH

**`state/volume-20-batch-0003-summary.md` IS THE RECORD OF THIS BLOCK AND IT IS THE ONE PLACE ITS FIGURES ARE PUBLISHED IN FULL. THE CHAPTER FILES ARE CANON AGAINST EVERY LINE OF WHAT FOLLOWS.**

1. **951, the two hundred and thirty-fifth. ESCALATION 7 — THE SUBSTITUTE BEGINS.** At about one the man of about forty-eight who keeps a tally says one reason out loud, once, about himself: **he has kept that tally nine years, nobody has ever come and asked him what it is a tally of, that is the whole of what he has got this morning, he is not going to say it a second time, and nobody has asked him for it this morning and nobody is going to ask him for it on any of the mornings after this one.** **NOBODY ASKED HIM FOR IT, NOBODY ASKED HIM WHETHER IT WAS ALREADY WRITTEN DOWN, NOBODY SAID IT FOR HIM, NOBODY ANSWERED HIM AND NOBODY THANKED HIM, AND HE SAID A SENTENCE BEFORE HIS NUMBER ON THIS MORNING AND ON NONE OF THE OTHERS.** The clerk reads the morning out loud and **writes one line carrying the morning and not the reason**. Orren Pike said **one thousand and ninety-four**. Page forty-seven. Lane thirty, unmoved.
2. **952, the two hundred and thirty-sixth.** Nobody says a reason and nobody says no. The man of thirty-eight carries his piece to the far end of the first table for an hour and brings it back. The woman who keeps a scale weighs two bars and does not stay past about one. **The clerk reads the morning out loud at about four and writes the morning on that page under the last line without a line, and about four people at the top of eleven feet have said since that a clerk reading a morning and writing nothing has not happened since the one hundred and eighty-fourth morning.** Orren Pike **said no figure out loud and said so out loud**. Page forty-seven.
3. **953, the two hundred and thirty-seventh.** The man who made the stone comes down the bank about ten and says one thing out loud, once: **he cut it and dressed it and set it under that wall with his own hands, he has never said so out loud in that yard, and the reason he has never once said it is that the only thing anybody in that yard has ever said about it is nothing.** **Nobody asked him for a second thing about that stone, nobody asked him how he knew what the only thing anybody had said about it was, nobody answered him and nobody thanked him for making it.** One line goes on the page carrying the morning and not that. Page forty-eight.
4. **954, the two hundred and thirty-eighth.** **The woman of about fifty-two comes down that bank at about a quarter to eleven with nothing in her hands at all, stands about nine feet back from the boards in about four feet of sun for the best part of the morning and goes back up at about twenty past two. NOBODY SAID ONE WORD TO HER, NOBODY ASKED HER WHAT SHE WAS STANDING THERE FOR AND NOBODY SAID GOOD MORNING TO HER. THE SECOND SPACE ON THAT FORM WAS EMPTY THE WHOLE MORNING AND SHE DID NOT FILL IT AND NOBODY ASKED HER TO.** The man of thirty-eight works about nine feet of ground off her and does not look over. Page forty-eight.
5. **955, the two hundred and thirty-ninth. ESCALATION 8 — THE COST, DEMONSTRATED.** At about half past ten the man of about twenty-nine who drives the cart comes through the gap in that bank, stops, and says one reason out loud, once, **about a morning he was not standing next to**: he was not in that yard on the morning of the one hundred and ninety-first morning, he is away from that yard three mornings out of ten, and he has one thing to say about that morning and he is saying it once and then he is taking his load out the far side. **Nobody asked him whether it was so and nobody asked him how he knew what that morning was.** A line goes on the page carrying the morning. **ABOUT FOUR PEOPLE AT THE TOP OF ELEVEN FEET HAVE SAID SINCE THAT A LINE CAN GO ON THAT PAGE FROM A MAN SAYING A THING ABOUT A MORNING HE WAS NOT STANDING NEXT TO, THAT THE MORNING IS THE ONLY THING THE LINE CARRIES, AND THAT NOBODY IN THAT YARD CAN FIND OUT AFTERWARDS WHETHER IT WAS SO, AND NOBODY IN THAT YARD ANSWERED THAT.** Page forty-nine.
6. **956, the two hundred and fortieth.** **From about ten until about twelve the clerk said nothing out loud to anybody**, with the front of that book open and her pen down beside it and not in it, and the one of the people at the boards who asked her, out loud, whether she was going to say anything about the morning before twelve got no answer. **Orren Pike says a figure out loud before the clerk's hand had moved and not one of the about four people who heard him asked him what it was about.** Page forty-nine. Lane thirty, unmoved.
7. **957, the two hundred and forty-first. ROW SIXTEEN REACHES FIVE HUNDRED.** **There are five hundred marks in chalk along the edge of that second table, an exact hundred with no *and* in it, and nobody in that yard said one word about it and nobody said it out loud at about four feet off the near end of the second table or anywhere else, and the figure block prints the figure and prints nothing about it.** The table of the man of about thirty-two stands for the two hundred and fortieth morning on the same day and is not the second table and is not the near rail. The turner's wheel did not change speed. The man who digs loam was at the north end and said nothing. Page forty-nine.
8. **958, the two hundred and forty-second. THE PAGE TAKES TWO LINES IN ONE MORNING.** At about eleven the man of about thirty-four says one reason out loud, once, about himself: he has been at that table for the whole of the time he has been in that yard, every one of the things he has said out loud in that yard he said standing at that table, and every one of them was about him. At about a quarter past eleven the woman of about fifty-two says one out loud from the top of that bank **without coming down**: she came down that bank with nothing to mend, she did not come down for anybody in that yard, and she is not going to start coming up it only when there is a net to mend. **THEY WERE ABOUT FOUR HUNDRED YARDS APART AND NEITHER KNEW THE OTHER HAD SAID ONE AND NEITHER WENT TOWARDS THE OTHER. THE CLERK READ THE MORNING OUT LOUD ONCE AND ONCE ONLY AND WROTE TWO LINES, BOTH CARRYING THE MORNING AND NEITHER CARRYING A REASON.** Nobody thanked either of them. Orren Pike said **two thousand and forty**. Page fifty-one.
9. **959, the two hundred and forty-third.** **The man of thirty-eight comes down the slope at about half past nine, works at about four feet off the near end of the first table until about four and says nothing out loud at all in that stretch**, and about four people at the top of eleven feet came within speaking distance of that table in the day and put nothing to him. That second table is carried a foot further along the ground at about nine by two men who do not speak to each other while they do it. Orren Pike **said no figure out loud and said so out loud**. Page fifty-one.
10. **960, the two hundred and forty-fourth. BLOCK 0003 ENDS ON A MORNING THE PAGE DID NOT MOVE ON.** **Nobody in that yard asked the man of thirty-eight anything at any point in the morning and he said nothing out loud at all in it.** A man of about sixty-four was at the foot of that low wall and was not asked one question, **which is the one hundredth morning running of that, and nothing in these ten files says one word about it.** The man of fifty-six's four words are heard for the hundred and thirty-third time running and nobody asks him anything. The clerk reads the morning out loud and writes it without a line. Page fifty-one. Lane thirty, unmoved.
